# Sūrah al-Furqān (Chapter 25) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:1

> Blessed is the One Who sent down the Standard to His servant, so that he may be a warner to the whole world

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Word as a Claim About Its Speaker**

The sūrah opens with *tabāraka*, a word whose Arabic root carries the meaning of continuous growth and increase in goodness (from *barakah*, blessing). Applied to God, it does not suggest that He changes or grows — a concern that moved some exegetes to prefer "holy" or "transcendent" as a translation — but declares that the source of all blessing is Himself, eternally and without interruption. The opening is not a greeting or an invocation; it is an assertion about the character of the One who acts. Everything that follows in the sūrah — the sending down, the warning, the rejection, the punishment, the reward — flows from a speaker whose nature is *tabāraka*. The word places the entire chapter under a theological claim: the revelation that arrives is not an afterthought but an expression of a generosity that precedes creation.

The structure mirrors other Qur'anic doxologies. Sūrah 67 opens with *tabāraka'lladhī bi-yadihi'l-mulk* — "Blessed is He in Whose hand is all sovereignty" (67:1). Sūrah 23:68 asks, "Have they not reflected on the Word, or has there come to them what did not come to their fathers of old?" The doxology in 25:1 answers that question by placing the Word inside a declaration of divine praise before any argument begins. The placement is architecturally significant: every objection the disbelievers raise in the chapters that follows — that the messenger eats food (25:7), that the revelation is fabricated (25:4), that it should have come all at once (25:32) — stands against a speaker who has already been named as the source of all blessing. The doxology is not decoration; it is the frame inside which every subsequent argument operates.

**Al-Furqān: The Criterion That Separates**

The title word *al-Furqān* comes from the root *f-r-q*, meaning to separate or divide. In the Qur'an it names both the Torah given to Moses (2:53; 21:48) and the revelation given to Muhammad ﷺ, functioning as a standard by which truth is distinguished from falsehood, the lawful from the prohibited, and the guided from the lost. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī, in his *Mufradāt*, defines it as that which separates truth from falsehood, emphasizing its role as a moral and judicial instrument rather than merely a literary text. The Quran is called al-Furqān because it does not sit alongside other claims as one opinion among many; it adjudicates between them.

Some modern scholars, drawing on pre-Islamic Arabic poetry and the work of the lexicographer al-Azharī (10th century), have proposed that *furqān* also carried the meaning of "dawn" — the moment when light separates from darkness. Whether or not this semantic layer was present in the audience's ear, the dominant Qur'anic usage is the one the sūrah exploits: a thing that cuts a line between two states that would otherwise blur. The word appears elsewhere with this force in 2:177, where the Book functions as a criterion within a larger list of beliefs and obligations, and in 8:29 and 8:41, where the *furqān* is given to those who believe and distinguish right from wrong. The Criterion is not a passive text; it is an active instrument that judges its reader as much as the reader judges it.

**A Warner to the Whole World**

The phrase *li-yakūna lil-'ālamīn nadhīrā* — "so that he may be a warner to the whole world" — is one of the earliest Qur'anic declarations of the universal scope of the prophetic mission. It was revealed in Makkah, when the Prophet ﷺ faced determined opposition from a single city, and it already names his audience as *al-'ālamīn*, the worlds: not only human beings of every language and era but, as some commentators note, also the jinn (see the introduction to Sūrah 72). The specification matters because it forecloses the objection that the message is tribal or regional. Compare 21:107, "We have sent you only as a mercy to the worlds," where the same word *'ālamīn* carries the same universal charge but the operative noun is mercy, not warning. Here the charge is warning — *nadhīr* — a word that implies an approaching danger the warner has seen and the audience has not.

The relationship between *Furqān* and *nadhīr* in this single verse is the engine of the whole sūrah. A criterion without a voice is inert; a voice without a criterion is noise. The verse pairs them: the Standard is sent down so that the warning can be made. Every objection the disbelievers raise in the verses that follow is an attempt to separate the criterion from the warner, to accept one while dismissing the other. The sūrah's structure refuses that separation. The warning requires the criterion because the criterion defines what the warning warns against; the criterion requires the warning because the criterion has no meaning if no one delivers it. The opening verse compresses the entire argument of the chapter into a single sentence.

**The Named Servant and the Unnamed Recipient**

The verse says "His servant" (*'abdihi*) without naming Muhammad ﷺ, a feature shared with 17:1 — "Glory be to the One Who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque." The title *'abd* (servant) is the highest honorific the Qur'an assigns to the Prophet; it appears at moments of supreme significance — the night journey, the opening of this sūrah, and the final commissioning in 33:56. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *'abd* here stresses total submission: the one who carries the Criterion is himself under its authority. A warner who was not first a servant would be a lecturer, not a witness.

The unnamed quality also widens the application. The Prophet ﷺ is the servant in question, but the grammatical form allows the reader to hear the verse as a principle: any servant who receives and transmits the Criterion fulfills the same role in miniature. This is not to diminish the uniqueness of prophethood — the sūrah will make clear that no messenger before him was different in kind (25:20) — but to establish that the Standard requires a human carrier, and the carrier's humanity is not a deficiency in the message. The sūrah's opening word is not the Prophet's name but God's attribute, and the servant is not the subject but the instrument. The architecture of the verse places the weight where it belongs: on the One who sends, not on the one who receives.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:2

> ˹Allah is˺ the One to Whom belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, Who has never had ˹any˺ offspring, nor does He have a partner in ˹governing˺ the kingdom. He has created everything, ordaining it precisely

**Expanded Commentary**

**Absolute Ownership as the Ground of Uniqueness**

The verse opens by asserting God's sole sovereignty over the heavens and the earth — *alladhī lahu mulku's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — before it denies two claims that would fracture that sovereignty: that He has offspring, and that He has a partner. The logic is sequential, not decorative. Ownership of all that exists is the premise; the denial of sonship and partnership is the consequence. If the kingdom belongs to one, there is no room for a second. The same structure appears in Sūrah 67:1 — "Blessed is He in Whose hand is sovereignty, and He has power over all things" — where the hand that holds the kingdom is the same hand that holds the power. Sovereignty in the Qur'an is never shared, delegated, or inherited. It is a quality inseparable from the One who holds it.

Al-Rāzī observes that the word *mulk* (kingdom, sovereignty) carries the additional force of dominion that cannot be taken away. Human kings hold their domains temporarily; God's *mulk* is intrinsic — it does not depend on subjects, armies, or borders. The denial of offspring (*lam yattakhidh waladan*) addresses both the pre-Islamic Arabian belief in the "daughters of God" — the angels were considered divine progeny by some tribes — and the Christian doctrine of divine sonship as understood by the Qur'an's Meccan audience. The denial is not metaphorical; it is ontological. A being whose sovereignty is total and unconditioned does not reproduce, because reproduction implies need, continuation, or succession, and none of these apply to the One described here. The verse does not argue the point; it states it as a fact, and the fact is the foundation for everything that follows in the sūrah's case against idolatry.

**Taqdīr: Ordaining With Precise Measure**

The phrase *wa qaddarahu taqdīrā* — "and He has created everything, ordaining it precisely" — uses a verb (*qaddara*) and its cognate noun (*taqdīr*) together, a construction the Qur'an employs for emphasis. The root carries two interlocking meanings: to know the measure of a thing, and to assign a measure to it. God does not merely create and then estimate; He creates by measuring, and the measurement is the creation's defining feature. Al-Rāzī distinguishes between human *taqdīr*, which is assessment after the fact, and divine *taqdīr*, which is determination before the fact — God's knowledge of a creature's entire existence, from origin to end, is itself the act of ordainment.

This has direct consequences for the theological debate over *qadar* (predestination). The verse does not settle the question of how human freedom relates to divine decree — later theology will spend centuries on that — but it does establish the premise: nothing in creation is unmeasured. Every creature, every event, every limit is set by the One who owns the kingdom. The commentators who emphasize *taqdīr* as ordainment point to verses like 54:49 — "Indeed, all things We created with *qadar*" — and 65:3 — "He will provide for him from where he does not expect." The measuring is comprehensive; it includes the provision, the term, and the outcome. The parallel in 76:16 — "reclining on couches lined with silk brocade, and the fruit of both gardens is near at hand" — uses the same root in a different context to describe the precision of Paradise's provisions, showing that *taqdīr* applies not only to creation but to reward.

**A Denial That Closes Every Exit**

The three denials in this verse — no offspring, no partner, everything precisely measured — function as a theological wall. The claim of offspring opens a door to mediation: if God has a child, the child can be worshipped alongside or instead of Him. The claim of a partner opens a door to dualism: if two share the kingdom, each can be credited with part of creation, and moral responsibility fragments. The claim that creation is unmeasured opens a door to randomness: if things happen without divine ordination, the universe is ungoverned and moral consequence becomes arbitrary. The verse closes all three doors in a single sentence.

This is not a philosophical treatise compressed into a line; it is a practical argument addressed to people who have "taken gods apart from Him" (25:3). Every denial in 25:2 is the ground for the indictment in 25:3. If God has no offspring and no partner, then the false gods are neither His children nor His delegates. If everything is precisely measured, then the false gods have no independent power to create, sustain, or destroy. The theology of 25:2 is not abstract; it is the prosecution's opening statement, and 25:3 will present the evidence.

**Cross-Referencing Sovereignty Across the Qur'an**

The declaration that the kingdom belongs to God alone recurs across the Qur'an in contexts that sharpen its force. In 3:189 — "To Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, and Allah has power over all things" — the statement follows a description of the Day when every soul will find what it has done presented before it. Sovereignty there is the framework for accountability: the Owner of all things judges all things. In 36:83 — "So glory be to the One in Whose hand is the dominion of all things, and to Him you will all be returned" — the word *subḥān* (glory) appears, and the return is specified. The same structure appears in 25:2: the One who owns everything has created everything with measure, and to that measured creation the disbelievers will return. The repetition across the Qur'an is not redundancy; it is insistence. Each appearance places the sovereignty claim in a different argument — here against idolatry, there against denial of the resurrection, elsewhere against the assumption that human power is self-sustaining. In every case, the sovereignty is total, the measure is precise, and the argument is the same: nothing exists outside the dominion of the One who made it.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:3

> Yet they have taken besides Him gods who cannot create anything but are themselves created. Nor can they protect or benefit themselves. Nor can they control life, death, or resurrection

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indictment in Four Charges**

The verse lays out a case against false objects of worship with prosecutorial precision. Four charges are stated, each one a self-contained clause: these gods create nothing; they are themselves created; they cannot protect or benefit themselves; they have no authority over death, life, or resurrection. The order is deliberate. The first charge targets the core claim of divinity — the power to bring something into existence from nothing. The second charge reverses the assumed relationship: the worshipped are not creators but creatures, dependent on the very power they are supposed to share. The third charge extends the dependency to self-preservation: these gods cannot keep themselves from harm or secure their own benefit, let alone those of their worshippers. The fourth charge removes them from the domain of ultimate consequence: life, death, and the raising of the dead are entirely beyond their reach.

Al-Rāzī notes that the sequence escalates. A thing that cannot create may still be powerful. A thing that is itself created is proven dependent. A thing that cannot protect itself is proven weak. A thing that has no authority over life and death is proven irrelevant to the matters that most concern a worshipper. By the end of the verse, the case is complete without a single word of rhetorical excess. The disbelievers who took these gods "besides Him" (*min dūnihi*) are not accused of foolishness; they are presented with evidence that their chosen objects of devotion fail every test of divinity. The verse does not insult; it lists facts, and the facts are sufficient.

**The Qur'an's Persistent Argument Against Idol Incompetence**

This verse does not stand alone; it belongs to a sustained Qur'anic argument that appears across multiple sūrahs with varying emphases. In 7:191 — "Do they associate with Him those who create nothing and are themselves created?" — the same first charge is stated as a question, forcing the audience to answer it themselves. In 16:20 — "Those they invoke besides Allah do not create anything; rather, they are themselves created" — the statement is even more compressed. The charge about inability to harm or benefit appears in 5:76, 10:18, 10:106, 13:16, and 21:66, each time in a slightly different argumentative context.

The Qur'an adds additional charges in other passages: idols cannot grant provision (16:73; 29:17), they cannot speak (78:37), and they cannot intercede on the Day of Judgment (30:13; 39:43; 43:86). Each new passage places the indictment in a fresh argument. Here, in 25:3, the charges follow the assertion of God's sole sovereignty and precise creation in 25:2. The argument is structural: the One who owns everything and measures everything has no need of, and no room for, a partner who owns nothing and measures nothing. The worshippers are not merely wrong; they are inconsistent with their own theology. If they believe God created everything with measure (25:2), they cannot logically worship what cannot create at all (25:3).

**The Widening Scope: From Idols to All False Dependencies**

Some commentators, al-Rāzī among them, note that while this verse is widely understood to address Arab idolaters in particular, the logic extends further. Any object of devotion that cannot create, cannot self-sustain, and cannot govern the fundamental events of human existence — birth, death, return — falls under the same indictment. The verse does not name the idols; it describes their deficiencies. This is a deliberate rhetorical choice. By defining false gods through what they lack rather than through what they are made of — wood, stone, or metaphorical attachment — the Qur'an ensures the argument reaches every era. A modern attachment that functions as a god — a system, an ideology, a source of security that creates nothing and cannot govern life and death — meets the same description.

The word *yamlikūn* (they possess, control) in the final clause is particularly sharp. The idols do not merely fail to control death and life; they possess no ownership over them at all. The same root (*m-l-k*) appeared in 25:2 to describe God's sovereignty. The false gods are being measured against the standard the verse before established: if sovereignty belongs to One alone, anything that lacks sovereignty is not God, regardless of how many people bow before it. The linguistic echo between the two verses — *mulk* in 25:2, *yamlikūn* in 25:3 — tightens the argument further: the One who owns the kingdom is contrasted with what owns nothing.

**The Body Speaks: When Idols Are Given a Voice**

One of the most striking features of the Qur'anic treatment of false worship is the scene, described elsewhere, in which the idols themselves testify against their worshippers. In 36:65 — "That Day, We will seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify to what they used to earn" — the human body is given voice; in the parallel scene involving the idols, the objects of worship are made to deny that they were ever worshipped at all. This will appear later in this very sūrah at 25:17-19, where God gathers the false deities and asks whether they misled His servants. The idols' answer, as recorded there, echoes the logic of 25:3: "Glory be to You! It was not right for us to take any lords besides You."

The incapacity described in 25:3 — they cannot create, cannot sustain, cannot govern — is precisely what the idols will confess when given the chance to speak. The charge made here in Mecca is the testimony they will give on the Day of Judgment. The effect is one of prophetic irony: the Qur'an pronounces the verdict before the trial, and the verdict will be confirmed by the defendants themselves. The false gods will say, on the Day when speech is granted to all things, exactly what this verse says about them now.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:4

> The disbelievers say, “This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but a fabrication which he made up with the help of others.” Their claim is totally unjustified and untrue

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Accusation and Its Anatomy**

The charge is specific: the Quran is not revelation but fabrication — *ifk* — and it was not produced alone but with assistance from other people. The two parts of the accusation are distinct. The first targets the content: what Muhammad ﷺ recites is a lie. The second targets the process: he could not have produced it himself, so others must have supplied the material. The combination was designed to be devastating. If the Quran were acknowledged as remarkable but attributed to human genius, the Prophet could still be respected as a poet or philosopher. If it were acknowledged as foreign but attributed to revelation, it could still be rejected as unwanted interference. The accusation fuses both: the content is false, and the source is human. No room is left for admiration without submission.

The identity of the alleged helpers is addressed in the parallel verses and in the historical record. Some commentators name specific individuals — Jews and Christians who had contact with the Prophet ﷺ — though the verse itself does not name them, and the next verse (25:5) will describe the alternative theory that the material was taken from ancient written sources. Maudūdī points out a practical difficulty the disbelievers never resolved: if these helpers existed and supplied the material, why did no one raid their houses to seize the source documents? The Quraysh were not constrained by scruples; they persecuted, boycotted, and fought. If physical evidence of collaboration existed, it would have been found. The accusation required the audience to believe that an elaborate literary fraud was being conducted under their noses in a small city where everyone knew everyone, and yet no participant could be identified, no source text could be produced, and no inconsistency could be found.

**Ifk: The Qur'an's Strongest Word for Falsehood**

The word *ifk* is one of the harshest terms the Qur'an uses for a lie. It appears in the context of the slander against Mary (19:27), and in the accusation against Joseph's master's wife (12:24, 12:28). In every case, *ifk* names not an honest mistake but a deliberate inversion of truth — calling good evil, calling revelation fabrication, calling chastity adultery. The disbelievers' use of this word against the Quran is itself an act of *ifk*: they take the most consequential truth claim of their time and label it the most consequential lie.

The verse's own response — *faqad jā'ū ẓulman wa zuwrā* — is translated as "Their claim is totally unjustified and untrue," but the Arabic is more forceful. *Ẓulm* is injustice, the placing of something in the wrong position; *zūr* is crookedness, deviation from the straight. The two words together describe a claim that is both morally wrong and factually distorted. The disbelievers have not merely made an error; they have twisted the truth and then stood on the twisted version as though it were straight. The pairing of *ẓulm* and *zūr* appears elsewhere in the Qur'an to describe the deepest kind of wrong — not the wrong of ignorance but the wrong of knowing the truth and refusing it.

**The Charge That Returns in Every Generation**

The accusation in 25:4 is not unique to seventh-century Makkah; it is the standard objection to any claim of revelation. The same charge was leveled against Moses in Pharaoh's court: "This is indeed a well-contrived sorcery" (10:76). The same charge appears in the description of those who say of the Quran, "This is nothing but the word of a mortal" (74:25). The sūrah's placement of this accusation at the very beginning — after the doxology (25:1) and the theology of sovereignty (25:2-3) — tells the reader what the chapter is about. This is not a historical report of an old argument; it is the argument the sūrah exists to answer.

The disbelievers' approach reveals a method. They do not engage the content of the Quran; they question its origin. This is the structure of every dismissal that avoids the text: rather than refute what the Quran says, they explain where it came from. The sūrah will respond to this strategy in multiple ways — by pointing to the Quran's own internal coherence (25:33), by asking why the disbelievers cannot produce something like it if it is human, and by describing the consequences of the rejection in vivid eschatological terms (25:11-16). But the response begins here, with a simple declaration: the accusation is *ẓulm* and *zūr*. Before any argument is offered, the claim is named for what it is.

**Why the Verse Does Not Argue Further**

A notable feature of 25:4 is its brevity. It records the accusation and pronounces it unjust and crooked, but it does not mount a defense. The defense comes in the verses that follow: the revelation comes from the Knower of all secrets (25:6), the Prophet is not unique in being human (25:20), and the Quran was sent in stages deliberately (25:32). The rhetorical strategy is important. The accusation is given its own verse — space, weight, and dignity — and then it is answered point by point across the rest of the sūrah. The effect is that the reader encounters the full force of the objection before hearing the reply, and the reply, when it comes, addresses the objection from every angle rather than from one. This is not evasion; it is architecture. The sūrah trusts its reader to hold the accusation in mind while the answer unfolds, and the answer is stronger for the delay. The accusation stands; the defense will follow; and the defense will outlast the accusation because it addresses not one charge but all of them.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:5

> And they say, “˹These revelations are only˺ ancient fables which he has had written down, and they are rehearsed to him morning and evening.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Accusation: Borrowed Tales**

Where 25:4 charged the Prophet ﷺ with fabricating the Quran with human help, 25:5 advances a different theory: the material was not invented but copied from older written sources. The phrase *asāṭīru'l-awwalīn* — "ancient fables" or "tales of the ancients" — recurs across the Qur'an (6:25; 8:31; 16:24; 23:83; 27:68; 46:17; 68:15; 83:13), each time as a label the disbelievers apply to the narratives the Quran contains: stories of earlier prophets, earlier nations, earlier punishments. The charge is that these are not divine communications but retellings of Jewish and Christian lore, acquired through contact with *ahl al-kitāb* (the People of the Book) and then read back to Muhammad ﷺ as though they were new.

The detail "morning and evening" (*bukratan wa aṣīlā*) specifies the method. Someone — the unnamed helpers of 25:4 — is said to write the stories down in advance, then present them to the Prophet at regular intervals so that he can memorize and recite them. The picture is one of a rehearsed performance, not a spontaneous revelation. Al-Ṭabarī connects this with a specific figure from the historical record: al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith, a Qurayshī storyteller who would recite tales from Persian history and legend, after which the disbelievers would compare his material with the Quran and declare, in the Prophet's presence, that al-Naḍr's stories were superior. The accusation in 25:5 is, in effect, an extension of this comparison: the Quran's narratives are no different from what any well-read storyteller could produce.

**The Internal Contradiction of the Two Charges**

Placed side by side, 25:4 and 25:5 contradict each other. The first says the Quran was fabricated — invented — with help. The second says it was copied from existing written sources. These are not compatible theories of origin. An invented text borrows from nothing; a copied text invents nothing. The disbelievers, in their eagerness to explain away the Quran, advance both charges without noticing the tension. The Qur'an records this without comment, allowing the contradiction to speak for itself. Al-Rāzī draws attention to the pairing, noting that the disbelievers' inability to settle on a single objection is itself evidence of their desperation. If the Quran were obviously fabricated, one explanation would suffice. The fact that multiple incompatible explanations are offered suggests that none of them accounts for what the Quran actually is.

This pattern — recording the objection and letting its internal weakness show — is a hallmark of the sūrah's method. The disbelievers are not refuted by counterargument alone; they are refuted by the accumulation of their own inconsistencies. The reader is trusted to notice that the same people who say "he invented it" in 25:4 say "he copied it" in 25:5, and to conclude that neither charge can stand on its own, and together they cancel each other out.

**What the Disbelievers Could Not Explain**

Maudūdī raises a practical objection to the copying theory that the disbelievers themselves never addressed. Muhammad ﷺ lived in Makkah for forty years before receiving revelation. He was known to the Quraysh as *al-Amīn*, the trustworthy, a man whose character they themselves attested. During those forty years, no one observed him studying Torah, engaging in theological discussion, or displaying the kind of learning the Quran would require. If he had been receiving instruction from Jews or Christians "morning and evening," as 25:5 claims, the Quraysh — who monitored his every move in order to oppose him — would have noticed. The accusation required the audience to believe both that the Prophet was unlettered (which the Qur'an itself affirms in 7:157) and that he was simultaneously conducting an intensive course of study in scriptural literature. The two claims do not sit together.

Furthermore, the people allegedly named as his teachers — those who had learned from Jewish sources — were themselves known in Makkah. They were not scholars; some were barely literate. The idea that they could have supplied the material for a text whose literary quality even its opponents acknowledged as extraordinary was, as Maudūdī notes, never seriously defended by the disbelievers themselves. The charge was made; the defense was never attempted. The silence of the defense is itself an argument.

**The Surah's Pattern: Objection, Then Answer**

The pairing of 25:4 and 25:5 establishes a pattern the sūrah will follow throughout. Objections are stated fully, often in the disbelievers' own words — *wa qālu*, "and they say" — and then answered. But the answers do not always come immediately. After these two verses of accusation, 25:6 offers a compressed reply: the Quran comes from the Knower of all secrets in the heavens and earth. The full answer unfolds across the next thirty verses: the Prophet's humanity is not a deficiency (25:7-9, 25:20), the promise of the Hereafter is real (25:10-16), the false gods will testify against their worshippers (25:17-19), and the Quran's gradual revelation is deliberate and serves the Prophet's heart (25:32). The disbelievers' objections are given pride of place at the start because the sūrah's purpose is to answer them. A reader who encounters the accusations first understands why the rest of the chapter exists.

**The Absence of Named Sources**

A notable feature of both 25:4 and 25:5 is the absence of specific names. The disbelievers do not identify the human helpers who allegedly helped fabricate the Quran, nor do they point to the specific written sources from which it was copied. This vagueness is itself an argument. If the evidence existed — if identifiable people had supplied the material, if specific books had been consulted — the disbelievers would have named them. They had the means, the motive, and the social power to investigate. They controlled Makkah's economy, its tribal networks, and its public discourse. An accusation backed by evidence would have been devastating; an accusation backed by nothing is merely noise. The Qur'an's decision to record the charges without refuting them in detail — simply letting the emptiness of the accusations stand on the page — is a more effective rebuttal than any counterargument. The reader is left with two charges that name no sources, no helpers, no texts, and no method, and the silence speaks louder than any defense could.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:6

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “This ˹Quran˺ has been revealed by the One Who knows the secrets of the heavens and the earth. Surely He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Answer in the Sūrah**

After two verses of accusation — fabrication (25:4), copying from ancient sources (25:5) — the reply is a single sentence addressed to the Prophet: say who sent it. The instruction to "Say" (*qul*) places the response in the Prophet's mouth as a divine directive; he does not construct the answer from his own reasoning but delivers it as commanded. The content is a single assertion: the source of this Quran is the One who knows the unseen (*sirr*) of the heavens and the earth. This is not a defensive argument. It does not engage the specific charges of fabrication or copying. It bypasses them entirely by stating the origin, and the origin — an omniscient being who knows what is hidden in both realms — is itself a refutation. A text that comes from the Knower of all secrets cannot be a copy of human tales, because the secrets it contains exceed what any human could compile.

The verse does not explain how God's knowledge refutes the accusation; it states it as a fact and moves on. This confidence is the sūrah's characteristic posture: the answer is given at the level of certainty, not at the level of debate. The disbelievers offered theories; the response offers a fact. The asymmetry is deliberate: theories can be argued with, but a stated fact either is accepted or rejected, and rejection is the disbelievers' problem, not the revelation's.

**Sirr: The Secret That No Helper Could Supply**

The word *sirr* (secret, hidden thing) appears here in the plural — *asrār* — covering everything concealed in the heavens and the earth. The charge in 25:4 was that unnamed helpers provided the Prophet ﷺ with material. The charge in 25:5 was that ancient written sources supplied the content. This verse answers both by pointing to a domain no helper can enter: the unseen. The Quran contains knowledge of events the Prophet ﷺ could not have witnessed — the creation of the heavens, the fate of earlier nations, the interior states of the human soul — and attributes this knowledge to its divine source. If the content exceeds what any human collaborator could know, the collaboration theory collapses.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the force of this against the specific charges: the disbelievers named the helpers and the books, but neither the helpers nor the books had access to what is secret in the heavens and the earth. The verse does not need to list the Quran's internal evidences of divine knowledge; the category itself — the unseen — is sufficient to disqualify all human sources. The argument is not "here is proof that the Quran is divine"; it is "the source of the Quran is the One who knows what no human knows, and that is the end of the question."

**Forgiveness After Judgment**

The verse closes with an attribute pair that has puzzled some readers: *innahu kāna ghafūran raḥīmā* — "Surely He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful." After asserting that He knows all secrets — which includes the secrets of the disbelievers' hearts, their plots and their mockery — the verse names forgiveness and mercy. The placement is deliberate. The One who could punish the disbelievers for their accusations against His revelation chooses to announce His willingness to forgive. This is not softness; it is a final offer. The sūrah will describe the punishment in detail (25:11-16), but before the description begins, the door to repentance is left open.

The same pattern appears in 6:54: "Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself, so that whoever among you does evil out of ignorance and then repents and does good — He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful." The tension between omniscience and mercy is the Qur'an's persistent theme. God knows what the disbelievers conceal; He does not need their confession to judge them. Yet He offers forgiveness before the judgment arrives. This is the theological structure that makes human responsibility real: the punishment is not inevitable, because the mercy precedes it, but the mercy can be refused, and refusal has consequences.

**The Command to Say: The Prophet as Messenger, Not Author**

The word *qul* — "Say" — appears over three hundred times in the Qur'an, and each appearance performs the same function: it separates the Prophet ﷺ from the message he delivers. He is not the author of the statement; he is the commanded speaker. In 25:6, this matters because the disbelievers' charge was precisely that he was the author. The response — *qul* — denies the premise. If God commands the Prophet to say these words, the Prophet did not compose them. The grammatical form of the command makes the separation visible within the text itself. Every reader encounters *qul* and knows that the sentence that follows belongs to God, not to the man who speaks it aloud. The command to "say" is itself the answer to the charge of fabrication: the speaker and the author are different, and the text makes that difference explicit every time *qul* appears.

**The Knowledge That Encompasses Both Realms**

The phrase "the secrets of the heavens and the earth" (*asrāra's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ*) is comprehensive. It does not refer to one domain of knowledge — not only astronomy, not only geology, not only human affairs — but to everything hidden in both realms. The heavens in the Qur'anic cosmology contain the celestial bodies, the angels, the preserved tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*), and the throne of God. The earth contains the natural world, the human heart, the hidden intentions behind every action. The One who knows the secrets of both realms possesses a knowledge that encompasses creation in its entirety. This is the source the verse names for the Quran, and the claim is staggering: the text that the disbelievers dismissed as borrowed tales comes from a knowledge that includes everything that has ever existed and everything that will exist. The implication is that no human source — no book, no teacher, no tradition — can supply what this knowledge contains, because human knowledge is partial and this knowledge is total.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:7

> And they say ˹mockingly˺, “What kind of messenger is this who eats food and goes about in market-places ˹for a living˺? If only an angel had been sent down with him to be his co-warner

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Disbelief That Demands a Different Messenger**

The objection shifts from the message to the messenger. The disbelievers no longer argue about whether the Quran is fabricated or borrowed; they now question whether the person delivering it could possibly be God's chosen envoy. The complaint is double: he eats food, and he walks in the markets. Both are markers of ordinary human life, and both are presented as disqualifications. A real messenger, in their view, should not need sustenance and should not be seen conducting business among common people. The logic is that divine authority and human normalcy are incompatible — a being who needs to eat cannot carry a message from the One who needs nothing.

This was not a novel objection. The same charge appears across the Qur'an in the mouths of different communities. The people of Noah said, "He is only a human being like you who wants to be superior to you" (23:24). The people of ʿĀd said of their messenger, "You are only a human being like us" (26:154). The Quraysh's version adds a specifically Meccan detail: the market-place. In Makkah, social status was closely tied to wealth and commercial success. The Prophet ﷺ had not accumulated the kind of fortune that would place him above reproach. His presence in the market — buying, selling, walking among the people — was, for the disbelievers, proof that he was no different from them, and therefore could not be their superior in spiritual authority. The objection reveals what the disbelievers valued: not truth but status, not message but credentials.

**The Desire for an Angel as Co-Warner**

The second part of the objection — "If only an angel had been sent down with him" — reveals what the disbelievers actually wanted: visible, physical confirmation of divine authority. An angel co-warner would have served as credentials, a supernatural endorsement that no human messenger could provide alone. This desire recurs in 6:8-9, where the disbelievers say, "Why has an angel not been sent down to him?" and the Qur'an responds, "Had We sent down an angel, the matter would have been decided, and they would not be reprieved" (6:8). The answer there reveals the hidden consequence of the demand: if an angel appeared, the era of testing would end immediately, because the evidence would be so overwhelming that denial would become impossible. The disbelievers were asking for the very thing that would end their opportunity to choose.

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the disbelievers' objection was not born of genuine curiosity but of contempt. The kings and rulers of the era considered themselves above walking in the markets among ordinary people; they expected God's representative to meet their standards of grandeur. The objection exposes their framework: in their world, authority is performed through distance from common life. The Quran reverses this: authority is demonstrated through proximity, because the messenger must reach the people he warns. An angel in the sky would impress; a man in the market instructs. The disbelievers wanted to be impressed. The Qur'an wanted them to be warned.

**Eating, Walking, and the Prophetic Pattern**

The specific charges — eating food and walking in markets — are addressed directly in 25:20: "We never sent any messenger before you who did not eat food and go about in market-places." The response reframes the objection by universalizing it: every prophet in history shared these characteristics. Noah, Abraham, Moses, David, Solomon — all ate, all walked among their people, all lived within the ordinary conditions of human existence. The disbelievers' demand for a non-human messenger would disqualify the very prophets they themselves acknowledged. If eating food and walking in markets disqualify Muhammad ﷺ, it disqualifies Moses and Abraham as well.

The deeper point is theological. The Quran insists that God's messengers are human beings, not angels, because the purpose of prophethood is to demonstrate that human life, lived under divine guidance, is sufficient for God's purposes. An angel messenger would prove that only angels can obey; a human messenger proves that humans can. The objection in 25:7 targets precisely this point without understanding it. The disbelievers see the Prophet's humanity as a deficiency; the Qur'an presents it as the design. The response, when it comes in 25:20, will make this explicit: the humanity of the messenger is not an accident but a pattern, and the pattern is universal.

**The Word "Co-Warner" and What It Reveals**

The phrase *nadīran maʿahu* — "a warner with him" or "a co-warner" — deserves separate attention because of what it concedes. The disbelievers do not deny that the Prophet is a warner; they deny that he is sufficient alone. The word *nadīr* (warner) is the same word used in 25:1 to describe the Prophet's mission — "so that he may be a warner to the whole world." The disbelievers, in the very act of rejecting him, repeat the Qur'an's own description of his function. They accept the category; they reject the person. This is a subtler form of denial than outright rejection: it says, in effect, "we believe in warning, but we do not believe you are the one doing it correctly." The demand for an angel co-warner is thus a demand for mediation — for someone else to verify the warning before it is accepted. The Qur'an's response is that the warning stands on its own, delivered by a human being chosen by God, and no co-signer is required. The demand for authentication is itself a form of rejection, because it places a condition on obedience that God has not set.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:8

> or a treasure had been cast down to him, or he had had a garden from which he may eat!” And the wrongdoers say ˹to the believers˺, “You are only following a bewitched man.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Wealth and Land as Divine Credentials**

The disbelievers continue their list of what a real messenger should possess. Having demanded an angel in 25:7, they now demand material signs: a treasure sent down from heaven and a garden from which the messenger can eat. The pattern of the demand reveals the values at work. In seventh-century Makkah, authority was measured by two visible markers: accumulated wealth and landed property. A man who possessed neither could not, in the social logic of the Quraysh, claim superiority over those who possessed both. The request for a treasure (*kanz*) and a garden (*jannah*) is not a request for proof of divine mission; it is a request for the social credentials the audience already respects. The disbelievers are not asking for a sign from God; they are asking for a sign that conforms to their own standards.

The parallel in 43:53 records a similar demand about Moses: "Why were armlets of gold not cast upon him, or angels not accompanying him?" The structure is identical: the messenger lacks what the audience considers necessary for authority, and the lack is treated as disproof of the message. The Qur'an's consistent response is that these material signs would not produce faith — they would produce obedience, which is not the same thing. Sūrah 6:111 makes the point explicitly: "Even if We had sent down to them the angels, and the dead had spoken to them, and We had gathered all things before them, they still would not have believed — unless Allah had willed." The disbelievers in 25:8 are asking for evidence, but the evidence they want is designed to satisfy a criterion they invented, not one God set. A treasure would prove wealth, not prophethood. A garden would prove ownership, not revelation. The demands are category errors dressed as reasonable requests.

**The Charge of Bewitchment**

The verse's final clause shifts the accusation to a different register: *mashūr* — bewitched. After calling the Prophet a fabricator (25:4), a borrower of ancient tales (25:5), and an ordinary man unworthy of prophethood (25:7), the disbelievers add one more label: he is under a spell. This is not a casual insult; it is an attempt to explain the Quran's undeniable effect on its listeners. The Qur'an moved people to tears, to conversion, to a willingness to abandon their families and their tribal allegiances. If it was not divine, and it was not ordinary human speech, some third explanation was needed. Sorcery filled the gap.

The charge appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in the mouths of other communities: the people of Pharaoh accused Moses of sorcery (10:76; 20:63; 26:34), and the people of ʿĀd accused their messenger of bewitchment (7:66). In pre-Islamic Arabia, sorcery was taken seriously as a real phenomenon, not merely as a metaphor for eloquence. To call someone *mashūr* was to place him in a dangerous category — one that could justify violence, since a sorcerer was understood to be a genuine threat to the community. The accusation against the Prophet ﷺ carried social consequences for everyone around him: anyone who followed a bewitched man could be accused of being bewitched themselves, or of complicity in sorcery.

**The Social Dynamics of the Objection**

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the disbelievers used the charge of bewitchment not only against the Prophet but directly at the believers: "You are only following a bewitched man." The shift from attacking the message to attacking the followers is a deliberate social strategy. By labeling the Prophet as sorcerer, the disbelievers made allegiance to him socially costly. This is the mechanism of the trial (*fitnah*) that 25:20 will describe: "We have made some of you a trial for others." The disbelievers' mockery is itself the instrument of testing, and the believers' continued allegiance in the face of mockery is the proof of their faith. The word *ẓālimūn* (wrongdoers) is the Qur'an's own label for the disbelievers in this verse, and it is applied not to their theological error alone but to the injustice of their social pressure. Labeling a truthful person as bewitched, and using that label to punish his followers, is *ẓulm* — the placing of something in the wrong position, the distortion of reality by force.

**What the Disbelievers Could Not Say**

The cumulative list of objections across 25:4-8 reveals the disbelievers' real difficulty. They cannot fault the content of the Quran on its merits. They cannot identify the human source. They cannot produce anything comparable. And they cannot ignore its effect on the people who hear it. Every objection is an attempt to explain away what they cannot explain: a text that exceeds human capacity, delivered by a man who fits no category of authority they recognize. The charge of bewitchment is the last resort, the explanation that fills the gap when all others fail. When fabrication, borrowing, and ordinary humanity are insufficient, sorcery is invoked — not because the evidence points there, but because no other explanation remains. The disbelievers have exhausted their categories, and the charge of bewitchment is the sound of that exhaustion.

**The Parallel in Saul and the Demand for Signs**

The disbelievers' demand for wealth and material proof before accepting the Prophet echoes a pattern found earlier in the Qur'an's account of the Israelites. In 2:247, when Samuel anointed Saul (Ṭālūt) as king, the Israelites objected: "How can he have authority over us when we are more worthy of authority than him, and he has not been given abundant wealth?" The objection is structurally identical to the one in 25:8: a man is chosen by God for leadership, and the people reject him because he lacks the material markers they associate with authority. The Qur'an's response to the Israelites was that God had chosen Saul and given him knowledge and stature; the response to the Quraysh, stated in 25:10, is that God can give far better than what they demand. The pattern repeats across prophetic history because the error is the same: measuring spiritual authority by material wealth, and rejecting what God chooses because it does not match what humans prefer.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:9

> See ˹O Prophet˺ how they call you names! So they have gone so ˹far˺ astray that they cannot find the ˹Right˺ Way

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse of Divine Observation**

The verse turns away from the disbelievers and speaks directly to the Prophet ﷺ: *unẓur kayfaḍarabū laka'l-amthāl* — "Look how they set forth their parables about you." The command to "see" is not an invitation to gather evidence or construct a response; it is a divine observation offered to the Prophet as consolation. God is showing him the absurdity of his opponents' position. They have called him a fabricator, a borrower of tales, an ordinary man unworthy of prophethood, and a sorcerer — all in the space of five verses — and each label contradicts the others. The purpose of *unẓur* is not to instruct the Prophet but to reassure him: the disbelievers' confusion is visible, and it is self-inflicted.

The word *amthāl* (parables, comparisons, descriptions) is significant. The disbelievers were not making arguments; they were making comparisons — comparing the Prophet to ordinary men, to sorcerers, to storytellers. Each comparison was designed to place him in a familiar category so that the unfamiliar could be dismissed. The Qur'an calls these *amthāl* — a word it reserves elsewhere for the illustrative parables God Himself sets forth (e.g., 2:26; 13:17; 24:35). The irony is sharp: the disbelievers' parables are false comparisons that obscure rather than illuminate, while God's parables reveal. The word choice exposes the disbelievers' method without naming it as such. They are doing what the Qur'an does — setting forth parables — but doing it badly, because their parables are designed to hide the truth rather than to display it.

**Astray and Without a Way**

The second half of the verse — *fa-ḍallū lā yastaṭī'ūna sabīlā* — declares that the disbelievers have gone so far astray that they cannot find the way. The word *ḍalāl* (astray, lost) is the Qur'an's standard term for misguidance, and *sabīl* (way, path) is its counterpart for the straight path of guidance. The pairing is structural: to be astray is to have lost the path, and the path that is lost here is not a physical road but the ability to assess the Prophet ﷺ correctly. The disbelievers have made so many incompatible accusations — fabrication, copying, bewitchment, ordinary humanity — that they have exhausted their explanatory frameworks and arrived at confusion. They cannot find the way because they have paved over every route that might have led them to the truth.

Al-Rāzī reads the verse as a comment on the futility of the disbelievers' method. By advancing multiple contradictory explanations, they have not strengthened their case but weakened it. If the Quran is fabricated, it is not copied; if it is copied, it is not fabricated; if the Prophet is bewitched, he is not an ordinary man; if he is an ordinary man, he is not bewitched. Each accusation, taken separately, might seem plausible; taken together, they cancel each other out. The disbelievers have gone astray not because they lack intelligence but because their refusal to accept the truth has forced them into positions that contradict one another.

**The Prophetic Response to Mockery**

The tone of this verse is instructive for understanding the Qur'an's guidance to the Prophet ﷺ in the face of sustained ridicule. The divine instruction is not "argue back" or "defend yourself" or "produce a sign." It is "look." The Prophet is invited to observe his opponents' confusion from the perspective of one who already knows the truth. This posture — observation rather than reaction — appears elsewhere in the Qur'an's address to the Prophet. In 6:176: "Do not grieve over those who rush into disbelief." In 15:97: "We certainly know that your heart is distressed by what they say." The emotional reality is acknowledged, but the strategic instruction is the same: observe, do not be consumed.

The word *fa-ḍallū* ("so they have gone astray") places the result on the disbelievers, not on the Prophet. He is not responsible for their misguidance; it is the product of their own choices. The Qur'an consistently holds this position: the messenger delivers, and the recipient decides. The Prophet's task is to convey, and the observation in 25:9 that his opponents have lost their way is a confirmation that the failure is theirs. The verse ends without a rebuttal, without a counterargument, without a new piece of evidence. It ends with a diagnosis: they are lost, and they cannot find the way. The diagnosis is enough.

**The Sūrah's Structural Placement of the Verse**

Verse 25:9 occupies a specific structural position: it is the last verse before the sūrah shifts from describing the disbelievers' objections to describing their consequences. The next verse (25:10) turns from the disbelievers' accusations to God's promise of gardens and palaces, and from there the sūrah moves into its extended eschatological passage (25:11-19). The placement of 25:9 at this hinge point is deliberate. The sūrah has given the disbelievers five verses to make their case — fabrication, copying, bewitchment, demands for signs — and now it pauses to pronounce judgment on their method before moving to the consequences. The pause is brief: one verse, one observation, one diagnosis. But it serves as the closing argument of the prosecution before the sentencing begins. The disbelievers have spoken; the sūrah has recorded their words faithfully; and now it pronounces, in God's voice, that those words have led the speakers nowhere. What follows — the fire, the garden, the courtroom — is the result of the path the disbelievers chose when they lost the ability to find any other way. The verse's placement is the sūrah's way of saying: the case is closed, and the verdict is in.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:10

> Blessed is the One Who—if He wills—can give you far better than ˹all˺ that: gardens under which rivers flow, and palaces as well

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response to Material Demands**

The disbelievers had demanded a treasure and a garden for the Prophet ﷺ in 25:8 — material signs that would confirm his prophethood in terms the Quraysh understood. God's answer does not provide those signs. Instead, it promises something better: gardens with rivers beneath them and palaces (*quṣūr*). The promise is not directed at the disbelievers who demanded the signs but at the Prophet who endured the demand. The shift from the audience's terms to God's terms is the verse's central move. The disbelievers asked for proof on earth; the answer points to reward in the Hereafter. They measured authority by what a person owns; the verse measures it by what a person is promised. The contrast is between two value systems: the one that judges by appearance, and the one that judges by outcome.

The word *tabāraka* ("Blessed") opens the verse as it opened the sūrah in 25:1, creating a structural echo. The One who sent down the Criterion is the same One who can give gardens and palaces. The repetition ties the revelation to the reward: the Quran is the means, and Paradise is the destination. The "if He wills" (*in shā'a*) introduces divine discretion — the promise is not automatic but contingent on God's will, a theological point that keeps the relationship between human effort and divine gift in tension. The Qur'an consistently holds both sides: humans are responsible for their choices, and God's mercy is not earned but granted. The phrase *in shā'a* does not introduce doubt; it affirms freedom — God promises from freedom, not from obligation, and a promise made freely is more reliable than one made under compulsion.

**Quṣūr: Palaces as a Marker of Restoration**

The word *quṣūr* (palaces, mansions) appears in several Qur'anic passages describing Paradise. In 7:74, it refers to the stone dwellings built by the Thamūd, the people of Ṣāliḥ — a historical usage that grounds the word in material reality before applying it to the eschatological context. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr note that for the Quraysh, *quṣūr* meant any dwelling made of stone, which was a mark of permanence and status in a society where most structures were made of mud and palm fronds. The promise of palaces in Paradise thus uses the audience's own standard of luxury and exceeds it.

In 22:45 — "How many a city We have destroyed while it was doing wrong, lying in ruins, with abandoned wells and lofty palaces" — the *quṣūr* are ruins, testimony to the impermanence of worldly power. The word in 25:10 reverses that image: the palaces God promises are permanent, not because stone endures but because the One who builds them does not allow decay. The contrast between the ruined palaces of destroyed cities and the eternal palaces of Paradise is implicit in the Qur'an's use of the same word across different contexts. The disbelievers who demanded a garden and a treasure in 25:8 are offered something their language already contains but their imagination could not reach.

**Why the Promise Comes Before the Warning**

The sequence of the sūrah is instructive. After five verses of accusation (25:4-8) and one verse of observation (25:9), the next response is not a warning but a promise. The garden and the palace come before the fire and the chains. This ordering is not accidental; it reflects the Qur'an's consistent preference for attraction over fear as the primary mode of persuasion. The warning will follow — 25:11 introduces the denial of the Hour and the preparation of the Blaze — but the promise comes first. The reader encounters the possibility of reward before the reality of punishment, and the reward is framed in terms the audience already desires: flowing water, permanent homes, a life without want.

Al-Rāzī observes that the phrase "far better than all that" (*khayran min dhālik*) compares the promised gardens not only to the treasure and garden the disbelievers demanded but to everything they possess in this world. The comparison is comprehensive: whatever the Quraysh have — their trade goods, their properties, their social standing — the Hereafter offers more. The Prophet ﷺ, who lived modestly and refused the Quraysh's offers of wealth and kingship, is here told that his refusal will be rewarded with something those offers could never have matched. The verse does not describe Paradise in detail; it names gardens and palaces and stops. The restraint is deliberate: the promise is better than what the disbelievers imagine, and any description would limit it to the limits of their imagination.

**The Relationship Between 25:8 and 25:10**

The verbal echoes between the two verses are exact. The disbelievers said *kanz* (treasure) and *jannah* (garden); the response offers *jannāt* (gardens, plural) and *quṣūr* (palaces). The disbelievers demanded one garden; God offers many. They demanded a treasure for one lifetime; the gardens under which rivers flow last forever (25:15-16). The echo is not accidental; it is a direct rebuttal that uses the disbelievers' own vocabulary and exceeds it on every count. The word *quṣūr* replaces *kanz* — not a hoarded treasure but a built dwelling, not wealth but habitation. The shift from what one has to where one lives changes the frame entirely: the disbelievers measured worth by accumulation, and God measures it by dwelling, by rest, by a place where the rivers flow and the walls do not crumble. The promise is better than what was demanded not because it is larger but because it is different in kind: the disbelievers asked for proof, and God offered a home — a permanent dwelling that satisfies the deepest human need for belonging and rest.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:11

> In fact, they deny the Hour. And for the deniers of the Hour, We have prepared a blazing Fire

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Root Diagnosis**

The sūrah identifies what is really wrong with the disbelievers. After cataloguing their objections — fabrication, borrowed tales, bewitchment, demands for material signs — the text names the underlying condition: *bal kadhdhabū bi's-sā'ah* — "In fact, they deny the Hour." The word *bal* ("in fact," "rather") functions as a correction. Everything the disbelievers have said — the accusations, the mockery, the demands — is surface. The real problem is not that they find the Prophet inadequate or the Quran unconvincing; it is that they reject the reality of accountability after death. Every objection is an expression of that rejection dressed in different clothes. If there is no Hour, there is no consequence; if there is no consequence, the messenger is unnecessary; if the messenger is unnecessary, the message can be dismissed. The verse strips away the arguments and exposes the foundation.

The Hour (*al-sā'ah*) is the Qur'an's term for the moment the created order ends and the Day of Judgment begins. It appears throughout the Qur'an as the single event the disbelievers most consistently deny: "They seek to hasten the Hour" (42:18); "The disbelievers are in doubt about the Hour" (41:54); "Woe on that Day to those who deny" (77:15). The denial is not incidental but structural to their worldview. If the Hour is real, everything changes: wealth is temporary, status is meaningless, and the man who walks in the markets carrying a message from God is more important than the kings who mock him. The denial of the Hour is the denial of the entire moral architecture the Quran is building, and the sūrah names it here as the root from which all other objections grow.

**Saʿīr: The Blaze Prepared in Advance**

The verse describes the punishment for denial as *saʿīr* — a blazing fire, a kindled flame. The word is used across the Qur'an as a near-synonym for *nār* (fire) but carries an additional intensity: *saʿīr* is fire that has been set alight deliberately, fire with agency. The term appears in 4:10, 4:55, 17:97, 22:4, 31:21, 33:64, 35:6, 42:7, 48:13, 67:5, 67:10, 67:11, 76:4, and 84:12. Its recurrence across diverse contexts — warnings to the disbelievers, descriptions of the Day, assurances to the Prophet — makes it one of the Qur'an's primary vocabulary items for the punishment of the Hereafter.

The word *aʿtadnā* ("We have prepared") uses the past tense, giving the fire a quality of already-existing readiness. This has theological implications that the commentators debate. Some, noting the preposition "for the deniers of the Hour," read it as supporting predestination: the fire was prepared specifically for those who will deny, suggesting that their fate was determined before their actions. Others read the verse as conditional: the fire is prepared as a destination for whoever freely chooses to deny. Al-Rāzī records both positions, noting that the verse can support either reading depending on the theological framework applied. What is not in dispute is the certainty: the fire is not a possibility but a prepared reality. The preparation has already happened; the fire is not being built as the disbelievers approach. It is waiting.

**Denial as the Engine of Every Other Error**

The verse's placement after the catalogue of objections is deliberate. By naming the denial of the Hour as the root, the sūrah reorders the disbelievers' arguments. The charge of fabrication (25:4) is not about evidence; it is about the refusal to accept that accountability exists. The demand for a treasure (25:8) is not about proof; it is about the assumption that material success is the only measure of worth, an assumption that collapses if the Hereafter is real. The accusation of bewitchment (25:8) is not about the Prophet's character; it is about the need to explain away an effect they cannot accept, because accepting it would mean accepting the source.

This diagnosis — that all objections reduce to one denial — is the sūrah's interpretive key. The rest of the chapter will describe the consequences of that denial in escalating detail: the fire will see them from afar (25:12), they will be compressed in a narrow space (25:13), they will call for destruction upon destruction (25:14). But the cause has been identified here, and everything that follows is consequence. The sūrah does not treat the disbelievers' various objections as separate errors requiring separate rebuttals; it treats them as symptoms of a single disease. The disease is the denial of the Hour, and the cure — faith in the unseen and accountability — is the very thing the disbelievers refuse.

**The Word "Blaze" and Its Place in the Qur'an's Vocabulary of Punishment**

The Qur'an uses a rich vocabulary for the punishment of the Hereafter, and each word carries a distinct shade of meaning. *Nār* (fire) is the most general. *Jaḥīm* (blazing fire) emphasizes intensity. *Saʿīr* (blaze, kindled flame) emphasizes preparation and agency — a fire that has been set alight deliberately. *Ḥuṭamah* (crushing) emphasizes destruction. *Lazā* (scorching flame) emphasizes heat. *Ḥamīm* (boiling water) emphasizes the liquid form of punishment. The word *saʿīr* in 25:11 belongs to this wider vocabulary, and its specific contribution is the notion of readiness. The fire is not a natural phenomenon that happens to exist; it is a prepared instrument, built for a purpose, kindled for a set of people who chose to deny what they knew to be true. The preparation is past tense — *aʿtadnā*, "We have prepared" — which means the fire exists now, before the Day arrives, waiting for its inhabitants. This temporal detail matters because it removes the possibility that the punishment is improvised or proportionate to the crime in a merely reactive sense. The fire was prepared in advance because the denial was foreseen, and the foreknowledge does not diminish the justice of the outcome.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:12

> Once it sees them from a distance, they will hear it fuming and growling

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hell as a Living Presence**

The verse uses a striking grammatical construction: the fire sees. *Idhā ra'athum min makānin baʿīd* — "When it sees them from a place far off." Hell is not described as a passive location; it is given the faculty of sight. This animation of the fire is one of the most vivid features of the Qur'anic eschatology. The fire has perception, and it perceives its victims before they arrive. The grammar places Hell as the subject of the verb "to see," making it an agent rather than a destination. The disbelievers do not enter Hell; Hell perceives them. The difference is enormous: a place can be escaped from; a perceiving entity has already made contact.

Al-Qurṭubī records a ḥadīth portraying the Fire as a sentient being on the Day of Resurrection — endowed with vision, hearing, and speech — that will seize the polytheists as swiftly as a bird swallows a seed. The theological debate over whether these attributes should be understood literally or metaphorically divides the Muʿtazilites, who reject such corporeal descriptions, from those who accept that God can endue the fire with whatever faculties He wills. The disagreement concerns the mode of interpretation, not the substance: the text itself states plainly that the fire perceives its victims. The ḥadīth, reported by al-Qurṭubī through multiple chains, gives the fire agency that goes beyond what the verse requires but does not contradict it. Whether the fire literally possesses sensory organs or the description conveys its terrifying responsiveness in human terms, the effect on the audience is the same: Hell is aware, and its awareness is part of its horror.

The phrase *min makānin baʿīd* ("from a place far off") does two things simultaneously. It establishes that the distance between the disbelievers and the fire is great — they are not yet inside it — and it establishes that the fire is already aware of their approach. The effect is one of inevitability. The fire does not wait passively; it anticipates. The victims are moving toward something that has already perceived them, and the perception produces a response: *taghayyuran wa zaftīrān* — "fuming and growling."

**The Sounds of the Fire**

The two nouns *taghayyur* and *zaftīr* describe the fire's audible response. *Taghayyur* conveys rage, fury, the sound of something boiling with anger. *Zaftīr* is a low, heavy breathing — a growl, the sound of something enormous and barely restrained. Together they paint a picture of a fire that is not merely burning but seething, a fire that has been building pressure and is now about to be released. The sounds are described from the perspective of the disbelievers: *samiʿū lahā* — "they will hear it." The hearing precedes the seeing for the victims; the sound arrives first, filling the space between them and the fire before they can make out its shape.

The Qur'an's use of sound to convey the horror of Hell appears in other passages as well. In 67:7 — "When they are thrown into it, they hear its roaring as it boils" — the sound is produced by the fire at the moment of contact. In 25:12, the sound precedes contact, creating a different psychological effect: dread before the experience, fear of something that has not yet arrived but is clearly coming. The extended approach multiplies the torment: the disbelievers spend the approach listening to what awaits them, and every step closer brings a louder growl.

**Distance as Torment**

The verse makes the distance itself part of the punishment. The disbelievers can hear the fire from far away — its rage, its growling — and they know they are moving toward it. This anticipatory suffering is a distinct element of the Qur'anic vision of Hell. In 14:50 — "Their garment will be of boiling fluid, and the Fire will cover their faces" — the punishment is physical and immediate. In 25:12, the punishment is psychological and gradual: the victims hear first, then presumably see, then feel. The gradual approach extends the torment across time rather than compressing it into a single moment.

Al-Rāzī notes that the fire's distance from the disbelievers and their distance from the fire are different things. The fire is far from them in the sense that it has not yet reached them; they are far from it in the sense that they are still being dragged toward it. The verse uses the passive construction — they are seen, they hear — making the disbelievers the objects of the fire's perception rather than its agents. They do not choose to approach; they are brought. The fire does not move to meet them; it waits. The passivity of the human and the activity of the fire reverse the expected relationship: the created thing has become more powerful than the people who denied its creator. The fire that the disbelievers dismissed as a myth in 25:11 is now the thing that sees them, hears them, and prepares to receive them.

**The Graduated Architecture of Dread**

The verse's contribution to the sūrah's eschatological sequence is the concept of graduated approach. The punishment of Hell as the Qur'an describes it is not a single event but a process, and 25:12 captures the first stage: perception at a distance. The disbelievers are still far from the fire, but they can hear it — its rage, its growling — and the hearing begins the punishment before the heat does. This graduated approach appears in other Qur'anic passages as well. In 32:12 — "If only you could see when the criminals will hang their heads before their Lord" — the humiliation precedes the sentencing. In 77:25-30 — "Woe on that Day to the deniers! Did We not make the earth a resting place, and the mountains as stakes?" — the questioning precedes the punishment. The Qur'an's eschatology is never instantaneous; it is always staged, because staging extends the experience of consequence across time, and time in Hell is itself part of the punishment.

**The Transition to Confinement**

Verse 25:12 ends with the fire fuming and growling, and verse 25:13 will describe the disbelievers tossed into a narrow space. The transition from sound to confinement is the next stage in the Qur'an's layered description of Hell's punishment. The disbelievers first hear the fire from a distance (25:12), then experience its compression (25:13), then call for annihilation (25:13), then are denied even that release (25:14). The sequence is designed to escalate: each verse adds a new dimension of suffering — auditory, spatial, existential — and the accumulation creates a picture of punishment that is total, not partial. The fire does not punish one sense or one faculty; it engages the whole person, from hearing to seeing to feeling to the deepest wish for non-existence.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:13

> And when they are tossed into a narrow place inside ˹Hell˺, chained together, then and there they will cry out for ˹instant˺ destruction

**Expanded Commentary**

**Compression After the Roar**

The description of Hell escalates from sound to confinement. Verse 25:12 gave the fire a voice; verse 25:13 places the disbelievers inside it — not in an open expanse but in a *makān ḍayyiq*, a narrow, cramped space. The Arabic root *ḍ-y-q* conveys constriction, suffocation, the inability to move freely. The fire does not merely surround them; it presses against them from every side. The effect is the opposite of what the disbelievers valued in life: they were people who measured authority by spaciousness — gardens, palaces, open markets — and the punishment strips that away entirely. What remains is closeness without comfort, proximity without intimacy, and pressure without relief.

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have described the constriction using the image of a spearhead forced into the shaft of a spear — a thing compressed so tightly that no gap remains. The metaphor conveys both the tightness and the force: the bodies inside the narrow place are not settling naturally; they are packed in, pushed together by an external power. The passive verb *ulqū* ("they are tossed") removes the last trace of agency. They do not walk into the narrow place; they are thrown. The throwing is itself a humiliation — the disbelievers who walked proudly in the markets of Makkah, who expected their Prophet to meet their standards of dignity, are now handled without regard for their preference or their pride.

**Muqarranīn: Chained Together**

The word *muqarranīn* describes the state of the disbelievers in the narrow place: bound, chained, connected to something they cannot escape. The commentators offer several interpretations of what the binding involves. Some hold that people will be chained to their individual devils — the companions (*qarin*) who whispered to them during life — so that the relationship between tempter and tempted is made permanent and visible. Others hold that their hands will be chained to their necks, a posture of total submission and helplessness. Al-Qurṭubī records both readings. What they share is the removal of the ability to move, to separate, to distance oneself from the other people in the same confinement.

The word *qarana* (to bind, to join, to chain) appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in a different register. In 4:36 — "Do not join one sin with another" — the word describes a moral binding: the accumulation of wrongs. In 25:13, the binding is physical, but the echo of the moral usage is not accidental. In life, the disbelievers accumulated sins without binding; in the narrow place, they are bound without accumulation — everything they avoided in consequence is now forced upon them in form. The chains are the visible shape of the invisible chains their sins forged: every act of denial, every mockery of the Prophet, every demand for a sign they did not deserve — all of it has become iron.

**Thubūr: The Cry for Annihilation**

The word *thubūr* means destruction, annihilation, the complete ending of existence. The disbelievers, pressed together in the narrow place, call out for it — not for relief, not for mercy, but for obliteration. This is the most extreme response the Qur'anic Hell can produce: the desire not to endure, not to escape, but to cease existing entirely. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *thubūr* as a kind of lamentation — a wail that carries the wish for extinction within it.

The theological implication is significant. The disbelievers who denied the Hour — who claimed that death is the end and there is nothing beyond — are now experiencing the reality of "beyond" and finding it so intolerable that they wish for the very thing they once assumed was true: non-existence. Their denial of the Hereafter included, implicitly, a denial that anything could be worse than nothing. The narrow place proves them wrong. The cry for *thubūr* is the confession that existence in Hell is worse than the non-existence they once took for granted. This confession will be answered in 25:14 with a devastating reversal: the annihilation they request will be multiplied, not granted.

**The Contrast With the Garden**

The juxtaposition of the narrow place in 25:13 with the gardens of 25:10 is one of the sūrah's structural devices. The gardens are described as places of flowing rivers, wide spaces, and permanent palaces; the narrow place is defined by its lack of space, its chains, and its absence of exit. The disbelievers demanded a garden in 25:8; they receive the opposite. The believers who were not given gardens in this world will receive them in the next; the disbelievers who lived in relative comfort will be compressed into the narrowest possible space. The sūrah does not comment on the irony; it simply places the two descriptions adjacent to each other and allows the reader to feel the weight of the contrast.

**The Active Fire and the Passive Human**

The grammar of 25:13 reinforces the reversal of power described in 25:12. In life, the disbelievers were agents: they chose their gods, they demanded their signs, they mocked the Prophet, they shaped the social world of Makkah according to their preferences. In the narrow place, they are objects. The verb *ulqū* ("they are tossed") is passive; the subject who tosses is unnamed, presumably God or His angels. The disbelievers do not walk, do not choose, do not act. They are acted upon.
