The word *muqarranīn* ("chained together") reinforces the passivity: they cannot separate, cannot move, cannot even choose their companions in punishment. The contrast with their former agency is total. In Makkah, they moved freely through markets and gathering places, controlled their social environment, and shaped their identities through their choices. In the narrow place, every freedom has been removed, and what remains is the bare human being, stripped of status, stripped of movement, stripped of the ability to act. The punishment is not merely pain; it is the removal of everything the disbelievers used to define themselves.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:14

> ˹They will be told,˺ “Do not cry only once for destruction, but cry many times over!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply That Compounds the Torment**

The verse is addressed directly to the people who have just cried out for annihilation in 25:13. Their plea — *thubūr*, destruction — is met not with silence, not with mercy, and not with the mercy of extinction, but with mockery. "Do not cry for one destruction; cry for many." The response is the Qur'an's most unflinching reversal of the relationship between punishment and prayer. The disbelievers' cry was the ultimate supplication — the wish to cease existing — and the answer tells them that one annihilation is not enough. Whatever they thought destruction would provide — relief, an ending, escape — will not be granted, and they should prepare for more.

The Arabic intensifies the cruelty of the response. *Lā tadʿū'l-yawma thubūran wāḥidan wa'dʿū thubūran kathīrā* — the word *kathīr* (many, numerous) replaces the singular with the plural. The punishment is not a single event that ends; it is a series, an unbroken chain of destructions, each one followed by another. The commentators understand this as a reference to the perpetual nature of Hell's torment. The pain does not peak and then fade; it renews. Each moment of suffering is followed by another moment of equal or greater suffering, and the desire for it to stop is itself denied. The word *kathīr* does not specify a number; it is indefinite, open-ended. The disbelievers asked for one destruction and are told to ask for many — and "many" in this context means without limit.

**The Qur'an's Recurring Theme of Unending Punishment**

The concept of punishment without relief appears throughout the Qur'an in various formulations. In 4:56 — "Every time their skins are roasted through, We will replace them with other skins so they may taste the punishment" — the renewal is physical: the body is destroyed and reconstituted so that it can suffer again. In 20:74 — "Whoever comes to his Lord as a criminal, for him is Hell, where he neither dies nor lives" — the punishment occupies a space between death and life, denying both. In 78:23-25 — "They will remain in it for ages; they will not taste any coolness or any drink, except boiling fluid and dark, murky filth" — the duration is specified as ages (*ḥuqub*, periods of time so long they become indistinguishable from eternity).

The statement in 25:14 adds a distinct tone: it is spoken directly to the victims, in their own words, turned against them. They asked for destruction; the answer uses their word — *thubūr* — and multiplies it. The rhetorical technique is one the Qur'an employs in several eschatological passages: the disbelievers' own speech is quoted back to them, reframed. In 23:107-108, the people of Hell say, "Our Lord, remove us from it, and if we return to evil, then we will be wrongdoers," and the response is, "Remain despised in it, and do not speak to Me." In 25:14, the response is not silence but amplification: you asked for destruction once; here is destruction upon destruction.

**Why the Verse Matters Theologically**

The denial of the cry for destruction establishes a theological principle: the punishment of the Hereafter is not corrective. It does not reform the punished; it does not lead to a better state. In this world, suffering can produce growth, repentance, or at least endurance. In the Hereafter as the Qur'an describes it, suffering produces only the desire for more suffering to end — a desire that is itself denied. The distinction between worldly and otherworldly punishment is one the Qur'an makes explicit elsewhere: "The punishment of the Hereafter is more terrible and more lasting" (20:127). The word "more lasting" (*aqām*) establishes that the punishment endures, and 25:14 confirms that it endures without interruption.

This has implications for the moral argument the sūrah is building. If the punishment were temporary, the disbelievers' gamble — deny the Hour, enjoy the world, endure whatever comes after — would be rational. The denial of even a single annihilation removes that rationality. The disbelievers have no exit strategy, and the verse tells them so in their own language. The multiplication of destruction is not sadism; it is the logical consequence of a denial that was itself multiplied. The disbelievers denied the Hour many times, in many ways, through many objections; the punishment returns their denial in kind, multiplied beyond counting.

**The Spoken Tone: Irony as Punishment**

The tone of the verse deserves attention. It is not solemn; it is ironic. The disbelievers are told to cry out for destruction — not once but many times — and the instruction carries the quality of dark humor. The punishment has turned their plea into a performance, and the performance will never end. This is a distinct register in the Qur'an's eschatological passages: alongside the solemn warnings and the vivid descriptions, there is a voice that mocks, that quotes the disbelievers' words back at them with an edge that cuts. The irony is not gratuitous; it mirrors the disbelievers' own mockery of the Prophet in 25:7-8. What they gave, they receive. What they sowed in laughter, they reap in the fire's response.

**The Sūrah's Place in the Qur'an's Eschatological Tradition**

Verse 25:14 belongs to a family of Qur'anic passages that describe the disbelievers' response to Hell with the same mixture of despair and denial. In 35:36-37, the people of Hell say, "Our Lord, remove us and we will do righteous deeds, not what we used to do," and the response is, "Did We not give you a lifespan in which anyone who would take heed could have done so? And the warner came to you, so taste it." In 43:77, they cry out, "O Mālik, let your Lord finish us off," and Mālik replies, "You will remain." The pattern is consistent: the people in Hell ask for an end, and the answer is that there is no end. The verse in 25:14 fits this pattern exactly. The disbelievers' request for destruction is turned into a command to request more destruction, and the command is itself the punishment. The multiplication of the request is the multiplication of the suffering, and the suffering does not resolve.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:15

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Is this better or the Garden of Eternity which the righteous have been promised, as a reward and ˹an ultimate˺ destination

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetorical Turn**

After four verses of unbroken description of Hell — the fire that sees (25:12), the narrow confinement (25:13), the denial of annihilation (25:14) — the sūrah pivots with a question addressed to the Prophet: *qul*, "Say." The question is one the audience is meant to answer for themselves: "Is this better, or the Garden of Eternity which the righteous have been promised?" The word *this* (*dhālik*) refers to everything described in 25:11-14: the fire, the compression, the chains, the multiplied destruction. The Garden of Eternity is presented as its opposite: not confinement but spaciousness, not destruction but permanence, not chains but rest. The question does not need an answer in the text; the answer is self-evident, and the verse's power lies in the audience's forced recognition that the self-evident answer is available only through the path the disbelievers have rejected.

The technique — a question whose answer condemns the questioner — is one the Qur'an uses in several passages. In 87:16-17: "But you prefer the life of this world, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting." In 9:38: "What is the matter with you that when you are told, 'Go forth in the cause of Allah,' you cling heavily to the earth?" The question in 25:15 does not argue; it juxtaposes. The reader sees both outcomes side by side and must choose. The juxtaposition is the argument, and the argument requires nothing beyond the two options laid out in the same sentence.

**Khuld: The Eternity That Gives the Garden Its Name**

The word *khuld* (eternity, everlastingness) is the same root from which the verb *khālidīn* ("abiding") comes in 25:16. It means "to remain," "to stay," and when applied to the Hereafter it carries the force of permanence without end. The Garden is not a temporary reward that expires; it is a state that continues. This is the feature that distinguishes it from every earthly garden, every human pleasure, every material satisfaction — all of which are subject to time. The disbelievers asked for a garden in 25:8; the Garden promised here exceeds their request in kind, not merely in degree. An earthly garden has seasons; the Garden of Eternity does not. An earthly garden can be seized by an enemy; the Garden of Eternity is beyond reach.

Al-Rāzī observes that the word *khuld* applies to both Paradise and Hell. The righteous abide in the Garden; the wicked abide in the Fire. The same permanence guarantees both outcomes, and this symmetry is part of the Qur'an's moral architecture: the reward for obedience is as irreversible as the punishment for refusal. The theological debate about whether the punishment is truly eternal or whether it might eventually end is addressed by the use of *khuld* in passages like 4:93: "Whoever kills a believer intentionally, his reward is Hell, wherein he will abide forever (*khālidan fīhā*)." The permanence is stated as a fact, not a possibility.

**Jazā'an wa Maṣīrā: Reward and Destination**

The two closing nouns — *jazā'* (reward, recompense) and *maṣīr* (destination, journey's end) — are not synonyms. *Jazā'* names the moral character of what the righteous receive: it is recompense, something earned, something that corresponds to what was done. *Maṣīr* names the finality: it is the last place, the destination after which there is no further movement. Together they describe a reward that is both deserved and permanent — not a gift that could be revoked and not a waystation that will be left behind.

The pairing of these two words appears elsewhere in the Qur'an's descriptions of the Hereafter. In 3:136 — "For those, their reward is forgiveness from their Lord and gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they will abide forever. Excellent is the reward of the workers" — the same combination of earned reward and permanent residence appears. In 25:15, the combination serves the rhetorical question: the Garden is both better than what the disbelievers will receive and final in a way that their worldly possessions can never be. The disbelievers measured their lives by what they accumulated; the verse measures the righteous by where they end up. *Maṣīr* is the last word of the verse, and it means: this is the end of the road, and there is nothing beyond it — not punishment, not wandering, not another move.

**The Bridge to 25:16**

The verse ends with a question mark, and the answer comes in 25:16, which describes the contents of the Garden: "There they will have whatever they wish for, forever." The bridge between the two verses is the word *mawʿūd* ("promised"), which appears in 25:15. The promise itself is part of the argument: the righteous have been told what awaits them, and the telling is a moral act — it makes the choice between the fire and the garden a conscious one. The disbelievers were offered the same choice and chose differently. The sūrah's structure places the two options — fire (25:11-14) and garden (25:15-16) — in the same breath, and the reader must decide which word resonates louder: *thubūr* (destruction) or *khuld* (eternity).

**The Righteous as the Intended Audience**

The verse specifies that the Garden is promised to the righteous — *al-muttaqīn*, those who practice *taqwā* (God-consciousness, mindful restraint). The word *muttaqīn* appears throughout the Qur'an as the defining quality of those who receive God's favor: "The Book, in which there is no doubt, a guidance for the God-conscious" (2:2); "Be conscious of Allah, and know that you will meet Him" (2:233). The Garden is not promised to the wealthy, the powerful, or the socially prominent — the very categories the disbelievers used to measure worth. It is promised to those who were conscious of God, and in the context of this sūrah, that means those who accepted the Criterion (25:1) and did not deny the Hour (25:11). The promise is specific, and the specificity is the point: the Garden is not a general reward for all humanity but a destination for those who walked the path the sūrah describes, beginning with the acceptance of the revelation the disbelievers rejected.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:16

> There they will have whatever they wish for, forever. That is a promise ˹to be sought after˺, binding on your Lord.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Content of the Garden: Unlimited and Permanent**

The verse describes what awaits the righteous inside the Garden of Eternity named in 25:15: *lahum fīhā mā yashā'ūna khālidīn* — "There they will have whatever they wish for, forever." The phrasing is comprehensive. It does not list specific blessings — rivers of milk, fruits of every kind, silk garments — though these appear elsewhere in the Qur'an (47:15; 56:20-23; 76:21). Instead, it grants the wish itself: whatever the inhabitant desires, that is what exists. This is a promise without specification, which means it is also without limit. The Qur'an makes this point explicit in 39:34: "They will have whatever they desire with their Lord. That is the reward of the doers of good." The same wording appears in 16:31; 42:22; and 50:35, each time reinforcing the principle: Paradise is not a place with a fixed menu but a place where the desire and the fulfillment are identical.

The word *khālidīn* ("forever," literally "abiding") ties this verse to the *khuld* of 25:15. The Garden is eternal not only in its existence but in its provision: the wish does not diminish, the fulfillment does not tire, and the eternity does not become monotonous. This last point matters because the human experience of satisfaction is always temporary — what delights at first ceases to delight with repetition. The Qur'an's promise of eternal fulfillment implies a change in the nature of the one who wishes, not merely an abundance of the things wished for. The inhabitants of the Garden are not the same as they were in the world; they have been purified, and their desires have been purified with them. The wish and the wished-for are both transformed.

**Mas'ūl: The Promise That Is Binding**

The word *mas'ūl* in the phrase *wa'dan mas'ūlā* is one of the most debated terms in the commentators' discussions of this verse. In its ordinary Arabic usage, *mas'ūl* can mean "asked for" — something that is demanded or requested — and it can mean "something one is obligated to do," that is, a duty or debt. The translation "to be sought after" captures the first meaning: the Garden is something human beings should ask for, pursue, seek. The translation "binding" captures the second: the promise is something God has obligated Himself to fulfill.

Al-Ṭabarī favors the reading that makes *mas'ūl* mean "asked for" by the servants — they ask for Paradise, and God grants what is asked. Others, including al-Rāzī, note the theological sensitivity of the word in its second meaning: if God's promise is "binding" in the sense of an obligation, it raises the question of whether God can be said to owe anything to His creatures. The resolution, as al-Qurṭubī explains, is that the binding is not a debt God owes but a promise God keeps: His nature is such that He does not break His word, and *mas'ūl* names that reliability, not a compulsion from outside. The promise is not a contract that can be renegotiated; it is an attribute of the One who made it, and His attributes do not change.

**The Theological Divide Over Deservedness**

The verse sits at the center of a theological debate between the Muʿtazilites and the Ash'arites. The Muʿtazilites argued that God's promises and threats must be fulfilled — if He promises Paradise to the righteous, He must deliver it, because failing to fulfill a promise would be a deficiency in His justice. The Ash'arites countered that God's Will is absolute and unconstrained; He can fulfill His promises or not, as He chooses, and His freedom is not limited by human expectations of fairness. Both sides cite verses like 25:16 to support their positions.

The verse itself navigates this tension with precision. It names the Garden as a *reward* (*jazā'* in 25:15) — earned, deserved — and as a *promise* (*wa'd*) that is *mas'ūl* — binding or sought after. The combination holds both sides of the debate: the reward is earned because it corresponds to the righteous actions of the inhabitants; the promise is reliable because it comes from a Lord who does not fail. The disbelievers, who denied the Hour entirely, stand outside both frameworks: they neither earn the reward nor seek the promise, and the verse makes the choice between the fire and the garden as stark as language can make it. The promise exists for those who pursue it; for those who deny it, the other promise — the fire prepared in 25:11 — is equally binding.

**The Simplicity of the Promise**

What the Garden contains, according to 25:16, is not a list but a principle: *mā yashā'ūna* — "whatever they wish for." The simplicity is deliberate. The Qur'an elsewhere describes Paradise in elaborate detail — rivers of water, milk, wine, and honey (47:15); fruits of every kind (56:20-23); silk garments and bracelets of gold (76:21); couches arranged in rows (56:15-16). In 25:16, none of these are mentioned. The verse reduces the entire content of Paradise to a single phrase: whatever the inhabitant desires. This reduction is not vagueness; it is the ultimate specification. A list of blessings can be exhausted; a wish cannot. The promise is not that Paradise contains a set number of pleasures but that every desire, including desires that have not yet been imagined, will be fulfilled. The *forever* that follows (*khālidīn*) ensures that the fulfillment does not diminish: what is wished for at the first moment is available at the last, and there is no last moment. The simplicity of the verse is its strength: it promises everything by naming the principle rather than the particulars.

**The Finality of the Garden**

The word *maṣīr* from 25:15 resonates into 25:16. The Garden is not a reward that leads to another stage; it is the last stop. The word *khālidīn* ("forever") appears in both verses — once to describe the Garden's nature, once to describe the inhabitants' condition — and in both cases it means the same thing: this does not end. The disbelievers asked for a garden in 25:8; they are offered one that makes their request look small. The treasure they wanted would have been spent; the garden they wanted would have died. The Garden of Eternity does neither, and the verse closes the section that began with the denial of the Hour (25:11) by stating, in the plainest possible terms, what the Hour will deliver to those who did not deny it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:17

> ˹Watch for˺ the Day He will gather them along with what they used to worship besides Allah, and ask ˹the objects of worship˺, “Was it you who misled these servants of Mine, or did they stray from the Way ˹on their own˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Courtroom of Judgment**

The verse introduces a scene of cosmic significance: a trial in which God Himself poses the question. The Day of Judgment is described not as a sentencing but as a hearing — a structured inquiry in which the accused, the objects of worship, and the witnesses are all present. God gathers the disbelievers together with the things they worshipped besides Him — idols, angels, jinn, or any created being elevated to divine status — and asks the objects of worship a direct question: "Was it you who misled these servants of Mine, or did they stray on their own?"

The question is not information-seeking; God already knows the answer. It is forensic — designed to establish, in the presence of all parties, the truth of what happened. The structure mirrors a legal proceeding: the judge (God), the defendants (the false objects of worship), and the people who followed them (the disbelievers). The effect of the question is to eliminate every excuse. If the objects of worship misled the disbelievers, the disbelievers cannot claim innocence, because they chose to follow without evidence. If the disbelievers strayed on their own, the objects of worship bear no blame, and the disbelievers have no one to accuse. In either case, the disbelievers lose their defense. The courtroom structure removes the possibility of passing blame, and the question is designed so that every answer leads to the same verdict.

**The Word ʿIbād: God Names Them His Servants**

A subtle but devastating detail in the verse is God's use of the word *'ibād* — "servants" — to describe the disbelievers. The same people who served other gods are here called God's servants, because in the Qur'anic framework, all created beings are God's servants regardless of what they worship. The disbelievers served idols, but they remained God's servants; their worship of others did not transfer them to another owner. The word *'ibādī* ("My servants") establishes God's claim over them and makes the question more pointed: "Was it you who misled *My* servants?" The false gods are being asked about their actions toward beings who belonged to God, not to them. The possessive pronoun reassigns the relationship, and the false gods must answer for having accepted a loyalty that was not theirs to receive.

This possessive claim appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in contexts of judgment. In 15:49 — "Inform My servants that I am the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful" — the same word *'ibādī* appears in a context of mercy. In 25:17, it appears in a context of judgment. The word carries both registers: the servants are God's whether they are being pardoned or condemned. The disbelievers' attempt to find another master — the idols, the false gods, the things they worshipped — did not succeed; they remained God's servants throughout, and the Day of Judgment makes that ownership inescapable.

**The Parallel in 5:116**

The scene in 25:17 closely parallels the interrogation of Jesus in 5:116: "And when Allah said, 'O Jesus, son of Mary! Did you say to people, "Take me and my mother as gods besides Allah?"' he said, 'Glory be to You! It is not for me to say what I have no right to say.'" The structure is identical: God asks the object of worship whether it encouraged the worship; the object denies it. The two passages together establish a universal principle: no being that was worshipped besides God will stand on the Day of Judgment as a co-defendant with its worshippers. Every false god will testify against those who worshipped it.

The identity of the objects of worship in 25:17 is specified by the commentators. Al-Qurṭubī includes angels, jinn, idols, and Jesus among the possibilities, noting that the verse is general enough to encompass every form of *shirk* (associating others with God) that has occurred in human history. The verse does not name the false gods because it does not need to; the question applies to all of them without exception. The generality is the point: the question covers every object of false worship that has ever existed, and the answer will be the same for each one. The scene in 25:17-18 will record those answers, and they will be unanimous: "Glory be to You! It was not right for us to take any lords besides You."

**The Architecture of Accountability**

The verse's placement in the sūrah completes the arc that began with the disbelievers' objections in 25:4-8. The disbelievers accused the Prophet of fabrication, demanded material signs, and labeled him bewitched. In 25:11-14, the sūrah described the punishment those objections deserve. In 25:15-16, it described the reward the disbelievers refused. In 25:17, it describes the scene where all parties — worshippers and objects of worship — are brought face to face. The sūrah's argument is complete: the disbelievers denied the Hour, and the Hour will contain exactly the confrontation they tried to avoid. They will stand beside the gods they chose, and the gods will say: we never asked for this.

**The Excluded Defense**

The structure of God's question in 25:17 leaves no room for a third option. The question offers two possibilities: either the objects of worship misled the disbelievers, or the disbelievers strayed on their own. There is no third answer — no "we were confused," no "the evidence was unclear," no "we did our best with what we had." The binary framing eliminates every form of excuse that falls between deception and willful error. The disbelievers cannot claim that the objects of worship were partly responsible and they were partly responsible, because the question does not allow shared blame. Each party is held to account for its own role: the objects of worship for what they did or did not do, and the disbelievers for what they chose. The courtroom of the Day of Judgment, as 25:17 presents it, does not accept ambiguous verdicts. The question forces a clear answer, and the clear answer forces a clear consequence.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:18

> They will say, “Glory be to You! It was not right for ˹others like˺ us to take any lords besides You, but You allowed enjoyment for them and their forefathers ˹for so long˺ that they forgot ˹Your˺ remembrance and became a doomed people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The False Gods' Confession**

The objects of worship answer God's question from 25:17 with an immediate declaration of His transcendent purity: *subḥānaka* — "Glory be to You!" This is the same word Jesus uses in 5:116 when asked whether he told people to worship him. The response is not merely a denial of responsibility; it is a theological statement. The false gods declare that it was never appropriate (*mā yanbaghī*) for them to accept worship — that the very category of "other lord" beside God is a contradiction in terms. The denial is absolute. They did not mislead the disbelievers because they never claimed lordship in the first place. The idols did not carve themselves into temples; the stones did not demand sacrifice; the trees did not request prostration. Every act of false worship was initiated by the worshipper, not by the worshipped.

The force of this confession is enormous in the Qur'anic courtroom. The disbelievers cannot claim they were deceived by powerful beings who actively solicited their worship. The false gods testify that they never asked for what was given to them. The responsibility, therefore, falls entirely on the disbelievers, who chose to worship what never requested worship and never had the power to accept it. The confession turns the disbelievers' defense into a prosecution: if the gods did not mislead them, then the misguidance was self-generated, and self-generated misguidance is the hardest kind to excuse.

**The Forefathers and the Mechanism of Forgetfulness**

The false gods continue their testimony with a diagnosis of how the misguidance occurred. It was not their doing; it was a process set in motion by *tamattuʿ* — "enjoyment" or "provision" — that God granted to the disbelievers and their forefathers over a long period. The word *mattaʿnā* ("We granted enjoyment") is significant because it places the provision of worldly goods within God's own action, not the false gods'. God gave them life, sustenance, time, and comfort. The result was not gratitude but amnesia: *fa-nsū'dh-dhikr* — "they forgot the remembrance." The remembrance (*dhikr*) they forgot is God Himself — His rights, His commands, His presence.

This mechanism — that prolonged comfort produces spiritual amnesia — appears throughout the Qur'an. In 20:124-126, God says of the one who turns away from His remembrance: "We will give him a life of hardship." In 17:67: "When adversity touches you at sea, lost are those you invoke except Him. But when He delivers you to land, you turn away." The false gods in 25:18 are citing the same pattern: God provided, humans forgot, and the forgetting became the basis for the misguidance they are now being asked about. The *forefathers* are mentioned because the pattern was inherited: the first generation forgot, and the second generation inherited the forgetting as custom. What began as spiritual negligence became cultural practice, and cultural practice became identity. By the time the disbelievers worshipped idols in Makkah, they were not making a theological choice; they were continuing what their fathers had done, and their fathers had forgotten why.

**"A Doomed People" and the Problem of Overextension**

The final phrase — *wa kānū qawman buwrā* — translates as "and they became a doomed people." The word *buwrā* means ruined, lost, beyond recovery. It describes not a single act of sin but a condition — a people who have gone so far into misguidance that correction from within is no longer possible. The false gods' testimony is that this condition was self-inflicted. They did not cause it; they did not encourage it; they stood by while the disbelievers brought it upon themselves through the slow process of forgetting God in the midst of His blessings.

The structure of the testimony serves the sūrah's larger argument. The disbelievers accused the Prophet ﷺ of fabrication (25:4), bewitchment (25:8), and ordinary humanity (25:7). In this verse, the objects of worship they chose instead of God testify that they themselves were never a legitimate alternative. The disbelievers' rejection of the Prophet and their worship of false gods are shown to share the same root: the refusal to acknowledge the One to whom all worship is owed. The false gods' confession is the mirror image of the disbelievers' denial: the objects of worship say *subḥānaka* (Glory be to You), and in doing so they model the response the disbelievers should have given but refused. The Day of Judgment reveals that even the stones and the trees know who God is; the only beings who forgot were the human beings He created.

**The Inheritance of Error and the Break in the Chain**

The mention of the forefathers in 25:18 is not incidental; it introduces the theme of inherited error. The false gods testify that the disbelievers' fathers were granted enjoyment, and the enjoyment led to forgetfulness, and the forgetfulness became custom, and the custom became identity. The disbelievers inherited not a conscious choice to worship false gods but a state of spiritual amnesia so deep that it felt like nature. This is the mechanism by which misguidance perpetuates itself: it does not require active malice; it requires only the absence of reflection. The children grow up in a household where idols are present, where rituals are performed, where the names of God are not spoken — and they absorb the absence as normality.

The Qur'an breaks this chain by insisting that each individual is responsible for their own belief. "No bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another" (6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38). The forefathers' error is the context, not the excuse. The false gods' testimony in 25:18 names the forefathers not to transfer blame but to explain the origin: the error began with a generation that forgot, and it continued because the next generation did not ask why. The Day of Judgment separates the inheritors from their inheritance and holds each person accountable for what they did with the life they were given — not what their fathers did with theirs.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:19

> ˹The doomed will be told˺, “Your gods have clearly denied your claims. So now you can neither ward off ˹the punishment˺ nor get any help.” And whoever of you does wrong, We will make them taste a horrible punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict After the Testimony**

The verse delivers God's ruling after the false gods have testified in 25:18. The disbelievers are addressed directly: *faqad kadhdhabūkum* — "Your gods have clearly denied your claims." The word *kadhdhabūkum* is the same root used throughout the sūrah for the disbelievers' rejection of the Prophet: they called him a liar (*kadhdhabū*) in 25:4, 25:5, and 25:11. Now the same charge is turned against them — not by a human messenger but by the very beings they worshipped. The false gods have called the disbelievers liars, and the disbelievers have no rebuttal. The symmetry is devastating: the people who denied the truth are now themselves denied by the objects of their misplaced devotion. The word *kadhdhabū* does not change; only the direction changes. What the disbelievers gave to the Prophet, the false gods now give to them.

The statement that follows — *fa-mā taṣṭī'ūna ḍarran wa-lā naṣrā* — "So now you can neither ward off the punishment nor get any help" — removes both strategies simultaneously. *Ḍarr* means to avert, to push away; *naṣr* means assistance, help from an ally. In life, the disbelievers had two resources: their own ability to resist consequences and the support of their tribal networks and false protectors. In the courtroom of the Day of Judgment, both are gone. They cannot push the punishment away by their own effort, and they cannot call on anyone to help them do so. The false gods, who were supposed to be their protectors, have just testified against them. The tribal networks that pressured the believers in Makkah are dissolved. Every social, political, and spiritual resource the disbelievers relied on has evaporated, and what remains is the individual standing alone before the One whose sovereignty was declared in 25:2.

**The Individual Within the Collective**

The final clause — *wa-man yaẓlim minkum nūdhiquhu ʿadhāban kabīrān* — introduces an individual register into a passage that has been addressing the disbelievers as a group. "Whoever among you does wrong" (*man yaẓlim minkum*) singles out each person. The punishment is not collective by default; it is assigned to individuals who chose wrong. This is the Qur'an's consistent position: the Day of Judgment separates people from their groups, their families, their tribes, and their communities. In 6:94 — "You have come to Us alone, as We first created you, leaving behind everything We gave you" — the individuality of the encounter is stated as a fact. In 19:95 — "Every one of them will come to Him alone on the Day of Resurrection" — the same principle appears.

The word *ẓulm* (wrongdoing, injustice) echoes the word used in 25:4 to describe the disbelievers' accusation against the Prophet: *faqad jā'ū ẓulman wa zuwrā* — "They have indeed produced a wrongdoing and a calumny." The same word describes the original act of rejection and the final act of punishment. In life, the disbelievers wronged the truth by calling it falsehood; in the Hereafter, they taste the consequence of that wrong. The word *kabīr* ("horrible," "great") intensifies the punishment — not merely pain but a magnitude of pain that corresponds to the magnitude of the denial. The disbelievers denied the greatest truth — the sovereignty of God and the reality of the Hour — and the punishment matches the scale of the denial.

**The End of the Eschatological Passage**

Verse 25:19 closes the extended eschatological section that began with the denial of the Hour in 25:11. Across nine verses, the sūrah has moved from diagnosis (the denial of the Hour is the root of all other errors) to description (the fire that sees, the narrow place, the cry for destruction, the denial of annihilation) to contrast (the Garden of Eternity promised to the righteous) to judgment (the false gods testify, the disbelievers are condemned). The passage answers the objections raised in 25:4-8 not by refuting them directly but by showing where they lead: a denial of accountability that produces, in the end, the very accountability that was denied. The disbelievers' mockery of the Prophet, their demands for material proof, and their accusation of bewitchment are all revealed as symptoms of the same disease — the refusal to believe that their choices have permanent consequences. Verse 25:20 will return to the earthly plane, answering the specific objection about the Prophet's humanity, but the answer will land in a world already illuminated by the fire and the garden that precede it.

**The Parallel Between Denial and Its Recompense**

The language of 25:19 completes a verbal arc that runs through the sūrah. The word *kadhdhabūkum* ("they have denied you") uses the same root — *k-dh-b* — that the Qur'an uses for the disbelievers' treatment of the Prophet in 25:4 (*kadhdhabū*), 25:5, and 25:11. The root appears more times in this sūrah than in almost any other, and its repetition is the sūrah's signature. The disbelievers denied the truth; the false gods now deny the disbelievers; and the word *kadhdhabū* connects both denials, making the second a mirror of the first. The sūrah teaches that denial is a reversible force: what is given returns, and what returns is identical in nature to what was given. The disbelievers called the Prophet a liar; their gods call them liars. The disbelievers denied the Hour; the Hour now denies them relief. The symmetry is not punishment but consequence — the natural outcome of a relationship with truth that was always adversarial.

**The Penalty for Wrongdoing: Individual and Certain**

The verse ends with an individualized warning: "Whoever among you does wrong, We will make them taste a horrible punishment." The shift from the collective "you" to the individual "whoever" is a standard Qur'anic move at the conclusion of a judgment passage. It ensures that no one in the audience can hide behind the group. The disbelievers addressed in 25:19 are not a mass; they are persons, each of whom made a choice, and each of whom will face the consequence of that choice alone. The word *nūdhiquhu* ("We will make him taste") is visceral: the punishment is not merely witnessed or announced but experienced, tasted on the tongue, felt in the body. The Qur'an uses the verb "to taste" for punishment in numerous passages (3:106; 10:70; 32:21; 78:24), and in each case it insists that the consequence is not abstract but physical, not future but present, not theoretical but certain.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:20

> We never sent any messenger before you ˹O Prophet˺, who did not eat food and go about in market-places. We have made some of you a trial for others. Will you ˹not then˺ be patient? And your Lord is All-Seeing

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universal Answer to the Specific Objection**

The verse returns to the objection raised in 25:7 — "What kind of messenger is this who eats food and walks in the markets?" — and answers it by universalizing the charge. Every messenger God sent before Muhammad ﷺ ate food and walked among their people. The phrasing is categorical: *mā arsalnā min qablika min rasūlin illā innahum la-ya'kulūna't-ṭa'ama wa yamtashūna fi'l-aswāq* — "We never sent any messenger before you who did not eat food and go about in market-places." The construction *mā...illā* (none...except) is a universal negative: no exception existed. The disbelievers' demand for a non-human messenger would disqualify every prophet in the chain of revelation, including those the Quraysh acknowledged as genuine — Abraham, Ishmael, Moses, Jesus.

The mention of market-places (*aswāq*) is not incidental. The markets were the center of Meccan public life, where social status was performed and observed. The Prophet's presence in the market was, for the disbelievers, a sign of his ordinariness. The verse reframes it as a sign of prophetic continuity: the market-place is where prophets have always been, because the people they warn are in the market-place. Prophets do not deliver messages from mountaintops; they deliver them where the audience lives. The objection in 25:7 was that the Prophet's humanity disqualified him; the answer in 25:20 is that his humanity qualified every prophet before him. The objection was not a discovery but a repetition — the same complaint, in different words, that every prophetic community had made.

**The Trial That Runs in Both Directions**

The phrase *wa jaʿalnā baʿḍakum li-baʿḍin fitnatan* — "We have made some of you a trial for others" — introduces a principle that transforms the meaning of the disbelievers' mockery. Their contempt for the Prophet and his followers is not merely social friction; it is a divinely designed test. The word *fitnah* means trial, tribulation, and also the smelting of gold — the process by which impurities are burned away to reveal what is genuine. The disbelievers' persecution of the believers is the fire that tests the metal of their faith.

The trial cuts both ways. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note that the believers are also a trial for the disbelievers: the existence of people who believe without material proof, who accept a messenger who walks in markets, who endure mockery without retaliation — this challenges the disbelievers' worldview and deepens their refusal. The poor and lowly Companions of the Prophet ﷺ were, for the Quraysh, evidence that the message was unworthy. The Quraysh's reasoning is recorded in 11:27 and 46:11, where the disbelievers say that if the message were good, the most powerful and wealthy would have been the first to accept it. The reversal is the point: the test reveals who judges by wealth and who judges by truth. The fitnah is not incidental to the prophetic mission; it is part of its architecture. Without the trial, faith would be unanimous and therefore meaningless. The trial makes choice real.

**Patience as the Appropriate Response**

*Hal anta ṣābirūn* — "Will you be patient?" — is both a question and an implicit command. The Arabic allows both readings: it asks whether the Prophet and the believers are capable of patience, and it instructs them to be patient. The word *ṣabr* in the Qur'an is not passive endurance but active steadfastness — holding one's position against pressure without moving, without compromising, without retaliating in kind. It is the quality the Qur'an most consistently demands of the Prophet ﷺ in the face of opposition: "So be patient, as the messengers of strong resolve were patient" (46:35); "Be patient, for the promise of Allah is true" (40:55).

The placement of the patience command after the explanation of the trial gives it force. The Prophet now understands that the mockery is not random but designed — it is the means by which faith is purified. Understanding the purpose of suffering does not remove the suffering, but it does provide a framework for enduring it. The question "Will you be patient?" asks whether the framework is enough — whether knowing that the trial is intentional and temporary is sufficient to endure the pain of it. The answer is left to the Prophet, and the answer he gave — decades of steadfastness, without retaliation, without compromise — is the historical record's reply.

**Wa Rabbuka Baṣīr: Your Lord Is All-Seeing**

The verse closes with *wa Rabbuka Baṣīr* — "And your Lord is All-Seeing." The word *Baṣīr* names one of God's attributes: the One who sees everything, including the suffering of His prophets and the actions of their oppressors. The statement is a consolation and a warning simultaneously. For the Prophet, it means: God sees what you endure, and your patience is not unnoticed. For the disbelievers, it means: God sees what you do, and your mockery will not be forgotten. The single word *Baṣīr* holds both meanings at once, and the verse allows the reader to feel whichever applies. The attribute appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in similar dual contexts: in 4:58 — "Allah commands you to render trusts to their owners, and when you judge between people, to judge with justice" — the instruction to judge justly is followed by "Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing," reminding the judge that his judgment is observed. In 25:20, the observation is aimed at both sides: the patient and the persecutor, the mocked and the mocker. Neither is unseen, and neither is unrecorded.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:21

> Those who do not expect to meet Us say, “If only the angels were sent down to us, or we could see our Lord!” They have certainly been carried away by their arrogance and have entirely exceeded all limits

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand Repeated and the Diagnosis Applied**

The verse returns to the same demand voiced in 25:7 — "If only an angel had been sent down with him" — and restates it with a broader scope: the disbelievers now want angels sent down to them directly, or a visible manifestation of God Himself. The demand has escalated. In 25:7, they asked for an angel to accompany the Prophet as a co-warner; in 25:21, they ask for angels to appear to them personally, or for God to show Himself. The escalation reveals that no amount of evidence would satisfy them, because the demand is not genuine. It is a rhetorical move designed to make faith appear impossible: set the evidentiary bar so high that nothing can clear it, then blame God for not clearing it.

The phrase *alladhīna lā yarjūna liqā'anā* — "those who do not expect to meet Us" — names the root problem, the same diagnosis offered in 25:11: the denial of the Hour. The word *yarjūna* (expect, hope, anticipate) carries a double edge. In its positive sense, it means to hope for something good; in its negative sense, it means to anticipate something feared. The disbelievers do not hope to meet God because they do not believe the meeting will occur. Their demand for angels and a visible Lord is made from within a worldview that assumes there is no reckoning, and therefore the demand can be as extravagant as they wish. If there is no meeting, demanding proof of the meeting costs nothing.

**The Parallel With Moses and the Theophany**

The demand to see God directly echoes the most dramatic theophany in the Qur'anic narrative: the request of the Israelites to Moses. In 2:55, they said, "O Moses, we will never believe you until we see God outright." The result was the thunder and lightning that struck them dead, followed by their resurrection. The parallel with 25:21 is structural: in both cases, the demand for visible proof comes from people who have already received sufficient guidance and are using the demand as a refusal dressed as inquiry. Moses had performed signs; the Prophet ﷺ had delivered the Quran. The demand for more was not curiosity but resistance.

Al-Rāzī draws a careful distinction between two types of seeing. The Israelites asked to see God with their physical eyes, which is impossible in this life because God is not contained by sensory perception. The disbelievers of Makkah ask for something that is possible in principle — angels can be sent down — but that God has chosen not to do, for reasons the Qur'an has already explained (6:8: if an angel appeared, the matter would be decided, and testing would end). The demand is therefore not impossible to satisfy but unnecessary to satisfy, because the evidence already given — the Quran — is sufficient for the purpose of guidance.

**Istikbār: Arrogance as a Cognitive Failure**

The verse diagnoses the disbelievers' condition as *istikbār* — arrogance, haughtiness, the refusal to accept a lower position. The word is not a moral judgment alone; it describes a cognitive failure. The disbelievers' arrogance prevents them from processing the evidence correctly. They have seen the Quran's effect on its listeners; they have heard its language; they know the Prophet's character. But their pride — their conviction that they are the measure of what is worthy — blocks the inference that the evidence supports. *Istikbār* is not the absence of intelligence; it is intelligence held hostage by self-regard.

The phrase *wa kānū ḥubran kabīrān* — "and they were insolent beyond all bounds" or "they exceeded all limits" — intensifies the diagnosis. The word *ḥubr* can mean insolence, excess, or transgression. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the disbelievers did not merely reject the truth; they exceeded the limits of rejection by actively working against it — persecuting the believers, mocking the Prophet, and demanding signs they knew would not be sent. The excess is the point: ordinary doubt asks questions; insolent doubt sets conditions it knows cannot be met and then treats the failure to meet them as justification for denial. The verse names this behavior as *ḥubr* — a transgression that goes beyond mere disbelief into active opposition.

**The Sūrah's Structural Placement of the Verse**

Verse 25:21 closes the second cycle of disbelievers' objections in the sūrah. The first cycle ran from 25:4 to 25:8 (charges of fabrication, borrowing, bewitchment, demands for material signs); the second cycle opens in 25:21 with a demand for angels or a visible God. The sūrah presents the same pattern twice because the disbelievers' method is repetitive: when one objection is answered, they produce another, using the same underlying logic — the refusal to accept the human messenger and the message he carries. The structural repetition is itself an argument: the disbelievers are not engaging with the evidence; they are cycling through different versions of the same refusal. The sūrah records each version and answers it, but the answers do not produce belief because the problem was never the quality of the answers. The problem, as 25:21 names it, is *istikbār* — arrogance — and arrogance is not defeated by evidence. It is defeated only by the willingness to submit, which is precisely what the disbelievers refuse to do. The verse's placement at the head of the second cycle marks the return of the same disease in a new form, and the sūrah's response — the descriptions of the Day, the angels, the gardens, and the fire — is the same medicine prescribed again.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:22

> ˹But˺ on the Day they will see the angels, there will be no good news for the wicked, who will cry, “Keep away! Away ˹from us˺!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Irony of Fulfilled Demand**

The disbelievers demanded to see the angels in 25:21, and 25:22 delivers the demand — on the Day they will see the angels. The verse opens with *yawma yarawna'l-malā'ikah* — "the Day they will see the angels" — using the same verb (*ra'a*, to see) that the disbelievers used in their demand. The seeing happens. But the context has changed entirely. In life, seeing the angels would have been proof that might have led to belief; on the Day of Judgment, seeing the angels is a confirmation of punishment. The demand is fulfilled after the window for belief has closed. This is the Qur'an's consistent treatment of demands for delayed evidence: the evidence arrives when it is too late to act on it.

The phrase *lā bushrā yawma'idhin lil-mujrimīn* — "there will be no good news for the wicked on that Day" — negates the possibility of comfort. The word *bushrā* (good news, glad tidings) appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in contexts of reward: the angels give good news to the righteous at death (16:32; 41:30). For the wicked, the same angels that deliver good news to others deliver dread. The disbelievers' demand in 25:21 was for the angels' appearance as a sign; what they receive is the angels' appearance as a verdict.

**"Keep Away! A Forbidden Barrier!"**

The disbelievers' cry — *ḥijran maḥjūrā* — is one of the most compressed expressions in the Qur'an. The word *ḥijr* means barrier, partition, forbidden zone. The doubled form (*ḥijran maḥjūrā*) intensifies it: an absolute barrier, a boundary that cannot be crossed. The commentators offer several interpretations of who speaks these words and to whom. Al-Rāzī records the opinion that the disbelievers cry out to the angels, begging them to stay away — the very beings they demanded to see are now the beings they cannot bear to look at. Others hold that the angels speak the words to the disbelievers, barring them from the gates of Paradise. Al-Qurṭubī records both readings and notes that the ambiguity may be deliberate: the same words function as both a plea and a sentence.

The idiomatic use of *ḥijran maḥjūrā* in Arabic is a cry one makes when encountering an enemy or attacker, equivalent to "get back!" or "stay away!" The disbelievers, who once demanded proximity to the angels, now beg for distance. The reversal is complete. The demand was made from a position of safety and mockery; the cry is made from a position of terror and helplessness. The angels have not changed; the context has. In life, the disbelievers controlled the terms of the encounter — they set the conditions, they named the proofs they required. On the Day, the angels control the terms, and the only condition the disbelievers can set is the request for distance.

**The Day as the Fulfillment of the Unwanted**

The verse contributes to the sūrah's broader pattern of fulfilled demands that produce the opposite of what was intended. The disbelievers demanded gardens and treasure in 25:8; the gardens are promised to the righteous, not to them. They demanded an angel co-warner in 25:7; the angels appear as a threat. They demanded to see God in 25:21; the meeting they avoided will occur on terms they did not choose. The sūrah's architecture turns every demand inside out: what was asked for is given, but in a form that proves the asker wrong. The demand for angels was supposed to produce belief; the appearance of angels produces terror. The demand for material proof was supposed to establish the Prophet's inadequacy; the Hereafter reveals that the Prophet was the adequate one all along.

**The Contrast Between Life's Demand and Death's Reality**

The sūrah's placement of 25:22 directly after 25:21 creates a demand-fulfillment pattern that is one of the chapter's most powerful structural devices. In life, the disbelievers demanded to see the angels; in death, they see them and beg them to go away. The demand was a rhetorical weapon — a way of making faith conditional on evidence they knew would not be provided. The fulfillment is a consequence — the evidence arrives in a context that makes faith irrelevant. The angels do not come as signs to encourage belief; they come as agents of judgment to execute the verdict that has already been rendered. The shift from demand to fulfillment, from mockery to terror, is the sūrah's way of showing that the disbelievers' strategy of setting conditions was always futile. They set conditions they thought could not be met; the conditions are met, but the meeting produces not faith but fear. The lesson is not that the evidence was insufficient but that the demand was dishonest. The disbelievers did not want to see the angels in order to believe; they wanted to set a condition they assumed would never be fulfilled, so that they could blame God for not providing it. The Day of Judgment removes the pretense: the angels appear, the demand is answered, and the answer is worse than the absence the disbelievers relied on.


**The Sūrah’s Pattern of Fulfilled Demands**

Verse 25:22 closes the second cycle of objections in the sūrah. The first cycle ran from 25:4 to 25:8; the second opens in 25:21 with a demand for angels or a visible Lord. The same pattern repeats because the disbelievers’ method is circular: when one objection is answered, another appears, built on the same underlying logic of refusal. The structural repetition is itself an argument: the disbelievers are not engaging with the evidence; they are cycling through variations of the same denial. Each variation is recorded and answered, but the answers do not produce belief because the problem was never the quality of the answers. The problem, as 25:21 names it, is istikbār — arrogance — and arrogance is not defeated by evidence. It is defeated only by the willingness to submit, which is precisely what the disbelievers refuse. The sūrah records the cycle and names the disease, trusting the reader to see that the repetition of objections is itself the proof of bad faith.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:23

> Then We will turn to whatever ˹good˺ deeds they did, reducing them to scattered dust

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nullification of Every Work**

The verse describes God's response to the deeds of the disbelievers: *wa qallibnā lahum a'mālahum ḥabā'an manthūrā* — "We will turn to whatever deeds they did, reducing them to scattered dust." The verb *qallibnā* (We will turn, We will examine) carries both the sense of scrutiny — turning a thing over to inspect it — and transformation — turning a thing into something else. The deeds that the disbelievers performed in life, including what they considered good — acts of charity, tribal loyalty, hospitality, honoring agreements — are examined and then disintegrated. The word *ḥabā'* means the fine particles of dust that float in the air, visible only when a shaft of light passes through them. The metaphor is precise: the deeds do not merely become worthless; they become invisible, weightless, incapable of being gathered or used.

The Qur'an's treatment of deeds reduced to dust appears across multiple passages, each with its own context. In 14:18 — "The example of those who disbelieve in their Lord: their deeds are like ashes which the wind blows forcefully on a stormy day" — the image is of ashes scattered by wind. In 18:104 — "Those whose efforts are in the life of this world, while they think they are doing good — they are those who disbelieve in the verses of their Lord and in meeting Him, so their deeds are worthless" — the disqualification is tied directly to disbelief. In 25:23, the same principle applies: the deeds are nullified not because they were intrinsically evil but because the doers denied the meeting with God. Disbelief removes the foundation on which all other deeds stand.

**The Relationship Between Deeds and Faith**

The verse raises a theological question that the commentators debate: does disbelief destroy good deeds entirely, or does it render them ineffective in the Hereafter while allowing their worldly benefit? The Muʿtazilite position holds that God is just and does not waste good deeds, even those of disbelievers, but that disbelief is so weighty a sin that it outweighs everything else. The Ash'arite position, closer to the plain reading of verses like 25:23, holds that deeds performed without faith are simply not accepted — not because of a deficiency in the deeds themselves but because faith is the precondition for acceptance. Al-Rāzī frames the issue carefully: a deed's value is determined not only by its outward form but by the intention and belief behind it. A charitable act performed by a disbeliever may benefit the recipient in this world, but it carries no weight in the Hereafter because the doer rejected the framework that gives deeds their ultimate meaning.

The word *manthūrā* (scattered, dispersed) intensifies the image. *Ḥabā'* alone means dust; *ḥabā'an manthūrā* means dust that has been spread out, scattered in every direction, beyond reassembly. The deeds are not merely reduced to dust — they are reduced to dispersed dust, dust that cannot be collected, weighed, or counted. The scattering is the finality. Even if the disbelievers could gather their deeds after the nullification, the deeds would slip through their fingers like the particles they have become.

**What "Turning To" Deeds Means**

The phrase *qallibnā lahum a'mālahum* — "We will turn to their deeds" — uses the pronoun *lahum* (for them), making the action personal. God does not nullify deeds in the abstract; He turns to the specific deeds of specific people. The personal address removes the possibility of anonymity: each person's works are examined individually, and each person's works are scattered individually. The Day of Judgment, as the Qur'an consistently describes it, is not a mass event but an individual encounter. The scattering of deeds is not collective punishment but individual reckoning: what you built is inspected, and what was built without faith is dissolved. The image of dust that was once a structure — once an act of charity, once a tribal obligation, once a moment of genuine human goodness — is the Qur'an's most poignant image of the cost of disbelief. The goodness was real; the dust is also real; and the one cancels the other.

**The Nullification of Every Work — The Particle Metaphor**

The image of deeds reduced to dust carries a specificity that deserves separate attention. The word *ḥabā'* refers not to ordinary dust on the ground but to the fine particles suspended in the air — the motes that become visible only when a shaft of sunlight passes through them. The choice of image is precise: the disbelievers' deeds are not turned into something heavy and solid that might be gathered; they are turned into something so insubstantial that it floats, that cannot be held, that disappears the moment the light moves. The particle metaphor conveys weightlessness and irretrievability simultaneously. A deed reduced to *ḥabā* has not merely lost its value; it has lost its substance. It exists in a form that makes it impossible to collect, impossible to weigh, impossible to present as evidence of anything at all. This is the ultimate fate of the works that the disbelievers performed without faith — not punishment, not reduction to a lesser reward, but total dematerialization. The charity they gave, the oaths they kept, the guests they honored — all of it becomes air. The theological implication is stark: the same act can be gold or dust depending on the foundation beneath it. A charitable act performed by a believer and the same act performed by a disbeliever produce identical benefits for the recipient but radically different consequences for the doer. The foundation — faith in the meeting with God — determines whether the structure stands or dissolves.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:24

> ˹But˺ on that Day the residents of Paradise will have the best settlement and the finest place to rest

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Best Dwelling and the Finest Rest**

The verse contrasts the fate of the wicked — whose deeds are scattered into dust (25:23) — with the fate of the righteous: *aṣḥābu'l-jannati yawma'idhin khayru mustaqarrin wa khayru maqīlā* — "the residents of Paradise on that Day will have the best settlement and the finest place to rest." The word *mustaqarr* means a place of settlement, a dwelling where one is established and at rest. The word *maqīl* means a place of midday rest — the *qaylūlah*, the afternoon nap that was a fixture of Arabian life. The pairing of the two words — permanent settlement and temporary rest — covers the full range of human repose. The righteous do not merely live in Paradise; they rest in it. Every form of comfort, from the deep security of belonging to the light relief of a moment's pause, is included.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that some commentators read this verse as the direct answer to the question posed in 25:15: "Is this better, or the Garden of Eternity?" The word *khayr* (best, better) appears in both verses, creating a verbal link: what was asked about in 25:15 is confirmed in 25:24. The Garden is indeed better — not merely better than the fire but better than anything the disbelievers possessed in life, better than the gardens and treasures they demanded for the Prophet in 25:8, better than the earthly standards by which they measured authority and worth.

**Maqīl: Rest as a Divine Gift**

The word *maqīl* deserves separate attention because of what it reveals about the Qur'anic vision of Paradise. In the Arabian context, the midday rest was a necessity — the heat of the day made activity impossible, and the *qaylūlah* was a universal practice. The mention of rest in Paradise suggests that the comfort of the Hereafter is not only spectacular — rivers of wine, palaces of gold — but also intimate, covering the small, everyday pleasures that make life bearable. The righteous will have not only the extraordinary but the ordinary, perfected.

Some commentators, including al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī, raise a more provocative question: does *maqīl* suggest that the inhabitants of Paradise sleep? The question has implications for the nature of the Hereafter, which the Qur'an describes as a place where "they will not hear therein any ill speech — only greetings of peace" (19:62). If Paradise contains rest, does it also contain the vulnerability and unconsciousness of sleep? The majority position is that the rest of Paradise is a state of complete peace and satisfaction, not the physical need for sleep that characterizes earthly life. The word *maqīl* borrows from the language of this world to describe a comfort that transcends it.

**Scattered Dust and Settled Rest: The Contrast Extended**

The structural placement of 25:24 immediately after 25:23 is the sūrah's most direct juxtaposition of the two outcomes. Verse 23 reduces the disbelievers' deeds to dust; verse 24 gives the righteous the best dwelling. The contrast is not elaborated — the sūrah does not comment on it, does not draw the reader's attention to it, does not explain the irony. It simply places the two verses side by side and lets the reader feel the weight. The disbelievers' deeds are scattered; the righteous are settled. The disbelievers' works are invisible particles; the righteous have the finest rest. The sūrah trusts its audience to see what the juxtaposition means without being told.

This structural device — placing the two outcomes adjacent without commentary — appears throughout the Qur'an's eschatological passages. In 56:27-40, the description of the blessed is followed immediately by the description of the damned, with no transition or explanation. In 75:22-30, the faces of the radiant are contrasted with the faces of the darkened. The technique is more powerful than any commentary because it makes the comparison automatic: the reader does not need to be told that one is better than the other; the reading itself makes the judgment.

**The Delayed Answer to 25:15's Question**

Several commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, read verse 25:24 as the direct answer to the question posed in 25:15: "Is this better, or the Garden of Eternity which the righteous have been promised?" The word *khayr* (best, better) appears in both verses, creating a verbal link across the intervening passage. Between the question in 25:15 and the answer in 25:24, the sūrah has inserted the full description of the fire (25:16-23), the angels' descent (25:25), and the sovereignty of the Most Compassionate (25:26). The answer to the question is therefore not given immediately but after the audience has absorbed the full weight of the punishment and the full scope of the Day. By the time the answer arrives — "the best dwelling and the finest rest" — the reader has earned it through the sustained experience of dread that precedes it. The delayed answer is the sūrah's way of making the promise of Paradise not merely a statement but a relief, a release from the tension that the fire descriptions built. The *maqīl* (rest) that the righteous receive is, in this reading, the rest that comes after the reader has been through the fire descriptions — the rest that the sūrah itself provides, not only to the righteous in the Hereafter but to the reader in the present, who has been absorbing the weight of the warnings for twenty verses and now receives the assurance that the outcome for the righteous is not the fire but the finest dwelling and the deepest rest. This literary architecture — tension followed by release, warning followed by promise — is one of the sūrah's most effective rhetorical strategies.

## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:25

> ˹Watch for˺ the Day the heavens will burst with clouds, and the angels will be sent down in successive ranks

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sky Breaks Open**

The verse describes the opening scene of the Day of Judgment: *yawma tashaqqaqu's-samā'u bi-ghamāmīn* — "the Day the heavens will burst with clouds." The verb *tashaqqaqu* (to split, to burst open) appears in other Qur'anic descriptions of cosmic dissolution: in 84:1 — "When the sky is split open" — and in 82:1 — "When the sky is torn apart." The splitting of the sky is not a gradual event but a rupture, a breaking of the structure that has held the heavens in place since creation. The word *ghamām* (clouds) adds a layer of imagery: the sky does not merely open; it opens with clouds — masses of dense, heavy material that pour through the rupture. Al-Qurṭubī and others read this as a description of the physical transformation of the cosmos: the familiar sky gives way to something unprecedented, and what was above comes down.

The splitting of the heavens appears in multiple Qur'anic passages, each adding a different detail. In 42:5 — "The heavens nearly split from above them as the angels glorify their Lord" — the splitting is caused by the weight of divine glory pressing down. In 69:16 — "The sky will split open, for on that Day it will be frail" — the sky loses its structural integrity. In 78:19 — "The sky will be opened up and become gateways" — the rupture creates passages through which things descend. The verse in 25:25 combines the splitting with clouds and then adds the descent of the angels, making the rupture the opening act of a larger drama: the cosmos breaks, and through the break come the agents of judgment.

**The Angels in Successive Ranks**

The phrase *wa'l-malā'ikatu munzalatan* — "and the angels will be sent down in successive ranks" — describes the second event of the Day: the descent of the angels. The word *munzalatan* means sent down, dispatched, brought from above. The angels do not simply appear; they descend, filling the space between the broken sky and the earth below. The image is of a vast army pouring through the rupture in ordered formations, rank after rank, filling the horizon.

The descent of the angels is mentioned elsewhere in the Qur'an in connection with the Day of Judgment. In 2:210 — "Do they wait until Allah and the angels come to them in the shadows of clouds?" — the descent is framed as something the disbelievers should anticipate. In 89:22 — "And your Lord comes, and the angels, rank upon rank" — the descent is described with the same sense of overwhelming presence. In 6:158 — "The Day when some of the signs of your Lord come, no soul will benefit from its faith unless it had believed before" — the arrival of the signs marks the end of the period in which belief is possible. The angels in 25:25 are part of this sequence: their descent is not a sign to encourage faith but a declaration that faith's opportunity has ended.

**The Cosmic Scale of the Scene**

The verse establishes the scale of what is happening by beginning with the heavens and ending with the angels. The sequence — sky, clouds, angels — moves from the cosmic to the personal, from the structure of the universe to the beings who inhabit it. The effect is one of totality: nothing is left untouched. The sky that sheltered the earth for millennia is breaking; the clouds that once carried rain now carry judgment; the angels who once operated unseen are now descending in visible, overwhelming force. The scene is not localized; it encompasses the entire cosmos. The Day of Judgment, as the Qur'an describes it, is not a human event that happens on earth; it is a cosmic event that happens to the entire created order.

The word *munzalatan* (in successive ranks) implies order, not chaos. The angels do not tumble through the broken sky; they descend in formation, rank by rank, each group in its appointed place. The order in the midst of cosmic dissolution is itself a statement: the One who breaks the sky also controls what comes through it. The destruction of the created order is not entropy; it is an act of divine will, carried out with the same precision that characterized creation (25:2: "He has created everything, ordaining it precisely"). The sūrah opens with God's sovereignty and precise creation; the Day of Judgment demonstrates that the sovereignty and precision extend to the uncreation as well.

**The Angels as Agents of Judgment**

The descent of the angels in successive ranks (*munzalatan*) carries a specific function in the Qur'anic eschatology that goes beyond the spectacular imagery. The angels are not merely present at the Day of Judgment; they are its executors. They carry the throne (69:17), they drive the sinners to Hell (74:30-31), they greet the righteous at the gates of Paradise (13:23-24), and they bear witness against the disbelievers (41:22). Their descent through the broken sky in 25:25 is therefore not a passive event but an active deployment — a cosmic army arriving for a specific mission. The word *munzalatan* (sent down, dispatched) implies purpose: the angels are not wandering through the rupture; they are being sent, directed, assigned their positions. The rank upon rank structure suggests organization, hierarchy, and command. This is not chaos; it is an operation. The effect on the disbelievers — described in 25:22 as the cry of "Keep away!" — is the natural response to a force of this magnitude. The disbelievers, who demanded to see the angels in 25:21 as a condition for belief, now face the angels as a condition for judgment, and the difference between the two conditions is the difference between an invitation and a sentence.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:26

> True authority on that Day will belong ˹only˺ to the Most Compassionate. And it will be a hard day for the disbelievers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Sovereignty That Remains**

The verse declares that on the Day described in 25:25 — the sky splitting, the angels descending in ranks — the *mulk* (sovereignty, dominion) will belong to the Most Compassionate alone. The phrase *al-mulku yawma'idhin al-ḥaqqu lil-Raḥmān* places the article *al-ḥaqq* (the true, the real) before God's name, making the statement not merely that God will possess sovereignty but that His sovereignty is the only real sovereignty — the only dominion that is true, legitimate, and ultimate. Every other claim to authority — every king, every empire, every tribal chief, every political system — is revealed on that Day as a temporary arrangement, a borrowed power that was never truly held.

The same declaration appears in other Qur'anic passages with the same force. In 18:44 — "There the authority belongs entirely to Allah, the True" — the context is a description of the destruction of a civilization, after which only God's dominion remains. In 40:16 — "The Day they come forth, nothing about them will be concealed from Allah. To whom belongs the sovereignty this Day? To Allah, the One, the All-Dominant" — the question is posed rhetorically, and the answer is stated before the audience can respond. In 25:26, the declaration follows the cosmic disruption of 25:25: the sky has broken, the angels have descended, and in the midst of that upheaval, the only thing that is stable, the only thing that is real, is the dominion of the Most Compassionate.

**Raḥmān: The Name That Implies Mercy in the Midst of Judgment**

The use of the name *al-Raḥmān* (the Most Compassionate) in a context of judgment deserves attention. *Raḥmān* is the intensive form of *raḥmah* (mercy, compassion), and the Qur'an uses it most frequently in contexts that emphasize God's overwhelming generosity and care. Its appearance in a verse about the Day of Judgment — "a hard day for the disbelievers" — creates a tension that the commentators address. Al-Rāzī notes that the name *al-Raḥmān* here emphasizes the breadth of God's dominion: even in the act of judging, His sovereignty is characterized by the quality from which creation emerged. The mercy is not removed by the judgment; it is the framework within which the judgment occurs. The Day is hard for the disbelievers not because God has ceased to be compassionate but because they have removed themselves from the reach of His compassion through their choices.

The sūrah's use of *al-Raḥmān* connects to its earlier appearances. In 25:60, the disbelievers will be asked to prostrate to the Most Compassionate and will refuse. In 25:63-76, the servants of the Most Compassionate are described in detail. The name *al-Raḥmān* is thus a thread running through the sūrah, connecting the judgment of the disbelievers to the character of the God they rejected. The same compassion they refused to acknowledge in life is the sovereignty they must face in death.

**Yawmun ʿalā'l-kāfirīna ʿasīr: The Day's Hardness**

The phrase *yawmun ʿalā'l-kāfirīna ʿasīr* — "a hard day for the disbelievers" — uses the word *ʿasīr* (difficult, hard, severe). The same word appears in 73:5 — "We will soon cast upon you a heavy word" — where it describes the weight of the Qur'anic revelation itself. In 25:26, the weight belongs to the Day: it presses down on the disbelievers with a force they cannot resist. The hardness is not abstract; it is experienced. The disbelievers will feel the weight of the Day in their bodies, their minds, and their spirits, and the weight corresponds to the gravity of what they denied. They denied the sovereignty of the Most Compassionate; the Day demonstrates that sovereignty in the most visible and inescapable way possible.

The phrase also serves as a bridge to the verses that follow. In 25:27, the wrongdoer will bite his nails in regret and wish he had followed the Messenger. The hardness of the Day produces that regret — not mere sorrow but the kind of all-consuming remorse that makes a person destroy their own hands in grief. The hardness is not a single event but a condition, a state of being that persists throughout the Day and produces ever-deeper consequences as it unfolds.

**The Meaning of True Sovereignty**

The phrase *al-mulku yawma'idhin al-ḥaqq* — "the sovereignty on that Day is the true sovereignty" — carries a theological depth that extends beyond the eschatological scene. The word *al-ḥaqq* (the true, the real) applied to sovereignty implies that sovereignty in this world is, in some sense, not fully real. Human kings and rulers exercise authority, but their authority is borrowed, temporary, and contingent on circumstances they do not control. The sovereignty of God is the only sovereignty that is *ḥaqq* — real, true, and ultimate. On the Day of Judgment, the pretense is stripped away: every ruler discovers that the power they wielded was never theirs, every empire reveals that its strength was borrowed, and every claim to authority collapses before the One whose authority is intrinsic. Al-Rāzī develops this point by noting that the word *al-ḥaqq* does not merely describe sovereignty but characterizes it: God's sovereignty is true in the sense that it corresponds to reality. Human sovereignty corresponds to social convention — a king is a king because people treat him as one. God's sovereignty corresponds to the structure of existence itself — He is the Creator, the Sustainer, the Owner of all things, and no convention or force can alter that fact.
