The Day of Judgment makes visible what was always the case: the Most Compassionate holds all authority, and every other claim to authority was a temporary illusion sustained by the permission of the One who holds the real thing.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:27

> And ˹beware of˺ the Day the wrongdoer will bite his nails ˹in regret˺ and say, “Oh! I wish I had followed the Way along with the Messenger

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gesture of Self-Destruction**

The verse describes the wrongdoer's response to the Day declared in 25:26: *yawma yaʿaddu'ẓ-ẓālimu ʿalā yadayhi* — "the Day the wrongdoer will bite his hands." The verb *yaʿaddu* means to bite, and the object is *yadayhi* — his two hands. The image is visceral: a person so consumed by regret that they attack their own body, biting their hands as though the hands were responsible for what they did. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the gesture is an expression of extreme grief in Arabian culture — the biting of the hands is a sign of helplessness, of the inability to undo what has been done, of rage directed inward because there is nowhere else to direct it.

The phrase *naḍiman ʿalā mā farrata fī'l-amr* — "in regret over what he neglected in the matter" — specifies the cause. The wrongdoer is not regretting what he did; he is regretting what he failed to do. The word *farrata* means to neglect, to let slip, to waste an opportunity. The regret is not for a sin committed but for a path not taken. The wrongdoer did not follow the Messenger, and now, standing in the wreckage of the Day, he understands what following would have meant. The regret is sharper than the regret for an active sin because it is the regret for a choice that required no effort — all he had to do was follow, and he did not.

**"I Wish I Had Taken a Way With the Messenger"**

The wrongdoer's wish — *yā laytanī ittakhadhtu maʿa'r-rasūli sabīlā* — uses the word *sabīl* (way, path), the same word that appeared in 25:9 to describe what the disbelievers could not find. In 25:9, they went so far astray that they "could not find the way"; in 25:27, the wrongdoer wishes he had taken the way with the Messenger. The verbal echo connects the two passages: the inability to find the way in life becomes the desperate wish for the way in death. The *sabīl* that was available — that was offered, that was walked by the Prophet and his companions — is now the thing the wrongdoer most desires and cannot have.

The phrase *maʿa'r-rasūli* ("with the Messenger") is significant. The wrongdoer does not wish he had followed the way in general; he wishes he had followed it specifically alongside the Messenger. The Prophet ﷺ was the embodiment of the path — the living example of what the Criterion (25:1) looked like in practice — and to walk with him would have been to walk the way. The wish is not for abstract faith but for companionship with the one who demonstrated faith. The wrongdoer's regret is therefore not merely theological but personal: he misses the person he rejected.

**The Transition to Blaming the Friend**

Verse 25:27 ends with the wrongdoer's self-reproach, and 25:28 will shift the blame to the friend who led him astray. The transition is psychologically precise. The first response to catastrophic regret is self-blame: "I wish I had followed." The second response, when self-blame becomes unbearable, is to find someone else to blame: "I wish I had never taken so-and-so as a friend." The movement from self-accusation to other-accusation is the human response to irreversible failure, and the Qur'an records both stages without choosing between them. The wrongdoer is responsible for his own choices, and the friend who influenced him is also responsible. The Day of Judgment will hold each accountable for their own part, but the wrongdoer, standing in the moment of regret, has not yet arrived at that distinction. He is still in the rawness of loss, and the rawness expresses itself as alternating waves of self-blame and blame of others.

**The Wrongdoer's Failed Attempt at Self-Justification**

The wrongdoer's words in 25:27 are not a confession in the theological sense — an act of repentance that might earn forgiveness. They are a cry of despair spoken too late. The verb *yaʿaddu* (to bite) conveys not just grief but rage — a physical violence directed at the self, as though the body were responsible for the mind's failure. The wrongdoer is not repenting; he is expressing the kind of regret that has no outlet, the kind that turns inward and destroys. The wish — "I wish I had followed the Way along with the Messenger" — is not a prayer but a lament. The wrongdoer does not address God; he addresses the empty air, speaking to no one, because there is no one left who can help. The Messenger he wishes he had followed is beyond reach; the Way he wishes he had taken is closed. What remains is the utterance itself — words that change nothing, spoken by a person who has arrived at the one place where words are worthless. This is the Qur'an's most severe portrait of regret: not the regret that leads to change but the regret that leads nowhere, the regret that is the final form of suffering. The sūrah places this portrait immediately after the declaration that true sovereignty belongs to the Most Compassionate (25:26), and the juxtaposition sharpens the contrast: the One who holds all authority is paired with the one who has no authority left, not even over his own hands.
 The wrongdoer’s physical self-harm — biting his hands — is the body’s response to the mind’s recognition that the opportunity for change has been permanently lost. In life, regret can be productive: it motivates repentance, correction, a change of direction. In the Hereafter, regret is sterile: it changes nothing, corrects nothing, and produces nothing but additional suffering. The wrongdoer’s wish to have followed the Messenger is the most painful form of regret because it names the specific alternative that was available and refused. He does not wish for something impossible; he wishes for something that was easy, that required nothing more than saying yes, and that he declined. The ease of the missed opportunity is the source of the anguish, and the anguish is the punishment.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:28

> Woe to me! I wish I had never taken so-and-so as a close friend

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shift From Self-Blame to Other-Blame**

The verse continues the wrongdoer's monologue from 25:27. Where the previous verse expressed regret for not following the Messenger, this verse redirects the anguish outward: *yā laytanī lam attakhidh fulānan khalīlā* — "Woe to me! I wish I had never taken so-and-so as a close friend." The transition is psychologically realistic. When self-blame reaches a threshold of pain that cannot be sustained, the mind seeks an external cause. The wrongdoer cannot undo his own failure, so he identifies the person who helped produce it — the *khalīl*, the intimate friend, the one whose companionship shaped his choices.

The word *khalīl* means more than an acquaintance or a casual companion. In Arabic, *khalīl* denotes a friend so close that the relationship penetrates to the interior — the word is related to *khulla*, meaning the innermost part of the heart. Abraham is called *khalīl Allāh* (the intimate friend of God) because of the depth of his relationship with God. When the wrongdoer wishes he had never taken this person as a *khalīl*, he is naming a bond that went beyond social convenience — a bond that shaped his worldview, his values, and his decisions. The regret is not for a superficial association but for a deep one, because deep associations are the ones that redirect the course of a life.

**So-and-So: The Unnamed Friend**

The verse uses *fulānan* — "so-and-so" — to refer to the friend, leaving the identity unnamed. This anonymity is deliberate. Al-Qurṭubī records that some commentators tie these verses to a specific historical incident — a man of Madinah who was led away from Islam by a companion — but the majority position, supported by al-Rāzī, is that the verses are general. The unnamed friend is anyone whose companionship leads a person away from the truth. The use of *fulānan* makes the warning universal: every person who has a close friend should consider whether that friendship is leading them toward the Messenger or away from him.

The anonymity also protects the wrongdoer from a particular kind of self-deception. If the friend were named, the wrongdoer could frame his regret as a specific, unrepeatable error — "I was misled by that particular person." By leaving the friend unnamed, the verse forces the wrongdoer (and the reader) to acknowledge that the choice of friendship was itself the error. The problem was not the friend's identity but the wrongdoer's willingness to make that friend the most influential voice in his life. The regret is not "I chose the wrong person" but "I chose to be shaped by a person rather than by the truth."

**The Companion Who Replaced the Reminder**

Verse 25:29 will explain what the friend did: he led the wrongdoer away from the Reminder after it had reached him. The placement of 25:28 before 25:29 is the sūrah's way of building the wrongdoer's confession in stages. First, self-blame (25:27): I wish I had followed the Messenger. Then, other-blame (25:28): I wish I had not taken this friend. Then, diagnosis (25:29): the friend led me astray from the Reminder. The sequence moves from regret to accusation to explanation, and each stage adds a layer of understanding. The wrongdoer is not merely suffering; he is analyzing his own failure, tracing it from its symptom (not following the Messenger) to its cause (the companion who replaced the Messenger's guidance with something else).

The theological implication is that companionship is a form of worship. The choice of who to listen to, who to trust, who to spend one's time with — these are moral choices with eternal consequences. The wrongdoer did not choose the friend innocently; he chose the friend over the Reminder, and the choice was a form of prioritization that amounts to worship. The friend became the *khalīl* — the intimate — and the Reminder was pushed to the margins. On the Day of Judgment, the wrongdoer sees what that prioritization cost, and the regret is the measure of the loss.

**The Weight of Companionship in the Qur'an**

The Qur'an assigns enormous moral weight to the choice of companions. In 25:27-28, the wrongdoer traces his ruin to a single relationship — the *khalīl* (intimate friend) who led him astray. This is not an isolated theme; it runs through the Qur'an's moral teaching. In 18:28 — "And keep yourself patient with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His countenance" — the Prophet is instructed to choose his companions from among the devoted. In 43:67 — "Close friends on that Day will be foes to one another, except for the God-conscious" — the dissolution of worldly friendships on the Day of Judgment is described. In 70:10-11 — "And no intimate friend will ask about his intimate friend, though they will be shown each other" — the bonds that seemed permanent in life prove to be temporary in death. The wrongdoer's wish in 25:28 — "I wish I had never taken so-and-so as a close friend" — is the negative expression of this teaching. The positive expression is the Qur'an's instruction to choose companions who draw one toward God rather than away from Him. The choice of a *khalīl* is not a social decision but a moral one, and the consequences of that choice extend beyond this life into the Day when friends become strangers and strangers become neighbors in the fire or the garden.
 The theological weight of companionship in the Qur’an is not merely moral but cosmological: the friends one chooses shape the soul’s orientation, and the soul’s orientation determines its destination. The wrongdoer’s khalīl did not merely distract him from the Reminder; the khalīl replaced the Reminder as the wrongdoer’s primary source of guidance. The friend’s voice became more authoritative than God’s voice, and the friend’s opinion became more compelling than God’s revelation. This replacement is the mechanism of misguidance: it is not the addition of falsehood but the substitution of one authority for another.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:29

> It was he who truly made me stray from the Reminder after it had reached me.” And Satan has always betrayed humanity

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reminder That Arrived and Was Rejected**

The wrongdoer completes his confession with a specific accusation: *la-qad aḍallanī ʿani'dh-dhikri baʿda idh jā'anī* — "He truly made me stray from the Reminder after it had reached me." The word *dhikr* (Reminder) is one of the Qur'an's names for itself — the thing that reminds, that calls to memory what the soul already knows. The wrongdoer's confession confirms that the Reminder did arrive: it was not lost, not hidden, not unavailable. It *came to him* — *jā'anī* — and he was in a position to receive it. The failure was not in the delivery but in the reception. The friend intervened after the Reminder had arrived, and the friend's influence was stronger than the Reminder's truth.

The word *la-qad* (truly, certainly) is an oath-like intensifier. The wrongdoer is not speculating about what happened; he is testifying to it. The friend did not merely contribute to the wrongdoer's misguidance; he caused it — *aḍallanī* is causative, meaning "he caused me to go astray." This does not remove the wrongdoer's responsibility; it distributes it. The wrongdoer chose the friend, chose to listen, chose to follow. The friend chose to lead astray. Both are culpable, and the Day of Judgment will hold each accountable for their own choice. But the wrongdoer, in his confession, is naming the mechanism: the Reminder was there, and the friend overrode it.

**Satan as the Archetype of Betrayal**

The verse closes with a general statement: *wa kāna's-shayṭānu lil-insāni khaḍūlā* — "And Satan has always betrayed humanity." The word *khaḍūl* means one who betrays, abandons, lets down. Satan's defining characteristic, in the Qur'anic portrayal, is not cruelty but desertion: he tempts, he leads astray, and then he abandons those who followed him. In 14:22 — "Satan will say when the matter is decided, 'Allah gave you a truthful promise, and I gave you a promise and broke it. I had no authority over you except that I invited you, and you responded to me'" — Satan confesses that he was never a true ally. He was a voice, a suggestion, an influence — and when the consequences arrive, he disowns his followers entirely.

The word *khaḍūl* applies to both Satan and to the human friend the wrongdoer blamed in 25:28. The friend who led him astray was a *shayṭān* in the extended sense the Qur'an allows: *shayṭān* (Satan) is not only the proper name of Iblīs but a category that includes any being — human or jinn — who leads others away from the truth. In 6:112 — "We have made for every prophet enemies — satans from among mankind and jinn, who inspire each other with flowery discourse in order to deceive" — the word *shayāṭīn* (satans) is applied to human beings who act as Satan's agents. The wrongdoer's friend in 25:28 is one such agent, and the closing statement of 25:29 names the pattern: Satan betrays, and those who serve Satan's purpose betray in the same way. The friend who led the wrongdoer astray will not stand with him on the Day of Judgment; like Satan, the friend will have abandoned his companion to face the consequences alone.

**The Universality of the Wrongdoer's Confession**

Although the wrongdoer in 25:27-29 speaks in the first person, his confession is not a private experience. The sūrah presents it as a representative voice — the voice of every person who, on the Day of Judgment, looks back and traces the path that led them to ruin. The stages are universal: the Reminder arrived (25:29), a companion intervened (25:28), the companion's influence overrode the Reminder (25:29), and the companion proved to be a betrayer (25:29). Every person who has been led away from the truth by the influence of a friend, a social group, or a cultural norm will recognize this pattern. The sūrah does not specify the friend's name because the friend is different for everyone; the pattern is the same.

**The Reminder That Was Abandoned**

The word *dhikr* (Reminder) in 25:29 refers to the Quran itself — one of whose names is *al-dhikr* (the Reminder) — and to the broader act of remembering God. The wrongdoer's confession that his friend led him away from the Reminder "after it had reached me" specifies a sequence: the Reminder arrived first, the friend intervened second. This sequence is crucial because it establishes that the wrongdoer had access to the truth before the friend's influence took hold. The Reminder was not abstract or distant; it had *reached* him — *jā'anī* — and was available for acceptance. The friend did not introduce doubt where none existed; the friend exploited an existing weakness, amplifying the wrongdoer's reluctance until it overcame the Reminder's pull. The mechanism is one the Qur'an describes elsewhere: "Satan promises them and arouses desire in them, but Satan does not promise them except delusion" (4:120). The friend served as a *shayṭān* — a tempter, an agent of misguidance — and the temptation succeeded because the wrongdoer valued the friend's companionship more than the Reminder's truth. The verse's closing declaration — "Satan has always been a betrayer of humanity" — names the archetype and warns that the pattern will repeat: every person who chooses a human companion over divine guidance will find, on the Day of Judgment, that the companion was a satan in disguise, and the betrayal was built into the relationship from the start.
 The sūrah’s presentation of this confession serves a didactic purpose for the present. The reader, who is still alive, is invited to examine their own friendships and ask the question the wrongdoer will ask on the Day: is my closest companion leading me toward the Reminder or away from it? The question is not abstract; it is the most practical question the sūrah poses. The choice of a friend is the choice of a direction, and the direction leads either to the Garden of Eternity or to the narrow place of torment.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:30

> The Messenger has cried, “O my Lord! My people have indeed received this Quran with neglect.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Complaint to God**

The verse shifts from the wrongdoer's confession to the Prophet's complaint: *wa qāla'r-rasūlu yā Rabbi inna qawmī ittakhadhū hādhā'l-qur'āna mahjūrā* — "And the Messenger said, 'O my Lord, my people have indeed taken this Quran as something neglected.'" The word *mahjūr* means neglected, abandoned, treated as something to be avoided. It is the same root as *ḥijr* (barrier, forbidden zone) in 25:22, where the disbelievers cried "Keep away! A forbidden barrier!" The verbal echo connects the two passages: the disbelievers kept away from the Quran, and the Prophet now reports to God that his people have treated the revelation as something from which to maintain distance.

The complaint is spoken either during the Prophet's earthly life — as a report of the treatment the Quran receives in Makkah — or in the Hereafter, as a final accounting of what his mission produced among his people. Al-Rāzī records both readings and notes that the present tense *ittakhadhū* ("they have taken") is compatible with either time frame. What matters is the content: the Prophet's people — *qawmī* — have not merely rejected the Quran; they have abandoned it, treating it as a thing to be discarded, a text beneath their attention. The word *mahjūr* carries the force of contempt: the Quran is not being argued with or debated; it is being ignored.

**The Prophet's Grief and the Limits of His Responsibility**

The verse reveals the emotional reality of prophethood. The Prophet ﷺ did not deliver the Quran with detachment; he delivered it with the full weight of his concern for his people. The complaint — "my people have received this Quran with neglect" — is not a report of facts; it is a cry of grief. The word *yā Rabbi* ("O my Lord") is an address of intimacy, the appeal of a servant to his master, a child to his parent. The Prophet is not asking for punishment or for vindication; he is expressing the pain of watching the thing he loves most — the revelation — be treated with contempt by the people he loves most — his community.

The Qur'an records this complaint without judgment. It does not rebuke the Prophet for expressing his grief, nor does it tell him to be more patient. The recording itself is an act of validation: the Prophet's grief is real, it is appropriate, and it is heard by God. The same dynamic appears in 5:41 — "O Messenger, do not be grieved by those who race toward disbelief" — where the divine response acknowledges the Prophet's emotional state without suppressing it. Prophethood in the Qur'an is not stoic; it is human, and the human cost of delivering a message that is rejected is the grief that 25:30 makes visible.

**The Link to the Wrongdoer's Regret**

The placement of 25:30 after the wrongdoer's confession (25:27-29) creates a structural parallel. The wrongdoer regrets not following the Messenger; the Messenger grieves that his people did not follow the Quran. Both are expressing the same loss from opposite sides: the one who was not followed, and the one who did not follow. The two perspectives together complete the picture: the rejection of the Quran was felt by both parties, but only one party felt it in time to change. The wrongdoer's regret comes too late — the Day has arrived, and the opportunity is gone. The Prophet's complaint comes in the midst of the rejection, while the opportunity still exists, and the divine response — the verse that follows (25:31) — will affirm that God is sufficient as a Guide and Helper.

**The Sūrah's Voice: From Disbelievers to Prophet**

The sūrah has moved through several voices: the disbelievers speak in 25:4-8 and 25:21, the wrongdoer speaks in 25:27-29, and now the Prophet speaks in 25:30. Each voice reveals a different dimension of the same conflict. The disbelievers' voice reveals their objections; the wrongdoer's voice reveals their consequences; the Prophet's voice reveals the human cost. The sūrah's structure is not linear but polyphonic — multiple speakers, multiple perspectives, all addressing the same central question: what happens when the truth is offered and refused? The Prophet's cry in 25:30 is the heart of the sūrah's emotional argument: the Quran was not merely rejected; it was neglected, abandoned, treated as though it did not exist. And the one who delivered it is left to bear the weight of that neglect alone.

**Mahjūr: The Quran as Something Abandoned**

The Prophet's use of the word *mahjūr* to describe his people's treatment of the Quran carries a precise meaning that goes beyond simple rejection. *Mahjūr* means not merely ignored but treated as something to be actively avoided — a thing from which one maintains distance, a source of contamination rather than guidance. The same root appears in 25:22, where the disbelievers cry *ḥijran maḥjūrā* ("Keep away! A forbidden barrier!") to the angels. The verbal echo is deliberate: the disbelievers created a barrier between themselves and the Quran, and the barrier is the same one they will erect between themselves and the angels on the Day of Judgment. The neglect of the Quran in this life is the rehearsal for the rejection of mercy in the next. The Prophet's complaint to God is therefore not merely about his people's indifference; it is about the pattern that indifference establishes. A people who treat the Quran as something to avoid will, on the Day of Judgment, treat God's mercy the same way — and the cry of "Keep away!" that they will direct at the angels is the natural extension of the distance they maintained from the revelation. The Prophet sees this pattern forming in real time and grieves for its consequence, and the grief is the grief of a man watching his people walk toward a fire they cannot see.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:31

> Similarly, We made enemies for every prophet from among the wicked, but sufficient is your Lord as a Guide and Helper

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophetic Pattern of Opposition**

The verse answers the Prophet's grief from 25:30 with a statement of historical fact: *wa kathālika jaʿalnā li-kulli nabiyyin ʿaduwwan min al-mujrimīn* — "Similarly, We have made for every prophet enemies from among the wicked." The word *kathālika* ("similarly," "in the same way") places the Prophet Muhammad's experience inside a universal pattern. His rejection is not unique; every prophet before him faced enemies drawn from the same category of people — *al-mujrimīn*, the sinners, the criminals, those who have placed themselves in the wrong. The comfort is not that the suffering is less but that it is shared: the Prophet stands in a line of messengers who were all opposed by the same kind of people for the same kind of reasons.

The verse that most closely parallels this one is 6:112 — "Similarly, We have made for every prophet enemies — satans from among mankind and jinn, who inspire each other with flowery discourse in order to deceive." That verse specifies the mechanism: the enemies are *shayāṭīn* (satans), both human and non-human, who work together to oppose the prophetic message. In 25:31, the mechanism is left unstated, but the category is the same: the enemies come from among the criminals, the people whose way of life is threatened by the message. The prophetic mission, by its nature, disrupts existing power structures, and those who benefit from the disruption's absence will oppose the messenger. The opposition is not a sign of the message's failure but of its relevance: a message that threatens no one changes nothing.

**God's Sufficiency as Guide and Helper**

The second half of the verse — *wa kafā bi-Rabbika hādiyan wa naṣīrān* — responds to the Prophet's complaint with a direct assurance: your Lord is sufficient as a Guide and a Helper. The word *kafā* (sufficient, enough) appears in similar contexts across the Qur'an. In 3:173 — "Allah is sufficient for us, and He is the best disposer of affairs" — the word is spoken by the believers when they face an army larger than their own. In 4:79 — "Whatever good comes to you is from Allah, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself" — the sufficiency of God is paired with the reality of human responsibility. In 25:31, the sufficiency addresses the Prophet's specific need: he has complained that his people neglect the Quran, and God responds that the Prophet does not need their acceptance. God is enough — as a Guide who directs the message to those who will receive it, and as a Helper who protects the messenger from those who reject it.

The two names — *hādī* (Guide) and *naṣīr* (Helper) — cover different aspects of divine support. The Guide provides direction: the Prophet is not left to figure out the next step on his own; God guides him through revelation, through inspiration, through the unfolding of events. The Helper provides protection: the Prophet is not left to face his enemies alone; God supports him through the believers, through the events of history, and through the ultimate promise that the message will endure. The combination of guidance and help is comprehensive: the Prophet will not lose his way, and he will not be overcome.

**The Subsequent Catalogue of Prophetic Enemies**

The commentators note that the verses following 25:31 — the mention of Moses and Aaron (25:35-36), Noah (25:37), ʿĀd and Thamūd (25:38), and the people of Lot (25:40) — function as an extended commentary on the principle stated in 25:31. Each of these prophets faced enemies from among the criminals, and each of those enemies was ultimately destroyed. The catalogue serves two purposes: it confirms the Prophet Muhammad's experience as part of a larger pattern, and it warns the disbelievers of Makkah that the pattern includes not only opposition but consequence. The prophets' enemies were not vindicated by history; they were destroyed by God. The disbelievers of Makkah are standing in the same position as the enemies of Noah, Moses, and Lot, and the verse invites them to consider whether they want to share that position's outcome.

**The Prophetic Consolation and Its Universal Application**

The assurance that God is sufficient as a Guide and Helper addresses the Prophet's specific situation but carries a universal application. Every person who delivers an unpopular truth — in the Prophet's time or in any other — faces the same dynamic: the message is rejected, the messenger is opposed, and the temptation is to conclude that the opposition proves the message wrong. The verse reverses this logic: the opposition proves the message relevant. If the truth did not threaten anyone, it would be because it changed nothing. The fact that every prophet had enemies from among the criminals is not a defect of the prophetic mission; it is a feature. The enemies are evidence that the message has reached its target. The consolation — "your Lord is sufficient as a Guide and Helper" — extends to anyone who stands in the prophetic position: the teacher whose students resist, the reformer whose community opposes, the parent whose child rejects. The sufficiency of God means that the outcome is not determined by the opposition's strength but by the message's truth, and the truth has a track record: every prophet who was opposed was ultimately vindicated, and every enemy who opposed them was ultimately destroyed. The pattern is the consolation, and the consolation is not theoretical but historical — it has happened before, and it will happen again.
 The verse’s closing assurance is not merely a comfort but a theological principle. The prophetic mission does not depend on human acceptance for its validity. The message is true regardless of how many people believe it, and the messenger is successful regardless of how many people follow him. Success in the prophetic sense is measured by fidelity to the message, not by the size of the audience. The disbelievers’ opposition, therefore, does not diminish the Prophet’s standing or weaken his mission. It confirms his standing as a prophet — because every prophet was opposed — and it strengthens his mission by demonstrating that the message is potent enough to provoke the kind of resistance that only the truth provokes.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:32

> The disbelievers say, “If only the Quran had been sent down to him all at once!” ˹We have sent it˺ as such ˹in stages˺ so We may reassure your heart with it. And We have revealed it at a deliberate pace

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Instant Revelation**

The disbelievers return with another objection, recorded in their own words: *wa qāla'lladhīna kafarū law-lā nuzzila ʿalayhi'l-qur'ānu jumlatan wāḥidatan* — "If only the Quran had been sent down to him all at once!" The demand echoes their earlier objections in 25:4-8: they have tried to explain the Quran as fabrication, as borrowed tales, as bewitchment, and now they try a new angle — it should have come all at once, like a book, like a completed text delivered in a single package. The implication is that the piecemeal revelation is evidence of human composition: a man writing a text produces it gradually, while God, who is omnipotent, would deliver it immediately.

The demand ignores the Torah and the Psalms, both of which were revealed in stages over extended periods, and both of which the disbelievers' own religious traditions acknowledged as divine. The objection is not theological but rhetorical: the disbelievers are not making a serious argument about how revelation should work; they are finding fault with whatever form the revelation takes. If it came all at once, they would ask why it was not sent gradually to match the needs of the community; if it came gradually, they ask why it was not sent all at once. The sūrah records the objection without engaging its logic, because the objection has no logic to engage.

**The Divine Reason: Reassurance of the Heart**

The divine response — *li-nuthabbita bihi fu'ādaka wa rattalnāhu tartīlā* — names a single purpose for the gradual revelation: "so We may reassure your heart with it." The word *nuthabbita* (to make firm, to establish, to reassure) is the same word used in 11:120 — "All that We relate to you of the stories of the messengers is that by which We make firm your heart." The Quran's stories, laws, and exhortations were not delivered to a finished prophet; they were delivered to a growing one. The piecemeal nature of the revelation allowed the Prophet ﷺ to absorb, internalize, and live each part before the next arrived. A single overwhelming delivery would have been a burden; the gradual delivery was a support.

The word *fu'ād* (heart, inner self) refers to the deepest center of human consciousness — not the physical organ but the seat of understanding, emotion, and will. The Quran's gradual revelation was designed to reach this depth. Each verse arrived at the moment the Prophet needed it most — during persecution, during doubt, during the establishment of a community, during the formulation of law. The timing was not arbitrary but deliberate, calibrated to the Prophet's circumstances and the community's needs. Al-Rāzī notes that the gradual revelation served multiple purposes simultaneously: it reassured the Prophet, it addressed the community's evolving needs, it made the Quran easier to memorize, and it kept the disbelievers in a state of constant anticipation, never knowing what the next revelation would contain.

**Tartīlā: The Pace of Deliberate Recitation**

The final word — *wa rattalnāhu tartīlā* — is translated as "We have revealed it at a deliberate pace," but the Arabic carries additional layers. *Tartīl* means measured, ordered, arranged in proper sequence. The same word is used in 73:4 — "And recite the Quran in a measured tone" — where it describes the Prophet's own recitation. The connection between the two verses reveals a correspondence: the revelation came in a measured way so that it could be received and recited in a measured way. The pace of revelation shaped the pace of worship, of memorization, of communal life. The piecemeal delivery was not a deficiency but a design, and the design produced a community that lived with the Quran as it arrived, absorbing it verse by verse, day by day, year by year.

The disbelievers' demand for a single delivery would have eliminated this gradual immersion. A book delivered all at once is read once and shelved; a revelation that arrives over twenty-three years is lived. The difference is between a text and a companion, and the Quran, as the sūrah presents it, was always intended to be the latter.

**The Architecture of Gradual Revelation**

The verse names the purpose of gradual revelation — *li-nuthabbita bihi fu'ādaka* ("to reassure your heart") — but the commentators identify additional purposes that the verse implies. Al-Rāzī lists several: gradual revelation allowed the community to absorb each command before receiving the next, preventing the overwhelm that a single delivery might have caused; it allowed the legal framework to develop in response to the community's evolving circumstances, so that each ruling arrived when it was most needed; it provided the Prophet with ongoing divine support during the years of persecution, ensuring that he received fresh guidance at the moments of greatest difficulty; and it kept the disbelievers in a state of uncertainty, never knowing when the next revelation would come or what it would contain, which prevented them from formulating a comprehensive strategy against it. The word *tartīl* (measured pace) names the quality of the delivery: it was not random but deliberate, each verse placed in its proper sequence, each revelation timed to its proper moment. The disbelievers' demand for a single delivery would have eliminated all of these functions. A book delivered at once is a finished product; a revelation delivered over twenty-three years is a living relationship between God and His community, and the relationship is the point. The Quran is not a book that was given; it is a guidance that was lived.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:33

> Whenever they bring you an argument, We come to you with the right refutation and the best explanation

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Guarantee of Refutation**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet with a promise: *wa lā ya'tūnaka bi-mathalin illā ji'nāka bi'l-ḥaqqi wa aḥsana tafsīrā* — "And they do not bring you any argument but that We come to you with the truth and a better explanation." The word *mathal* (parable, argument, comparison) refers to the objections the disbelievers raised throughout the sūrah — the charges of fabrication (25:4), borrowing (25:5), bewitchment (25:8), and the demands for material signs (25:7-8). Each of these was a *mathal*, a rhetorical construction designed to discredit the Prophet. The promise is that every such construction will be met with *al-ḥaqq* (the truth) and *aḥsana tafsīrā* (the best explanation).

The word *tafsīr* is significant because it later became the technical term for Qur'anic exegesis. In 25:33, it refers to God's own explanation of the truth — not human commentary but divine clarification. The explanation is always better (*aḥsana*) than the argument it answers, because it comes from a higher source and addresses a deeper level. The disbelievers' arguments operate on the surface — accusation, mockery, comparison. God's explanations operate at the level of reality — what is true, what is real, what will last. The asymmetry is permanent: no argument the disbelievers construct can exceed the explanation God provides, because the explanations come from the One who knows everything (25:6: "the One Who knows the secrets of the heavens and the earth").

**The Sūrah's Own Demonstration of This Promise**

The verse can be read as a description of the sūrah's own structure. The sūrah opens with the disbelievers' objections (25:4-8), and each objection is answered by the verses that follow. The charge of fabrication is answered by the declaration of divine origin (25:6). The demand for material signs is answered by the promise of Paradise (25:10). The denial of the Hour is answered by the description of the fire and the garden (25:11-16). The demand for visible angels is answered by the description of the angels' descent on the Day of Judgment (25:22, 25:25). The sūrah itself is the demonstration of the principle 25:33 states: every argument is met with a better explanation, and the explanations accumulate until the case is complete.

The word *al-ḥaqq* (the truth) names the quality of the refutation. It is not merely clever or persuasive; it is true. The disbelievers' arguments may be rhetorically effective — they were, after all, sufficient to keep the Quraysh united in opposition for years — but they are not true. The distinction between effectiveness and truth is the Qur'an's consistent point: an argument can be persuasive without being correct, and the Day of Judgment will reveal the difference. The truth that God provides in response to the disbelievers' arguments is not a counter-argument; it is reality itself, and reality does not need to be persuasive. It only needs to be revealed.

**The Prophet's Role in the Exchange**

The verse positions the Prophet as the recipient of both the arguments and the answers. The disbelievers bring their arguments to him (*ya'tūnaka*), and God brings the answers to him (*ji'nāka*). The Prophet stands in the middle, receiving from both sides, and his role is to deliver what he receives. He does not construct the refutation; he conveys it. This is the meaning of *qul* (say) throughout the sūrah: the Prophet speaks what God tells him to speak, and the truth of the speech belongs to God, not to the speaker. The verse's assurance — "We come to you with the truth" — is therefore not merely a promise of support but a description of the prophetic function: the Prophet receives the truth and transmits it, and the transmission is the message. The disbelievers' arguments are addressed to a human being; the answers come from the divine source that human being serves.

**The Meaning of Tafsīr in the Verse**

The word *tafsīr* in 25:33 — "the best explanation" — is the same word that later became the technical term for the scholarly discipline of Qur'anic interpretation. In this verse, the word refers to God's own explanation of the truth — not human commentary but divine clarification. The explanation is always better (*aḥsana*) than the argument it answers, because it comes from a higher source and addresses a deeper level. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the word *mathal* (parable, argument) in this verse refers specifically to the rhetorical constructions the disbelievers used to challenge the Prophet — comparisons, objections, accusations — and that God's response to each of these was not merely adequate but superior. The superiority is qualitative: the disbelievers' arguments operate on the surface of things, addressing form and appearance, while God's explanations operate at the level of truth, addressing substance and reality. The verse establishes a principle that governs the entire sūrah: every challenge the disbelievers raise will be answered, and the answer will be better than the challenge. This is not merely a promise of rhetorical victory; it is a declaration that truth always exceeds falsehood. The disbelievers can construct arguments, but they cannot construct truth, because truth belongs to God alone. The word *tafsīr* — explanation, clarification — names the mode of God's response: He does not merely refute; He clarifies. He shows what the disbelievers' arguments obscure, and what He shows is always more complete, more accurate, and more compelling than what the arguments attempt to hide.
 The verse’s promise that God will provide a better explanation for every argument the disbelievers raise also implies that the Qur’an is self-defending. The Prophet does not need to construct apologetics or develop philosophical proofs for the Qur’an’s divine origin. The Qur’an defends itself by being what it is: a text whose coherence, guidance, and transformative power exceed what any human author could produce. The disbelievers’ arguments against it are answered not by counter-argument but by the Qur’an’s own continued existence and influence.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:34

> Those who will be dragged into Hell on their faces will be in the worst place, and are ˹now˺ farthest from the ˹Right˺ Way

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Face as the Instrument of Punishment**

The verse describes the manner of entry into Hell: *alladhīna yuḥsharūna ʿalā wujūhim ilā jahannam* — "those who will be gathered upon their faces into Hell." The image is one of total degradation: human beings dragged to punishment not on their feet, not walking, not even carried, but on their faces. The face in every culture is the seat of identity, dignity, and recognition. To be dragged on the face is to have every form of dignity removed — the person cannot see where they are going, cannot choose their direction, cannot present themselves as anything other than what they are: a creature in the hands of a power that has decided their destination.

The same image appears in other Qur'anic eschatological passages. In 17:97 — "We will gather them on the Day of Resurrection, fallen on their faces, blind, mute, and deaf" — the loss of faculties (sight, speech, hearing) accompanies the loss of posture. In 54:48 — "The Day they are dragged into the Fire on their faces" — the verb *yusḥarūna* (dragged) emphasizes the force involved. In 8:50 — "If only you could see when the angels take the souls of the disbelievers, striking their faces and their backs" — the face is the target of the angels' blows. The Qur'an's consistent association of the face with the punishment of the disbelievers is not incidental: it is the ultimate reversal of the human tendency to present the face — to show dignity, to demand respect, to maintain appearance. The face that was used to project pride in life is used to absorb humiliation in death.

**Ashqa: The Worst Position**

The phrase *aulā'u sharrun makānan wa aḍallu sabīlā* — "those are the worst in position and the most astray from the path" — uses the word *ashqa* (most wretched, most miserable) in other recitations, and *sharr* (worst, most evil) in the reading reflected in this translation. The word *makān* (position, place) is both literal and figurative: the disbelievers are in the worst physical place — Hell — and in the worst spiritual condition — the greatest distance from guidance. The two are related: the place reflects the condition, and the condition produced the place.

The word *aḍallu sabīlā* — "farthest from the Way" — echoes the sūrah's recurring use of *sabīl* (way, path) to describe guidance. In 25:9, the disbelievers "could not find the way." In 25:27, the wrongdoer wishes he "had taken a way with the Messenger." In 25:34, the final position of the disbelievers is defined by their distance from the Way. The sūrah tracks the concept of *sabīl* from inability (25:9) to regret (25:27) to final distance (25:34), and the trajectory is one of increasing loss. The Way was available at every point; the disbelievers moved further from it at every point; and the final position — dragged into Hell on their faces — is the terminus of that movement.

**The Present Tense: Already Astray**

A striking feature of the verse is the shift to the present tense in the final clause: *wa aḍallu sabīlā* — "and are farthest from the Way." The verb is present, not future. The disbelievers who will be dragged into Hell are already, now, in this life, the farthest from the Way. The punishment does not create the condition; it reveals it. The distance from guidance that the disbelievers experience on the Day of Judgment is the same distance they have been building throughout their lives. The future tense (*yuḥsharūna*, "they will be gathered") describes what will happen to their bodies; the present tense (*aḍallu*, "they are astray") describes what has already happened to their souls. The physical punishment is the visible form of a spiritual reality that exists now, unseen, and will be made visible on the Day when all hidden things are exposed.

**The Face as the Seat of Identity and Dignity**

The Qur'an's consistent association of the face with the punishment of the disbelievers and with the reward of the righteous creates a theological vocabulary centered on the human face. In 80:38-39 — "Faces on that Day will be radiant, laughing, rejoicing" — the righteous are described through their faces. In 88:2-3 — "Some faces on that Day will be humbled, exhausted and weary" — the disbelievers are described the same way. The face is the visible seat of the self — the place where identity, emotion, and dignity are displayed — and the Qur'an uses it as the primary marker of a person's spiritual state. In 25:34, the face is pressed into the ground, dragged toward Hell, and the degradation is total because the face is the last thing a person surrenders. The body can be controlled, the voice can be silenced, but the face — the expression, the gaze, the set of the mouth — is the final frontier of human dignity. The punishment in 25:34 crosses that frontier: the face is not merely humiliated but used as the instrument of transport, and the person is reduced to the physical surface of their body. The contrast with the radiant faces of the righteous (80:38) is the sūrah's unstated comparison: what the righteous receive in beauty, the disbelievers receive in degradation, and the organ of both is the same.
 The description of the disbelievers being dragged on their faces also serves as a warning for the present. The face that is used for pride and self-presentation in this life will be used for punishment in the next. The social face — the one maintained for appearances, the one that projects dignity and demands respect — is the same face that will be pressed into the ground of Hell. The sūrah does not make this connection explicitly, but it does not need to: the image is vivid enough to carry its own application.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:35

> We certainly gave Moses the Book and appointed his brother Aaron as his helper

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Introduction of Moses Into the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse marks a shift in the sūrah's method. Since 25:4, the text has been responding to the disbelievers' objections directly — answering their charges, describing their punishment, recording their regret. Beginning with 25:35, the sūrah introduces a catalogue of earlier prophets and the fates of their opponents. Moses is mentioned first, with a specific detail: God gave him the Book (*al-kitāb*) and appointed his brother Aaron (*Hārūn*) as his helper (*wazīr*). The mention of Moses is not a digression; it is the sūrah's way of grounding the Prophet Muhammad's experience in the longer history of revelation. The disbelievers of Makkah are not the first to oppose a messenger; they are the latest in a line that stretches back to Moses and beyond.

The word *wazīr* (helper, minister) is the same word used in modern Arabic for a government minister, and its Qur'anic usage establishes the concept: a *wazīr* is one who bears the burden alongside the leader. In 20:29-32, Moses asks God to "appoint for me a minister from my family, Aaron my brother. Strengthen me through him and let him share my task." The request and the granting are recorded in 25:35 without elaboration, because the sūrah's purpose is not to retell the story but to establish the pattern: every prophet had help, and every prophet's help came from God.

**The Book as a Criterion**

The phrase *ātaynā Mūsā'l-kitāb* — "We gave Moses the Book" — refers to the Torah, which the Qur'an elsewhere calls *al-Furqān* (the Criterion). In 2:53 — "And when We gave Moses the Scripture and the Criterion, that you might be guided" — the Torah is given the same title as this sūrah. In 21:48 — "We gave Moses and Aaron the Criterion and a light and a reminder for the God-conscious" — the Criterion is paired with light and reminder. The connection is significant: Moses received the Criterion, and Muhammad ﷺ received the Criterion, and the two revelations serve the same function — to distinguish truth from falsehood, to provide guidance, to separate the believers from the disbelievers. The mention of Moses' Book in 25:35 is thus not merely historical; it is structural. The Criterion that names this sūrah is the same kind of revelation that Moses received, and the opposition it provokes is the same kind of opposition.

**The Verse's Role in the Prophetic Catalogue**

Verses 25:35-40 form a catalogue of prophets and the destruction of their enemies. Moses and Aaron are mentioned in 25:35-36, Noah in 25:37, ʿĀd and Thamūd and the people of the Water-pit in 25:38-39, and the people of Lot in 25:40. The catalogue answers the statement of 25:31 — "We have made enemies for every prophet from among the wicked" — by providing specific examples. Each example follows the same pattern: a prophet was sent, the people rejected him, and God destroyed the rejecters. The pattern is the sūrah's final argument against the disbelievers of Makkah: the history of prophetic opposition is also a history of prophetic vindication. The prophets were opposed, but the prophets survived; the enemies were powerful, but the enemies were destroyed. The disbelievers of Makkah are invited to place themselves in this history and decide which role they are playing.

The mention of Aaron as Moses' helper also serves the Prophet Muhammad indirectly. The disbelievers mocked the Prophet for being an ordinary man (25:7); Moses, the greatest prophet in the Qur'an's telling, was also an ordinary man who needed a helper. The fact that God gave Moses a helper does not diminish Moses; it demonstrates that prophetic missions are carried out by human beings within human limitations, supported by God through human means. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not have a human helper in the same sense — he was both the messenger and the community builder — but the principle is the same: the mission does not require the absence of human need; it requires the presence of divine support.

**The Book and the Helper: Moses' Prophetic Model**

The pairing of the Book and the helper in 25:35 establishes a model of prophetic ministry that the Qur'an applies to Muhammad as well. Moses received the Torah (the Book) and Aaron (the helper); Muhammad received the Quran (the Criterion) and, in a different sense, the community of believers (the helpers). The two elements — revelation and support — are both necessary. A revelation without a community to carry it is a voice in the wilderness; a community without a revelation to guide it is a crowd without direction. Moses needed Aaron to speak on his behalf (20:29-34: "My brother Aaron is more eloquent than me, so send him with me as a helper"); Muhammad needed the companions to memorize, practice, and transmit the Quran. The difference is that Moses' helper was singular and named, while Muhammad's helpers were plural and collective — the entire *ummah* served the function that Aaron served for Moses. This comparison is not made explicit in 25:35, but it is the structural implication: the sūrah's mention of Moses' Book and helper is a mirror in which the reader can see the Prophet Muhammad's own situation reflected. The prophetic model is consistent: every prophet receives a revelation and a helper, and the helper's role is to support the prophet in delivering the revelation to the people. The specific form of the help changes; the principle does not.
 The mention of the Book and the helper also establishes a principle about the prophetic mission that applies beyond Moses. The Book is the message; the helper is the support. Every prophetic mission requires both, and the absence of either creates a deficiency that God remedies. Moses needed the Book to guide his people and the helper to support his delivery of the Book; Muhammad needed the Qur’an to guide his community and the companions to support his establishment of the community. The structural parallel reflects the Qur’an’s consistent presentation of the prophetic mission as a single institution with different instantiations.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:36

> We had ordered ˹them˺, “Go to the people who would deny Our signs.” Then We annihilated the deniers entirely

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command and Its Execution**

The verse compresses the essential structure of the prophetic mission into a single sentence: a command, an audience, and a consequence. *Fa-qulnā'dhhabā ilā'l-qawmi'lladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — "So We said, 'Go to the people who denied Our signs.'" The command is given in the dual form, addressed to Moses and Aaron together, which is consistent with the Qur'anic account in 20:42 and 26:12-16 where both are commissioned. The object of the command is specific: go to the people who denied Our signs. The people in question are the Egyptians under Pharaoh, who rejected the evidentiary signs (*āyāt*) that Moses brought — the staff, the leprous hand, the plagues, the parting of the sea.

The word *āyāt* (signs, verses) is deliberately ambiguous in the Qur'an: it refers both to the miraculous signs given to prophets and to the verses of revelation itself. In 25:36, it refers to the miraculous signs, but the sūrah's reader has been trained by this point to hear the echo: the disbelievers of Makkah have also denied the signs — the signs of the Quran's divine origin, the signs in creation, the signs in the fate of earlier nations. The dual meaning makes the verse a bridge between Moses' situation and Muhammad's: the same charge — denial of signs — and the same consequence — destruction — apply across both contexts.

**Dammarnā: The Word for Complete Destruction**

The verse's final clause — *fa-dammarnāhum tadbīrān* — "Then We destroyed them completely" — uses the verb *dammara*, which means to annihilate, to destroy utterly, to bring to total ruin. The same verb appears in 7:137, 17:16, 26:172, 27:51, 37:136, and 47:10, each time describing God's complete destruction of a people who rejected His messenger. The word *tadbīr* intensifies it: the destruction was thorough, systematic, and total. Nothing was left. The Egyptians who denied Moses' signs were not merely defeated; they were erased.

Al-Qurṭubī notes the connection between the command to "go to" the deniers and the destruction that followed. The prophets did not destroy their enemies; God did. The prophets' role was to deliver the message and to stand their ground; the consequence was God's alone. This division of labor is consistent across the Qur'an's prophetic narratives: the messenger warns, the people reject, and God executes the judgment. The prophets are not warriors; they are witnesses. Their presence among the people is the final warning, and when the warning is rejected, the consequence follows — not from the messenger's hand but from God's.

**The Bridge to the Broader Catalogue**

Verse 25:36 closes the Moses episode and opens the way for the broader catalogue that follows. The pattern established in 25:35-36 — a prophet was sent, the people denied, God destroyed them — will be repeated for Noah (25:37), ʿĀd and Thamūd (25:38), and the people of Lot (25:40). The repetition is deliberate: each repetition reinforces the same principle, and the accumulation of examples makes the principle inescapable. The disbelievers of Makkah are not facing a novel situation; they are facing the same situation that has played out dozens of times in human history, and the outcome has always been the same. The catalogue is the sūrah's way of saying: look at the pattern, see where it leads, and decide before the consequence arrives.

**The Dual Form and Its Implication**

The command in 25:36 — "Go to the people who denied Our signs" — is given in the dual form (*dhahabā*), addressed to Moses and Aaron together. This detail, though grammatically minor, carries a narrative significance that al-Qurṭubī highlights. In some Qur'anic passages, the command to confront Pharaoh is addressed to Moses alone (20:24; 26:16); in others, it is addressed to both brothers (20:42; 26:12-16). The variation reflects the evolving composition of the prophetic mission: Moses was the primary messenger, and Aaron was appointed as his helper after Moses requested assistance. The dual form in 25:36 records the state of the mission after the appointment — both brothers, working together, confronting the deniers. The phrase "the people who denied Our signs" identifies the target not by ethnicity (the Egyptians) but by behavior (denial of signs). The same behavior is what defines the disbelievers of Makkah, and the sūrah's use of the behavioral description rather than the ethnic name makes the parallel explicit. The deniers of Moses' signs and the deniers of Muhammad's signs are the same kind of people, distinguished by the same act — the refusal to recognize the divine origin of the evidence presented to them. The destruction that followed the denial in Moses' case is the warning for Muhammad's case, and the warning is the reason the story is told.

**Dammara: Total Annihilation as Divine Response**

The word dammara (destroyed, annihilated) in 25:36 names the specific form of divine intervention that followed the denial. The destruction of the Egyptians under Pharaoh is described in the Qur'an as a series of plagues, the parting of the sea, and the drowning of Pharaoh and his army. The word tadbīrān (utterly, completely) intensifies the destruction: it was not partial but total, not selective but comprehensive. The Egyptians who denied Moses' signs were not merely defeated; they were erased as a political and military power. The disbelievers of Makkah, who also denied the signs of revelation, are invited by the sūrah's structure to consider whether they wish to share the fate of a civilization that once dominated the known world and is now known only through the account of its ruin. The pattern is consistent across every prophetic story in the Qur'an: denial produces consequence, and the consequence is proportional to the denial.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:37

> And when the people of Noah rejected the messengers, We drowned them, making them an example to humanity. And We have prepared a painful punishment for the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah: The First and the Universal**

The verse introduces Noah into the catalogue with a detail that goes beyond the Moses episode: the people of Noah rejected not one messenger but "the messengers" (*al-mursalīn*), in the plural. This has puzzled some commentators, since Noah is typically understood as the first messenger in the Qur'anic narrative. Al-Qurṭubī explains that the plural is used because rejecting one messenger is equivalent to rejecting all of them — the message is one, the source is one, and the principle of rejection is universal. Others hold that Noah's people rejected the messengers in the sense that they rejected the principle of messengership itself, refusing to accept that God sends anyone to guide humanity. In either case, the plural intensifies the charge: the people of Noah did not merely reject a man; they rejected the entire institution of prophetic guidance.

Noah occupies a unique position in the Qur'anic narrative as the first prophet to be sent to a community that had fallen into idolatry (7:59). His story is told at greater length than any other prophet's in the Qur'an — it appears in full or in part in at least seventeen sūrahs — and his experience establishes the template for every subsequent prophet: a righteous man is sent to a corrupt people, the people reject him, and God intervenes with punishment. The mention of Noah in 25:37 invokes this template and applies it to the Prophet Muhammad's situation: the disbelievers of Makkah are standing in the position of Noah's people, and the consequence is the same.

**Drowning as a Sign for Humanity**

The phrase *fa-aghraqnāhum wa jaʿalnāhum li'l-nāsi āyah* — "We drowned them and made them a sign for humanity" — specifies two outcomes: destruction and lesson. The drowning is the physical consequence; the *āyah* (sign) is the moral consequence. Noah's people were destroyed not merely as punishment but as evidence — visible proof, preserved in the landscape and in the collective memory, that rejection of God's messenger leads to ruin. The word *āyah* (sign) is the same word used for the miraculous signs given to prophets and for the verses of revelation itself. The drowned people become a verse of God's book written in the landscape: anyone who passes through the area where Noah's people lived can read the consequence.

This use of historical destruction as a sign appears throughout the Qur'an. In 15:79 — "So We took retribution from them, and both are on a clear highway" — the ruins of the destroyed cities are situated on a road that travelers pass. In 29:40 — "We seized each people for their sin" — the punishment is linked to specific places that still exist. The sūrah itself will use this device in 25:40, where the disbelievers are reminded that they have passed by the ruins of Sodom. The pattern is consistent: God destroys, preserves the ruins, and invites the living to reflect on what the ruins mean. The people of Noah are the first entry in this catalogue of ruins, and the lesson they teach is the same lesson every entry teaches: rejection has consequences, and the consequences are visible to anyone willing to look.

**The Recurring Phrase: A Painful Punishment for the Wrongdoers**

The verse ends with *wa aʿtadnā li'ẓ-ẓālimīna ʿadhāban alīmā* — "And We have prepared a painful punishment for the wrongdoers." This phrase, or close variants of it, appears multiple times in the sūrah and across the Qur'an as a refrain. Its repetition serves a dual purpose: for the prophets' enemies of the past, it confirms the outcome — they were punished; for the disbelievers of the present, it warns of the same outcome approaching. The word *aʿtadnā* (We have prepared) uses the past tense, as in 25:11, giving the punishment the quality of readiness. It is not being assembled; it exists, waiting for those who join the ranks of the wrongdoers by repeating the pattern the catalogue describes.

**Noah as the Archetypal Rejected Prophet**

Noah's placement in the catalogue immediately after Moses serves a specific argumentative function. Moses is the prophet most respected by the Quraysh's Jewish and Christian neighbors; Noah is the prophet whose story is the most universal. Every community in the Abrahamic tradition acknowledges Noah, and his flood narrative appears in Mesopotamian, biblical, and Qur'anic sources. By mentioning Noah, the sūrah extends the prophetic catalogue beyond the Arabian context and into the shared human past. The destruction of Noah's people was not a local event but a global one — the flood covered the earth and erased an entire civilization. The scale of the destruction matches the scale of the rejection: the people of Noah denied the principle of prophetic guidance, and the consequence was proportional. Al-Rāzī notes that the mention of Noah in 25:37 also serves as a reminder that the disbelievers of Makkah, despite their sense of uniqueness, were repeating a pattern that is as old as humanity itself. The first prophet was rejected, and every prophet since has been rejected, and the consequence has always been the same. The novelty the Makkans imagine in their opposition — their clever arguments, their social pressure, their mockery — is an illusion. They are doing what has been done before, and the outcome will be what it has always been.
 The phrase āyah li’l-nās (a sign for humanity) specifies that the destruction of Noah’s people was intended not only as punishment but as pedagogy. The flood was a lesson preserved in the landscape and in the collective memory of humanity — a visible reminder that the rejection of God’s messenger produces consequences that extend beyond the individuals who reject. The word āyah (sign) places the destruction in the same category as the miraculous signs given to prophets and the verses of revelation itself. The flood is a verse of God’s book written in the history of the world, and anyone who reflects on it can read the message: the One who destroyed the first generation of rejecters will destroy the last.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:38

> Also ˹We destroyed˺ ’Ȃd, Thamûd, and the people of the Water-pit, as well as many peoples in between

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arab Catastrophes: Destructions Within Living Memory**

The verse names three destroyed peoples — ʿĀd, Thamūd, and the people of the Water-pit (*al-Rass*) — and adds "many peoples in between." The first two are central to the Qur'an's prophetic geography because their ruins were visible in the Arabian Peninsula. The ʿĀd, the people of the prophet Hūd, lived in the region of al-Aḥqāf in southern Arabia and were destroyed by a devastating wind (41:16; 69:6-7; 89:6-7). The Thamūd, the people of the prophet Ṣāliḥ, lived in the region of al-Ḥijr (modern Mada'in Ṣāliḥ in northwestern Saudi Arabia) and were destroyed by an earthquake and a blast (7:78; 11:67; 15:80-83). Both peoples left physical ruins that the Quraysh knew — the Thamūd's carved rock dwellings were visible on the trade routes to Syria — and the Qur'an repeatedly invites its audience to visit these ruins and learn from them (15:84; 29:38; 46:25).

The mention of these peoples in 25:38 is not merely historical; it is geographic. The disbelievers of Makkah lived in a landscape scarred by divine punishment. The ruins of the ʿĀd and the Thamūd were not distant legends; they were features of the physical world the Quraysh moved through. The sūrah's catalogue of destroyed peoples is a map of the Arabian Peninsula drawn in consequence: north, the ruins of Thamūd; south, the ruins of ʿĀd; and the disbelievers sit in the middle, on the same land, under the same sky, making the same choices.

**Al-Rass: The People of the Water-Pit**

The identity of the people of al-Rass is uncertain, and the commentators acknowledge this directly. Al-Rāzī lists several possibilities: some scholars say the name refers to a geographical location, others that it refers to the act of throwing their prophet into a well or pit (*rass* can mean "well" or "pit"). The people of al-Rass are also mentioned in 50:12 alongside the ʿĀd, Pharaoh, and the brethren of Lot, suggesting they were a well-known destroyed community in the Qur'anic audience's frame of reference. The Qur'an does not elaborate on their story, and the commentators who attempt identification acknowledge that the details are not ascertainable. What is certain is the pattern: a prophet was sent, the people rejected him, and they were destroyed. The identity of the prophet and the exact location are less important than the principle.

**"Many Peoples in Between"**

The phrase *wa quranan bayna dhālika kathīrān* — "and many peoples in between" — expands the catalogue beyond the named examples. The destruction of communities that rejected their prophets was not limited to a few dramatic cases; it was a recurring feature of human history. The word *quran* (generations, peoples) suggests that entire generations were erased — not just individuals but communities, cultures, civilizations. The phrase "in between" implies that the destructions were distributed across geography and time, covering the whole of the inhabited world. The catalogue is therefore not exhaustive but representative: the named examples are the ones the audience would recognize, and the unnamed ones are the ones that history has forgotten but God has not.

The effect of the phrase is to make the pattern universal. The disbelievers of Makkah might be tempted to think that the destruction of the ʿĀd and the Thamūd was exceptional — a few extreme cases that do not apply to them. The phrase "many peoples in between" closes that escape: the destruction was not exceptional but normal. Every community that rejected its messenger was destroyed, and the unnamed multitude is the evidence. The catalogue's message is cumulative: the more examples that are cited, the more certain the pattern becomes, and the more certain the pattern becomes, the more inevitable the consequence for those who repeat it.

**The Geography of Punishment in the Arabian Landscape**

The mention of ʿĀd, Thamūd, and the people of the Water-pit in 25:38 anchors the sūrah's eschatological argument in physical geography. The ruins of these destroyed communities were not hidden; they were landmarks on the trade routes that the Quraysh traveled regularly. The Thamūd's carved rock dwellings at al-Ḥijr were visible on the road to Syria; the ʿĀd's territory in al-Aḥqāf was known in southern Arabia. The Qur'an uses these ruins as evidence — not the kind of evidence the disbelievers demanded (angels, treasures, visible signs) but the kind they already had and refused to interpret. The ruins were a message written in stone: this is what happens to communities that reject their prophets. The disbelievers' failure to read the message was not a failure of intelligence but a failure of expectation — they did not expect the resurrection (25:40), and without that expectation, the ruins were curiosities rather than warnings. The phrase "many peoples in between" expands the catalogue beyond the named examples, suggesting that the Arabian landscape is littered with the remains of destroyed communities, most of which are forgotten by history but known to God. The sūrah's catalogue is therefore not exhaustive but representative: the named examples are the ones the audience can verify by visiting the ruins; the unnamed ones are the ones that God knows and the audience does not. The argument is cumulative: the more destructions that are named, the more certain the pattern becomes, and the more certain the pattern becomes, the more inevitable the consequence for those who repeat it.
 The inclusion of a community whose remains are uncertain strengthens the sūrah’s argument by suggesting that the known examples are only a fraction of the total. The pattern of prophetic rejection and divine punishment was not limited to a few dramatic cases; it was a recurring feature of human history, distributed across geography and time, encompassing communities that history remembers and communities that history has forgotten. The sūrah’s catalogue is therefore not a complete list but a representative sample, and the sample is sufficient to establish the pattern beyond reasonable doubt.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:39

> For each We set forth ˹various˺ lessons, and We ultimately destroyed each

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pattern Distilled to Its Essence**

The verse reduces the entire catalogue of destroyed peoples to a single sentence: *wa kullanḍarabnā lahu'l-amthāla wa kullan tabbirnā tadbīrān* — "For each We set forth parables, and each We utterly destroyed." The word *mathal* (parable, lesson, example) refers to the warnings, the arguments, the evidentiary signs, and the prophetic messages that each community received before its destruction. The word *tabbīr* (utter destruction) is the intensive form of the same root used in 25:36 (*tadbīr*), and its repetition in the cognate construction (*tabbirnā tadbīrān*) intensifies the finality: the destruction was total, systematic, and complete.

The verse performs a compression: it takes the multi-verse catalogues of 25:35-38 and reduces them to a formula. The formula has two parts: (1) God provided each community with lessons (*amthāl*), and (2) God destroyed each community. The order is important: the lessons came first, and the destruction came after. The destruction was not arbitrary; it was the consequence of ignoring the lessons. The *amthāl* included the prophets' warnings, the miraculous signs, the physical evidence of God's power in creation, and the historical ruins of previously destroyed communities. Every possible form of guidance was offered, and every form was refused. The destruction that followed was therefore not a punishment inflicted on the innocent but a consequence accepted by the guilty.

**The Word Amthāl: Parables as Warnings**

The word *amthāl* in 25:39 does not mean "parables" in the literary sense — short stories with moral lessons — but something broader: examples, comparisons, warnings drawn from experience. Al-Ṭabarī glosses the word as *ibrar wa ḥujaj* — "admonitions and proofs." Each destroyed community was given not only a prophet but also the means to understand the prophet's message: signs in nature, signs in their own history, signs in the fate of communities that had gone before them. The word *amthāl* encompasses all of these. God did not simply send a messenger and wait; He provided the evidence that the messenger's claim was true, and He provided it in forms the audience could recognize.

The use of *amthāl* connects this verse to 25:33, where the same word appears in the context of the disbelievers' arguments: "Whenever they bring you an argument (*mathal*), We come to you with the truth and a better explanation." In 25:33, the *mathal* is the disbelievers' argument against the Prophet; in 25:39, the *amthāl* are God's arguments against the disbelievers. The word is the same; the direction is reversed. What the disbelievers offer as rhetorical weapons against the Prophet, God offers as evidence against them. The symmetry is deliberate: every argument the disbelievers construct has already been answered, and every answer has been given in a form they can understand. The destruction of each community in the catalogue is the proof that the answers were sufficient.

**The Finality of Tabbīr**

The word *tabbīr* (utter destruction) appears here in its most emphatic form: *kullan tabbirnā tadbīrān* — "each We destroyed completely." The cognate accusative (*tabbirnā tadbīrān*) is a standard Arabic device for intensification: the action is not merely done but done thoroughly, done to the fullest extent possible. The destruction of each community in the catalogue was not partial — a city damaged, a population reduced, a leadership overthrown. It was total. The ʿĀd were annihilated by wind. The Thamūd were killed by an earthquake. The people of Noah were drowned. The people of Lot were stoned from above. In each case, the community ceased to exist. The word *tabbīr* names this total erasure, and its repetition across the catalogue is the sūrah's way of saying: the pattern is not gentle, and the consequence is not negotiable.

**The Formula of Warning and Destruction**

Verse 25:39 distills the sūrah's prophetic catalogue to its essential formula: God provided lessons (*amthāl*), and God destroyed (*tabbīr*). The formula has two verbs and no middle ground — there is no third option between receiving the lessons and being destroyed. This binary structure is the sūrah's most severe theological claim: there is no path that avoids both outcomes. The lessons are the prophets, the signs, the warnings, and the historical evidence of prior destructions. A person who receives these lessons and responds to them moves toward the Garden described in 25:15-16 and 25:24. A person who receives them and refuses them moves toward the fire described in 25:11-14. There is no neutral position, no middle state, no third destination. The word *amthāl* (lessons, parables) encompasses every form of divine communication: the Quran's verses, the prophets' words, the signs in creation, and the ruins of destroyed communities. All of these are *amthāl* — comparisons, examples, demonstrations — designed to teach, warn, and persuade. The destruction that follows their refusal is not arbitrary but logical: a community that rejects every form of guidance has removed itself from the possibility of guidance, and the destruction is the consequence of that removal. The formula in 25:39 is the sūrah's way of saying: the evidence was given, the choice was made, and the outcome was determined by the choice.
 The word mathal (parable, lesson) in 25:39 connects this verse to 25:33, where the same word appears in the context of the disbelievers’ arguments. In 25:33, the mathal is the disbelievers’ rhetorical challenge; in 25:39, the amthāl are God’s warnings to the destroyed communities. The word is the same, but the direction is reversed. The disbelievers construct amthāl to discredit the Prophet; God constructs amthāl to warn the disbelievers. The symmetry is deliberate: the sūrah is showing that the rhetorical tools the disbelievers use against the Prophet are the same tools God has used against every previous generation of rejecters.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:40

> They have certainly passed by the city ˹of Sodom˺, which had been showered with a dreadful rain ˹of stones˺. Have they not seen its ruins? But they do not expect to be resurrected

**Expanded Commentary**

**The City the Disbelievers Walked Past**

The verse addresses the disbelievers of Makkah directly, connecting the catalogue of destroyed peoples to their own experience: *wa la-qad ātaw ʿalā'l-qaryati'llatī umṭirat matra'r-saw'* — "And they have certainly passed by the city that was showered with a dreadful rain."
