The city is Sodom, the community of the prophet Lot, which was destroyed by a rain of stones (7:84; 11:82; 15:73-74; 26:173; 27:58; 29:34; 51:33-34). The Quraysh, in their trade journeys to Syria and the north, would have passed through or near the region where Lot's people lived, and the ruins would have been visible. The word *ātaw* (they have passed by) is in the past tense — they have already done this, they have already seen the ruins — and the question that follows — *a-falam yakūnū yarawnahā* — "Have they not seen it?" — assumes the answer is yes.

The rhetorical force is enormous. The disbelievers demand signs (25:7-8, 25:21), and the sūrah points out that they have already walked through the most vivid sign available: the ruins of a community that was destroyed for the same kind of rejection they are now committing. The ruins are not hidden; they are on the road. The evidence is not abstract; it is physical. The disbelievers do not need an angel to descend or a treasure to appear; they need only to look at what they have already seen and draw the conclusion. The verse's question — "Have they not seen it?" — is the sūrah's most direct challenge: the sign is already in front of you, and you have walked past it without understanding.

**Matra'r-Saw': The Rain of Stones**

The phrase *matra'r-saw'* ("a dreadful rain" or "an evil rain") refers to the rain of baked clay stones that destroyed Lot's people. In 11:82 — "We turned it upside down and rained upon them stones of baked clay" — the destruction is described in spatial terms: the city was inverted and then pelted from above. In 15:73-74 — "So the (mighty) Blast overtook them at sunrise, and We turned the cities upside down and We rained upon them stones of baked clay" — the blast and the rain are combined. The rain is not water but stone — specifically, stones of *sijjīl* (baked clay), a word that intensifies the horror: these are manufactured projectiles, not natural phenomena. The destruction of Lot's people was not a natural disaster; it was a targeted act of divine judgment.

Al-Ṭabarī connects the word *saw'* (evil, dreadful) with the nature of the rain: it was not ordinary rain that nourishes but rain that destroys, rain that falls with the force and the intent of a weapon. The same word appears in 27:58 — "And We rained upon them a rain, and evil was the rain of those who were warned" — where the adjective *saw'* is applied directly to the rain itself. The rain is "evil" not because of a moral quality but because of its effect: it killed, it destroyed, it erased.

**The Refusal to Expect Resurrection**

The verse ends with the statement *bal kānū lā yarjūna nushūrā* — "But they do not expect to be resurrected." The word *yarjūna* (expect, hope, anticipate) is the same word used in 25:21 — "Those who do not expect to meet Us." The repetition connects the verse to the sūrah's central diagnosis: the disbelievers' problem is not a lack of evidence but a lack of expectation. They have seen the ruins of Sodom; they have heard the Quran; they know the Prophet's character. But they do not expect the resurrection, and without that expectation, none of the evidence produces action. The ruins are a curiosity, not a warning. The Quran is a text, not a reckoning. The Prophet is a man, not a messenger. Everything depends on the expectation of the resurrection, and the expectation is precisely what the disbelievers refuse to have.

The word *nushūr* (resurrection) means a rising, an uprising, a bringing-forth from the dead. Its use at the end of this verse — and at the end of the prophetic catalogue — serves as a reminder that the pattern the sūrah has described (warning, rejection, destruction) is not complete without the final chapter: the resurrection. The communities of the past were destroyed in this world; the disbelievers of the present will face destruction in the next. The ruins of Sodom are a preview, not a conclusion. The conclusion comes on the Day when the dead are raised and every community gives account. The disbelievers who walked past the ruins without seeing them will, on that Day, see everything they refused to see — and the seeing will be the beginning of a consequence from which there is no escape.

**The Ruins as Evidence for the Resurrection**

The verse's final clause — "But they do not expect to be resurrected" — connects the ruins of Sodom to the sūrah's central theological claim: the denial of the Hour (25:11) is the root of every other error. The disbelievers pass by the ruins, see the physical evidence of divine punishment, and draw no conclusion because they do not expect the resurrection. If the resurrection were real to them, the ruins would be a warning; because it is not real, the ruins are a curiosity. The word *yarjūna* (expect, hope, anticipate) appears in 25:21 in the same context — "those who do not expect to meet Us" — and the repetition links 25:40 back to the diagnosis in 25:11. The sūrah has come full circle: it opened with the disbelievers' denial of the Hour, described their objections, recorded the punishment and the reward, catalogued the prophets and the destroyed communities, and now returns to the starting point — the denial of the resurrection. The circular structure is deliberate: the sūrah's argument is not linear but spiral, returning to the same point with increasing force. Each return adds a new layer of evidence: the first return (25:11) names the denial; the second (25:21) shows its consequences; the third (25:40) grounds it in physical reality. The ruins of Sodom are the final evidence, and the question — "Have they not seen it?" — is the final challenge. The evidence is before them; the choice is theirs; and the choice has consequences that the ruins themselves illustrate.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:41

> When they see you ˹O Prophet˺, they only make fun of you, ˹saying,˺ “Is this the one that Allah has sent as a messenger

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mockery as a Response to the Unfamiliar**

The verse describes a specific social behavior: the disbelievers see the Prophet ﷺ and immediately begin to ridicule him. The word *yahzarūnaka* (they mock you, they make fun of you) names the act precisely — it is not argument, not debate, not engagement with the message, but derision directed at the person. The mockers do not ask "Is this message true?" They ask "Is this the one God sent?" The question targets the messenger, not the message, and it assumes the answer is no. The grammar of the question — *a-hādhā'lladhī ba'atha'llāhu rasūlā* — is a rhetorical construction that expects rejection: the very phrasing implies that the person in front of them cannot possibly be the answer to the question of who God would send.

This pattern of mockery is not unique to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. The Qur'an records the same response from every prophetic community: "How many a prophet did We send to those of old! Yet never did a prophet come to them but they mocked him" (43:6-7). The mockery of Noah (26:116), Hud (26:141), Salih (26:141), Lot (26:165), and Shu'ayb (26:176) follows the same script — a righteous person arrives, and the community's first response is not reflection but ridicule. The recurrence of the pattern across prophetic history tells the reader something about the nature of the human response to divine guidance: the first instinct is not to examine but to dismiss, and the dismissal takes the form of mockery because mockery is the cheapest form of rejection. It costs nothing, requires no evidence, and produces immediate social solidarity among the mockers.

**The Social Function of Ridicule in Makkah**

The mockery described in 25:41 was not a private sentiment but a public performance. In Makkah's tribal society, social standing was everything, and the disbelievers' mockery of the Prophet served a specific social function: it signaled to others that allegiance to this man was socially unacceptable. The act of laughing at the Prophet in public was a way of enforcing the social boundary between the followers and the non-followers, making it clear that anyone who sided with Muhammad would be joining the object of ridicule rather than the group that ridicules him. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the disbelievers used mockery as a weapon because they could not use argument — the Quran's literary quality was beyond their ability to challenge, and the Prophet's personal integrity was beyond their ability to impugn. Mockery filled the gap left by the absence of a substantive objection.

The question "Is this the one that Allah has sent as a messenger?" is also a form of social gatekeeping. By framing the Prophet's mission as absurd, the mockers prevent others from taking it seriously. The question does not invite investigation; it forecloses it. The implied answer — "surely not" — becomes the default position of anyone who hears the question and does not want to be associated with the object of ridicule. The mockery is therefore not merely an insult to the Prophet; it is a strategy for maintaining the social order against the threat of his message.

**The Mockery of the Prophet's Followers**

Al-Qurṭubī connects this verse with the broader Qur'anic theme of the disbelievers' incredulity at God's choice of followers. In 11:27, the disbelievers say of Noah's followers, "We see that none follow you but the lowliest among us." In 46:11, the disbelievers say of the Muslims, "Had this Quran been anything good, they would not have preceded us to it." The pattern is consistent: the disbelievers judge the message by the social status of its followers, and because the early Muslims were predominantly poor and socially marginal, the message is dismissed as unworthy. The mockery in 25:41 targets the Prophet directly, but the same mockery extends to everyone who follows him. The social logic is circular: the message is rejected because its followers are lowly, and the followers are lowly because the message is rejected. The Qur'an breaks the circle by insisting that God's criteria for worthiness are not the same as the Quraysh's criteria, and that the lowly in this world may be the exalted in the next.

**The Echo Across Prophetic History**

The pattern of mockery the verse describes is not unique to the Prophet Muhammad. Every prophet who brought an uncomfortable truth was met with the same response. The Qur'an records this with such consistency that mockery becomes, paradoxically, one of the signs of authentic prophethood. A messenger who is not mocked is a messenger who does not threaten the existing order, and a messenger who does not threaten the existing order is not delivering a message from God. The mockery is therefore not evidence against the Prophet but evidence for him: it confirms that his message has reached its target and provoked the response that every divine message provokes. The disbelievers of Makkah, in their mockery, are doing what every previous generation of rejecters did, and the sūrah records their mockery alongside the mockery of Noah’s people, ‘Ād’s people, and every other community that rejected its prophet. The catalogue of mockers is the sūrah’s way of saying: you are not the first, and you will not be the last, and the outcome has always been the same.

**The Verse’s Role in the Sūrah’s Structure**

Verse 25:41 opens the second cycle of disbelievers’ objections in the sūrah. The first cycle (25:4-8) focused on the Quran and the Prophet’s credentials; the second cycle (25:41-44) focuses on the social dynamics of rejection. The shift is from theology to sociology: the disbelievers’ objections are no longer about the message’s content but about the messenger’s social acceptability. The mockery in 25:41 is a social act, performed in public, designed to enforce the boundary between the followers and the non-followers. The sūrah’s placement of this social dynamic after the catalogue of natural signs (25:45-50) and before the eschatological warnings (25:11-19) serves a specific purpose: it shows that the disbelievers’ rejection is not based on evidence but on social pressure. The signs are there; the evidence is clear; and the disbelievers reject it not because it is insufficient but because accepting it would cost them their social standing. The mockery is the price they charge for acceptance, and the price is the evidence of their priorities.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:42

> He would have almost tricked us away from our gods, had we not been so devoted to them.” ˹But˺ soon they will know, when they face the punishment, who is far astray from the ˹Right˺ Way

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Disbelievers' Self-Diagnosis**

The verse records the disbelievers' own assessment of the Prophet's effect on them: *la-qad arādanā ʿan ālihatinā law-lā an ṣabarnā ʿalayhā* — "He would have nearly turned us away from our gods, had we not been steadfast upon them." The confession is remarkable because it acknowledges the Prophet's power. The disbelievers are not saying the message is weak or the Prophet is unconvincing; they are saying the opposite — he nearly succeeded. The word *arāda* (to intend, to desire, to nearly cause) conveys the sense of a near-miss: the Prophet came close to achieving what he set out to do, and only the disbelievers' stubbornness prevented it.

The phrase *ṣabarnā ʿalayhā* — "we were steadfast upon them" — uses the word *sabr* (patience, steadfastness) in an ironic context. *Ṣabr* is the quality the Qur'an demands of the believers (25:20: "Will you not then be patient?"), and the disbelievers are here applying the same word to their attachment to their idols. They are patient — not in the face of suffering or temptation, but in the face of the truth. The misuse of *ṣabr* is part of the verse's irony: the quality that produces salvation in the believers produces ruin in the disbelievers, because it is applied to the wrong object. The disbelievers are steadfast in the service of idols; the believers are steadfast in the service of God. The same word, the same effort, and opposite results.

**The Punishment as the Judge**

The second half of the verse — *sa-yaʿlamūna ʿinda ra'yati'l-ʿadhābi man aḍallu sabīlā* — shifts to the future: "They will know, when they face the punishment, who is farther astray from the Way." The punishment itself will serve as the judge. The disbelievers believed they were on the right path; the believers believed the disbelievers were on the wrong path. The disagreement cannot be resolved in this life because both sides claim the same thing: that the other is astray. The verse resolves the disagreement by pointing to the punishment as the final arbiter. When the punishment arrives, it will settle the question not by argument but by demonstration. The one who is astray will be the one who suffers, and the suffering will be the proof.

The word *sabīl* (Way, path) recurs in this verse for the eleventh time in the sūrah, maintaining the thread that runs from 25:9 (the disbelievers who "could not find the Way") through 25:27 (the wrongdoer who wishes he "had taken a Way with the Messenger") to 25:34 (those who are "farthest from the Right Way"). The repetition of *sabīl* across the sūrah creates a continuous argument: the Way was offered, the Way was refused, and the punishment will reveal who was on the Way and who was not. The disbelievers in 25:42 claim steadfastness; the punishment will show that their steadfastness was the very thing that kept them from the Way.

**The Imminence of the Warning**

The word *sa-ʿarafūna* or *sa-yaʿlamūna* ("they will soon know") uses the prefix *sa-*, which in Arabic indicates the near future — something that will happen soon, imminently, without delay. The Qur'an does not say "they will eventually know" or "they will one day know"; it says they will soon know. The imminence is part of the warning: the punishment is not distant, not theoretical, not reserved for a far-off Day. It is approaching, and the approach is measured in the present tense. This urgency appears across the Qur'an's warning passages: "The Hour has drawn near" (54:1); "The reckoning of the people has drawn near, yet they are in heedlessness, turning away" (21:1). The disbelievers of Makkah live in the interval between the warning and its fulfillment, and the interval is shorter than they think.

**The Disbelievers’ Use of Sabr**

The disbelievers’ claim that they were “steadfast” upon their gods is one of the sūrah’s sharpest ironies. The word sabr (patience, steadfastness) is the quality the Qur’an most consistently demands of the believers. In 25:20 the Prophet is asked, “Will you not then be patient?” (25:20) In 31:17 the command is, “Be patient, for the promise of Allah is true.” In 46:35 the instruction is, “So be patient, as the messengers of strong resolve were patient.” The disbelievers in 25:42 use the same word for their attachment to idols. They are patient — not in the face of hardship or temptation, but in the face of the truth. The misuse of sabr reveals the moral neutrality of the virtue itself: patience is valuable only when it is applied to the right object. Patient attachment to falsehood is not a virtue but a vice, and the disbelievers’ steadfastness in the service of idols is the mirror image of the believers’ steadfastness in the service of God. The same word, the same effort, and opposite outcomes.

**The Knowledge That Comes Too Late**

The verse’s closing promise — “They will know, when they face the punishment, who is farther astray” — names a knowledge that arrives after the window for action has closed. The disbelievers will know the truth when the punishment comes, but the knowledge will not help them because the punishment will already be in progress. This is the Qur’an’s most severe form of eschatological irony: the truth is revealed at the moment when it can no longer be acted upon. The disbelievers who demanded evidence in life will receive the most conclusive evidence possible — the punishment itself — and the evidence will confirm what they denied. The knowledge is therefore not salvific but condemnatory: it adds the weight of understanding to the weight of suffering. The disbelievers will suffer and understand why they suffer, and the understanding will make the suffering worse. This is the meaning of the phrase “who is farther astray”: the punishment will settle the question the disbelievers raised in life, and the settlement will be final.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:43

> Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ the one who has taken their own desires as their god? Will you then be a keeper over them

**Expanded Commentary**

**Desire as a False God**

The verse poses a rhetorical question that redefines idolatry: *a-ra'ayta mani ittakhadhha ilāhahu hawāhu* — "Have you considered the one who has made desire his god?" The word *hawā* (desire, passion, caprice) refers to the inclinations of the lower self — the pull toward comfort, pleasure, status, and self-gratification that competes with the pull of divine guidance. By calling *hawā* a god (*ilāh*), the verse performs a theological reclassification. The disbelievers who worship idols are, in this verse, revealed to be worshipping something deeper than wood and stone: they are worshipping themselves — specifically, the part of themselves that resists submission to anything greater than its own appetites.

The redefinition is not metaphorical. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same move: "Have you seen the one who takes his desire as his god, and God leads him astray knowingly" (45:23). In that verse, the connection between desire-as-god and divine misguidance is explicit: the person who worships desire is allowed by God to follow it to its logical end, and the end is misguidance. The connection in 25:43 is subtler but identical in structure. The disbelievers' attachment to their idols is not a reasoned theological commitment; it is an expression of desire — the desire to maintain their social order, their habits, their comfort, their identity. The idols are the visible form of the desire; the desire is the real object of worship.

**The Prophet's Role: Messenger, Not Warden**

The second half of the verse — *a-fanta lahum bi-wakīl* — asks the Prophet whether he intends to be a "guardian" or "keeper" over the disbelievers. The word *wakīl* means a guardian, a trustee, a responsible caretaker. The question is a rebuke and a release simultaneously. The Prophet is not responsible for the disbelievers' choices. He has delivered the message; the message has been received; and the disbelievers have responded by worshipping their own desires. The Prophet's duty ends with delivery. He is not their warden; he is their warner. The distinction is crucial: a warden manages and controls; a warner informs and departs. The Prophet's grief over his people's rejection (25:30) was genuine, but the grief does not create a responsibility to force them to accept what they refuse.

The same rebuke appears in 88:21-22: "So remind, for you are only a reminder; you are not a warder over them." The Prophet's role is defined by its limits: he delivers the message, and God handles the consequences. The disbelievers who have taken desire as their god are beyond the Prophet's reach — not because the message is insufficient, but because their attachment to desire has closed the door that the message was designed to open. The verse's question — "Will you then be a keeper over them?" — is God's way of telling the Prophet to let go: the responsibility for their guidance was never his, and the responsibility for their misguidance is not his either.

**The Deepening Definition of Idolatry in the Sūrah**

The verse adds a layer to the sūrah's evolving definition of idolatry. In 25:3, the false gods were described as things that cannot create, cannot protect, and cannot govern life and death. In 25:17-19, the false gods will testify against their worshippers. In 25:43, the false god is revealed to be the worshipper's own desire — an internal idol, harder to see than a stone statue and harder to destroy. The progression from external idols to internal desire is the sūrah's way of deepening the reader's understanding of what *shirk* (associating others with God) really means. It is not merely the worship of a physical object; it is the elevation of anything — including one's own appetites — to the position of ultimate authority. The person who follows desire without submitting it to divine guidance has made desire their god, and the god they worship is more dangerous than any idol because it lives inside them, speaks in their own voice, and disguises its commands as their own thoughts.

**The Prophetic Rejection of Wardenship**

The command to the Prophet in the second half of 25:43 — “Will you then be a keeper over them?” (25:43) — defines the limits of prophetic responsibility. The Prophet’s role is to deliver the message, not to enforce compliance. He is a warner, not a warden; a messenger, not a manager. The distinction is crucial because it removes the burden of the disbelievers’ rejection from the Prophet’s shoulders. He has delivered the message; the message has been heard; and the disbelievers have chosen to follow their desires instead. The choice is theirs, and the consequence is theirs. The same principle appears in 88:21-22: “So remind, for you are only a reminder; you are not a warder over them.” (88:22) (88:22) The Prophet’s grief over his people’s rejection (25:30) was real, but the grief does not create a responsibility to force them to accept what they refuse. The Prophet’s duty ends with delivery, and the delivery is complete. The question “Will you then be a keeper over them?” (25:43) is God’s way of releasing the Prophet from a responsibility that was never his.

**The Connection to the Sūrah’s Earlier Definition of Idolatry**

The verse’s redefinition of idolatry as the worship of desire connects to the sūrah’s earlier definition in 25:3, where the false gods were described as things that cannot create, cannot protect, and cannot govern life and death. Desire meets the same description: it cannot create anything of lasting value, it cannot protect the one who serves it, and it has no authority over life, death, or resurrection. The person who serves desire is serving something as powerless as the idols of wood and stone, and the service is therefore as futile. The sūrah’s progression from external idols (25:3) to internal desire (25:43) deepens the reader’s understanding of what shirk really means: it is not merely the worship of a physical object but the elevation of anything to the position of ultimate authority. The idol that lives inside is harder to see than the idol that stands in a temple, and it is harder to destroy because it speaks in the voice of the one who serves it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:44

> Or do you think that most of them listen or understand? They are only like cattle—no, more than that, they are astray from the ˹Right˺ Way

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal to Listen and the Refusal to Understand**

The verse addresses the Prophet with another rhetorical question: *am taḥsabu anna aktharahum yasma'ūna aw ya'qilūna* — "Or do you think that most of them listen or understand?" The question expects a negative answer: no, they do not listen, and no, they do not understand. The two verbs — *yasma'ūna* (to hear, to listen) and *ya'qilūna* (to reason, to understand) — cover the two stages of comprehension. First, the sound must enter the ear; then, the mind must process it into meaning. The disbelievers fail at both. They do not hear the Quran's message — not because their ears are blocked but because their attention is elsewhere, fixed on desire (25:43), on social standing (25:41), on the preservation of their inherited customs (25:42). And even when the words do reach them, they do not reason their way to understanding — not because their minds are deficient but because understanding would require them to act on what they understand, and action would require them to change.

The distinction between hearing and understanding is important because it addresses a common assumption: that if people just heard the message, they would believe. The Qur'an's position is that hearing is not sufficient; understanding is required, and understanding requires a disposition that the disbelievers lack. The deficiency is not auditory but volitional. They hear the sounds; they do not hear the meaning. They process the words; they do not process the implications. The failure is not in the information but in the receiver.

**The Cattle Comparison and Its Limitations**

The comparison — *innahum illā ka'l-an'ām* — "They are only like cattle" — is one of the Qur'an's harshest judgments. The same comparison appears in 7:179 — "They are like cattle; rather, they are more astray" — and in 25:44 the same intensifier appears: *bal hum aḍallu sabīlā* — "rather, they are more astray from the Way." The comparison is not to insult but to diagnose. Cattle follow their instincts — they eat, drink, sleep, and reproduce, driven by appetite and habit. The disbelievers do the same: they eat, drink, accumulate wealth, and reproduce, driven by desire and custom. The parallel is precise: both operate at the level of appetite, without submitting their appetites to a higher purpose.

But the verse does not stop at the comparison; it exceeds it: *bal hum aḍallu sabīlā* — "rather, they are more astray." Cattle are not condemned for following instinct because they have no capacity for higher reasoning. Human beings do. The disbelievers have the capacity to listen, to reason, to understand, and to choose — and they have chosen not to use it. This makes their condition worse than that of cattle, because the failure is not a limitation of nature but a misuse of capacity. Cattle cannot be astray because they have no path to follow; human beings can be astray because they have a path and have chosen to leave it. Al-Zamakhsharī observes that cattle know what is good and bad for them — they seek food and avoid danger — whereas some human beings fail to recognize what is good for their souls and what is harmful. The comparison therefore cuts in two directions: the disbelievers are like cattle in their focus on appetite, and they are worse than cattle in their failure to use the faculties that distinguish them from animals.

**The Word Sabīl and the Sūrah's Persistent Thread**

The phrase *aḍallu sabīlā* — "more astray from the Way" — repeats the sūrah's central motif for the twelfth time. The word *sabīl* (Way, path) has appeared in 25:9, 25:10, 25:27, 25:34, 25:42, and now 25:44, each time in the context of guidance either offered or refused. The repetition is not decorative; it is the sūrah's way of insisting that there is a Way, that the Way is available, and that the disbelievers' condition is not a matter of fate but of choice. Cattle do not have a Way; they have instinct. Human beings have a Way — the Criterion (25:1), the Messenger (25:56), the Reminder (25:29) — and the refusal to walk it is the defining error the sūrah describes. The comparison to cattle is therefore not a final judgment but an intermediate one: the disbelievers are behaving like animals, but they are not animals, and the Day of Judgment will hold them to the standard of the faculties they refused to use.

**The Capacity That Makes the Comparison Devastating**

The comparison to cattle would be merely insulting if it were not also accurate. The disbelievers’ behavior — eating, drinking, accumulating, reproducing, and ignoring the larger questions of existence — is structurally identical to the behavior of livestock. But the comparison is devastating because human beings are not cattle. Cattle have no capacity for abstract thought, no ability to contemplate the afterlife, no faculty for recognizing the divine signs in the natural world. Human beings have all of these, and the disbelievers’ refusal to use them is what makes their condition worse than that of animals. A cow that does not contemplate God is not guilty of anything; a human being who does not contemplate God is guilty of wasting the one faculty that distinguishes them from every other creature. The comparison in 25:44 is therefore not a dismissal of human dignity but a lament for its misuse. The disbelievers have the tools to find the Way; they have chosen not to use them; and the unused tools are the source of their condemnation. The sūrah's comparison is thus both a warning and an appeal: an appeal to use the faculties that distinguish human beings from every other creature in the created order.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:45

> Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shade—He could have simply made it ˹remain˺ still if He so willed—then We make the sun its guide

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shade as a Sign of Divine Sovereignty**

The verse begins a new section of the sūrah — a sustained meditation on natural phenomena as evidence of God's power and wisdom. The first sign is the shade (*ẓill*): *a-lam tara ilā Rabbika kayfa madda'ẓ-ẓill* — "Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shade?" The verb *madda* (to extend, to stretch out) describes the gradual movement of shadow across the earth as the sun moves through the sky. The shade is not static; it lengthens, shortens, and shifts direction throughout the day, responding to the sun's position. The verse invites the Prophet — and through him, the reader — to observe this daily phenomenon and recognize the divine hand behind it.

The phrase "He could have simply made it still if He so willed" introduces the concept of divine choice into the observation. God is not constrained by the mechanics of light and shadow; He could have made the shade permanent, unmoving, fixed in place. The fact that the shade moves — that it responds to the sun, that it changes throughout the day — is a choice, not a necessity. The movement serves a purpose: it marks time, it signals the passage of the day, and it provides relief from the sun's heat in varying degrees. If the shade were still, these functions would disappear. The movement is therefore a mercy, not a limitation — God chose to make the shade dynamic because dynamism serves the creatures who live beneath it.

**The Sun as a Guide**

The phrase *thumma ja'alnāhu shamsan qayidan* — "then We make the sun its guide" — describes the relationship between the sun and the shade. The sun guides the shade: as the sun moves, the shade follows. The word *qayid* (guide, rein, leash) implies a controlling relationship — the sun leads, and the shade is led. The metaphor is one of a rider and a mount: the sun holds the reins, and the shade goes where the sun directs. This relationship is a sign (*āyah*) because it demonstrates order in the natural world — the sun and the shade are not random; they are linked by a precise, predictable, mathematically describable relationship. The regularity of the relationship is itself evidence of design, and design is evidence of a designer.

The commentators offer additional layers of meaning. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the shade in this verse refers to the period from dawn to sunrise — the twilight between darkness and light — which gradually shortens as the sun rises and eventually disappears. The sun does not merely move the shade; it gradually erases it, replacing darkness with light in measured increments. The erasure is gentle, not sudden — the shade retreats step by step, not all at once. This gentleness is part of the sign: the transition from night to day is not a violent rupture but a gradual unfolding, and the gradual unfolding is a mercy to the creatures who need time to adjust.

**The Spiritual Allegory of Shade and Light**

Several commentators, including Ibn ʿAjībah, read verses 25:45-46 as a spiritual allegory. The shade represents the human soul — or every created thing — which is a shadow cast from the Unseen. The sun represents sacred knowledge, which illuminates the soul's true nature and causes the shadow of ignorance to retreat. The gradual withdrawal of the shade (25:46) represents the process of spiritual purification, in which the soul is drawn gradually toward God until it is annihilated (*fanā'*) in the divine light. The allegory is not the verse's primary meaning — the primary meaning is a sign in the natural world — but it demonstrates the Qur'an's capacity to operate on multiple levels simultaneously. The physical phenomenon (shade moving in response to the sun) points to a spiritual truth (the soul moving in response to divine knowledge), and both point to the same conclusion: everything in creation is guided by a power beyond itself.

**The Shade as a Metaphor for the Temporary**

The shade in 25:45 is, at one level, a physical phenomenon — the shadow cast by the earth as the sun moves across the sky. But the shade also carries a metaphorical weight that the Qur’an develops elsewhere. In 13:15 — “Whatever is in the heavens and earth prostrates to Allah, willingly or unwillingly, and their shadows in the morning and afternoon” — the shadows themselves are described as worshipping God. In 25:45-46, the shade is extended, guided by the sun, and then withdrawn toward God. The sequence — extension, guidance, withdrawal — mirrors the arc of every created thing: it comes into existence, it is guided through its term, and it returns to its source. The shade is temporary by nature; it exists only in relation to the light that casts it, and when the light changes, the shade changes with it. The human being is, in this reading, a shade — a temporary existence cast by a permanent light, destined to be withdrawn when the light shifts. The metaphor is not the verse’s primary meaning, but it enriches the observation and demonstrates the Qur’an’s capacity to operate on multiple registers simultaneously.

**The Universality of the Sign**

The shade is one of the most universal signs in the natural world. Every human being on earth experiences the movement of shadows — in the Arctic, in the tropics, in the desert, in the forest. The sign requires no telescope, no microscope, no specialized knowledge. It requires only the willingness to observe, and the observation leads to the conclusion the sūrah draws: the One who controls the shade controls everything. The universality of the sign matches the universality of the message: the Criterion (25:1) was sent to “the whole world,” and the sign that points to the Criterion is available to the whole world. The disbelievers who demand specialized signs — angels, treasures, visible miracles — are demanding something that only they can see. The shade is a sign that everyone can see, and the seeing is the invitation. The sūrah’s method is to replace the exclusive sign with the inclusive one, and the inclusive one is the more powerful because it speaks to every human being in every era.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:46

> causing the shade to retreat gradually

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gentle Withdrawal**

The verse is compressed to a single clause: *thumma qabadnāhu ilaynā qabḍan yasīrā* — "then We withdraw it to Ourselves, a gentle withdrawal." The word *qabadnā* (We seize, We withdraw) describes the act of pulling the shade back, and the pronoun *ilaynā* (to Us, toward Ourselves) names the direction: the shade is drawn toward God. The image is of a curtain being pulled back by an unseen hand — not torn away, not suddenly removed, but drawn gently, gradually, until what was hidden behind the shade is fully revealed. The word *yasīr* (gentle, easy, light) describes the quality of the withdrawal: it is not violent, not abrupt, not painful. The shade retreats step by step, and each step is barely noticeable. The transition from darkness to light, from shadow to illumination, happens so smoothly that the eye does not register the change until it is complete.

Al-Ṭabarī glosses *yasīr* as "quickly" or "in a hidden way," while al-Zamakhsharī reads it as "gradually." The two readings are not contradictory: the withdrawal is both gentle and imperceptible — it happens quickly enough that it seems sudden only in retrospect, and gradually enough that it does not disturb the creatures who depend on the shade. The dual quality — speed and gentleness — mirrors the Qur'an's description of divine action elsewhere: "His command, when He intends a thing, is only that He says to it 'Be!' and it is" (36:82). The withdrawal of the shade is a microcosm of creative power: God wills, and it happens — not with violence but with ease.

**The Connotation of the Hour**

Al-Rāzī observes that the word *yasīr* (easy, gentle) carries a secondary connotation related to the Hour — the end of the world. In 29:19 — "Do they not see how Allah originates creation and then reproduces it? That is easy for Allah" — the word *yasīr* describes God's power to create and re-create. In 50:44 — "The Day the earth breaks away from them, and they emerge; that is an easy gathering for Us" — the same word describes the ease of the resurrection. The withdrawal of the shade in 25:46, read alongside these passages, becomes a parable of the Hour: just as the shade is gently withdrawn toward God, so the created order will be gently withdrawn toward its Maker. The ease of the withdrawal is the point: what seems impossible to human beings — the end of the world, the resurrection of the dead — is, for God, as easy as the movement of a shadow across the ground.

**The Relationship Between 25:45 and 25:46**

The two verses form a single observation divided across two sentences. Verse 25:45 describes the extension of the shade and the sun's role as its guide; verse 25:46 describes the shade's withdrawal toward God. The sequence — extension, guiding, withdrawal — mirrors the sequence of human life: the soul is extended into the body, guided through life by the signs God provides, and then withdrawn back toward its source. The commentators who read these verses as spiritual allegory see in this sequence the entire arc of the human experience: creation, guidance, and return. The shade is the temporary thing; the sun is the permanent thing; and the withdrawal is the moment when the temporary yields to the permanent. The sūrah does not state the allegory explicitly, but the structure invites it, and the invitation is part of the Qur'an's method: the natural world is not merely a collection of physical phenomena but a text to be read, and the reading leads to the same conclusion the revelation leads to — that everything returns to God.

**The Ease of Divine Action**

The word yasīr (gentle, easy) in 25:46 describes the quality of God’s withdrawal of the shade, and the same word appears in other Qur’anic passages to describe the ease of divine action. In 29:19 — “Do they not see how Allah originates creation and then reproduces it? Indeed, that, for Allah, is easy” — the word yasīr describes the ease of creation itself. In 50:44 — “The Day the earth breaks away from them, and they emerge; that is an easy gathering for Us” — the word describes the ease of the resurrection. The withdrawal of the shade in 25:46 is therefore a small-scale demonstration of a cosmic principle: what seems impossible to human beings — the end of the world, the resurrection of the dead, the uncreation of the created order — is, for God, as easy as the movement of a shadow across the ground. The ease is not a diminishment of the act but a revelation of the actor: the power that withdraws the shade is the same power that will withdraw the world, and both withdrawals are gentle, gradual, and certain.

**The Verse’s Place in the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:46 completes the two-verse observation about shade that began in 25:45. The pair — extension of shade, withdrawal of shade — is one unit of sign, and the unit demonstrates both God’s generosity and His sovereignty. The generosity is in the extension: the shade provides relief from the heat, a space for rest, a covering for the creatures beneath it. The sovereignty is in the withdrawal: the shade does not stay because the creatures want it to stay; it retreats because God wills it to retreat. The combination of generosity and sovereignty is the sūrah’s portrait of divine action: God gives freely and takes back authoritatively, and the giving and the taking are both expressions of the same will. The disbelievers who enjoy the shade without acknowledging its source are enjoying a gift they did not earn from a giver they do not recognize. The sūrah’s observation is designed to close the gap between enjoyment and acknowledgment: the shade is the evidence, and the evidence points to the One who extends it and withdraws it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:47

> He is the One Who has made the night for you as a cover, and ˹made˺ sleep for resting, and the day for rising

**Expanded Commentary**

**Night as a Garment**

The verse describes three gifts of God in a single sentence: night, sleep, and day. The night is called *libās* — a garment, a covering, something that wraps around the body and conceals it. The metaphor is precise: just as a garment protects the body from cold and exposure, the night protects human beings from the exhaustion of constant activity. The darkness is not a deficiency but a mercy — it provides the conditions under which rest becomes possible. Without night, there would be no pause, no interval of stillness, no period in which the body and mind can recover from the demands of the day. The word *libās* (garment) implies intimacy: the night does not merely exist around the human being; it wraps around them, covering them as a garment covers the body.

The same metaphor appears in 78:10 — "And We made the night as a covering" — where the sense of envelopment is the same. The night is one of God's most basic gifts, so basic that it is easily overlooked. The sūrah's placement of this observation in the midst of its arguments against the disbelievers serves a specific purpose: the same God who threatens the disbelievers with punishment also provides them with the nightly rest that sustains their lives. The mercy and the warning come from the same source, and the disbelievers' failure to recognize the source in either case is the error the sūrah diagnoses.

**Sleep as Death's Brother**

The word *subātan* (sleep, rest) is described as something God "made" — *ja'ala* — placing it in the same category as night and day: not a natural occurrence but a divine provision. Al-Ṭabarī notes the Arabic proverb "Sleep is the brother of death" (*al-nawm akhu'l-mawt*), and the Qur'an itself draws the connection: "God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that have not died, during their sleep" (39:42). Sleep, in this reading, is a minor death — a temporary removal of the soul from the body, a rehearsal for the permanent removal that death will bring. The body lies still, the senses are suspended, and the person exists in a state that is, experientially, indistinguishable from death. The difference is that sleep is reversible and death is not — but the experience is the same, and the sameness is the lesson. The disbelievers who deny the resurrection (25:11) undergo a miniature version of it every night and do not recognize it.

**Day as Resurrection**

The word *nushūrā* (rising, resurrection) is used for the day, creating a triple parallel: night is covering, sleep is rest, and day is rising. The word *nushūr* is the same word used for the resurrection of the dead — the rising from the grave on the Day of Judgment. By applying it to the daily experience of waking up, the verse draws a parallel between the micro-resurrection of each morning and the macro-resurrection of the Day of Judgment. Every morning, the sleeper rises from a state that resembles death; every Day of Judgment, the dead will rise from a state that is death. The daily cycle is a rehearsal for the eschatological event, and the rehearsal is so regular, so predictable, so deeply embedded in the rhythm of human life, that it passes without notice. The sūrah's purpose in naming it is to make the unnoticed noticed: the rising from sleep is the sign; the rising from death is the reality the sign points to.

**The Daily Cycle as Eschatological Rehearsal**

The three elements of verse 25:47 — night, sleep, and day — correspond to three stages of the human spiritual journey. The night is the covering that conceals; sleep is the temporary death that suspends consciousness; and the day is the resurrection that restores it. The daily cycle is a microcosm of the eschatological cycle: every evening, the world enters a kind of death; every night, the sleeper enters a deeper death; and every morning, the world and the sleeper are resurrected. The correspondence is not accidental; it is the Qur'an's way of teaching the reality of the resurrection through the familiarity of daily experience. The disbelievers who deny the resurrection experience a small resurrection every morning and do not recognize it. The sūrah's placement of this observation in the midst of its catalogue of natural signs serves the same purpose as the other signs: to demonstrate that the evidence for God's power is not hidden in a distant miracle but embedded in the most ordinary rhythms of human life. The evidence is in the waking, and the waking is every day.


**The Verse's Connection to the Sūrah's Warnings**

The description of night, sleep, and day in 25:47 is not a digression from the sūrah's argument but a continuation of it. The sūrah has described the punishment of the disbelievers in the fire (25:11-14), the reward of the righteous in the garden (25:15-16), and the natural signs that point to God's power (25:45-46). Verse 25:47 adds the daily cycle to the catalogue of signs, and the addition serves the sūrah's central argument: the evidence for God's existence and power is not hidden in a distant miracle but embedded in the most ordinary rhythms of human life. The disbelievers who deny the resurrection (25:11) experience a small resurrection every morning and do not recognize it. The sūrah's purpose in naming the daily cycle is to make the unnoticed noticed: the rising from sleep is the sign; the rising from death is the reality the sign points to. The connection between the daily sign and the eschatological reality is the sūrah's way of saying: the resurrection is not an extraordinary claim; it is the natural extension of an ordinary experience. The disbelievers who accept the daily cycle without recognizing its theological implications are living inside the evidence and refusing to read it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:48

> And He is the One Who sends the winds ushering in His mercy, and We send down pure rain from the sky

**Expanded Commentary**

**Winds as Harbingers of Mercy**

The verse continues the catalogue of natural signs that began in 25:45. The winds are described as *mubashshirāt* — bearers of good news, heralds of glad tidings — sent ahead of God's mercy (*raḥmah*). The mercy in question is rain, and the winds are its advance guard. In the Arabian climate, the arrival of wind signaled the approach of rain clouds — a sequence so regular that it became a sign the audience could recognize without being told. The word *mubashshirāt* (bearers of good news) is the same word used for the angels who bring good news to the righteous at death (16:32; 41:30). The parallel is deliberate: the winds that herald rain are doing the same work as the angels who herald reward — they are messengers of mercy, announcing what is about to arrive.

The connection between wind and rain appears throughout the Qur'an. In 7:57 — "It is He who sends the winds as good news before His mercy, until when they carry heavy rain clouds, We drive them to a dead land and send down rain, bringing forth all kinds of fruits" — the same sequence is described in greater detail: the winds carry the clouds, the clouds release the rain, and the rain brings dead land to life. In 30:46 — "Among His signs is that He sends the winds bearing good news, so that He may make you taste His mercy" — the tasting of mercy through rain is made explicit. The sūrah's use of this image in 25:48 adds to its catalogue of signs: after the shade (25:45-46) and the night, sleep, and day (25:47), the winds and rain complete the picture of a world designed for human benefit.

**Pure Rain: The Word *Mubārak***

The rain is described as *ṭahūr* — pure, purifying, clean. The word carries both meanings: the rain itself is pure (free from contamination), and it purifies what it touches (cleansing the earth, the air, and the bodies that receive it). Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ṭahūr* can also mean "blessed" (*mubārak*), and several commentators read it as carrying both senses simultaneously: the rain is pure and it brings blessing. The physical purity of rainwater — its transparency, its taste, its suitability for drinking and irrigation — is a sign of divine care: God could have made rain muddy, salty, or unfit for consumption, but He made it pure because purity serves the creatures who depend on it.

The word *ṭahūr* also connects to the Qur'anic concept of ritual purification. In 8:11 — "He sent down upon you water from the sky to purify you thereby" — rain is described as a means of spiritual as well as physical cleansing. The rain that washes the earth also washes the soul, and the regularity of the rain — season after season, year after year — is a regular renewal of the opportunity for purification. The disbelievers who receive the rain and do not recognize its source are receiving mercy they do not acknowledge, and the unacknowledged mercy becomes, on the Day of Judgment, an additional count in the case against them.

**The Natural Signs as the Answer the Disbelievers Refused**

The catalogue of natural signs in 25:45-48 — shade, night, sleep, day, wind, rain — serves as the sūrah's alternative to the spectacular signs the disbelievers demanded. They asked for angels (25:7, 25:21); God points to the wind. They asked for treasure (25:8); God points to the rain. They asked for visible, dramatic proof of divine power; God offers the shade that moves across the ground and the wind that heralds the rain. The signs are not less powerful for being quiet; they are more powerful for being constant. An angel appearing once would be a spectacle; the daily cycle of shade and light is a permanent, universal, undeniable demonstration of divine sovereignty. The sūrah's method is to replace the disbelievers' imagined signs with the real ones — the signs they have already seen and failed to interpret — and to ask whether the failure to interpret them is a failure of evidence or a failure of attention.

**Rain as the Sustaining Gift**

The rain described in 25:48 is not merely water falling from the sky; it is a comprehensive provision that sustains every form of life on earth. The rain feeds the rivers, waters the crops, nourishes the livestock, fills the wells, and replenishes the aquifers that supply human communities. The word ṭahūr (pure, purifying) applied to the rain describes both its physical quality — clean, drinkable, life-giving — and its spiritual quality — a reminder of God’s mercy that purifies the soul of ingratitude. The Qur’an’s consistent pairing of rain with divine mercy (7:57; 16:10; 25:48; 30:24) establishes a theological framework in which the natural world is not a neutral backdrop but a continuous expression of God’s care. The disbelievers who receive the rain and do not acknowledge its source are receiving mercy they do not deserve, and the unacknowledged mercy becomes, on the Day of Judgment, an additional count in the case against them. The rain falls on believers and disbelievers alike, and the universality of the provision is the universality of the invitation: God’s mercy is available to everyone, and the only barrier between the mercy and the recipient is the recipient’s refusal to acknowledge its source.

**The Verse’s Placement in the Sūrah’s Natural Sign Catalogue**

Verse 25:48 continues the catalogue of natural signs that began in 25:45 with the shade. The sequence — shade (45-46), night-sleep-day (47), wind and rain (48) — builds a cumulative argument: each sign adds a new dimension of divine power and generosity. The shade demonstrates God’s control of light and darkness; the night-sleep-day cycle demonstrates His control of time and consciousness; the wind and rain demonstrate His control of the atmosphere and the sustenance of life. The cumulative effect is stronger than any single sign because it demonstrates not one act of power but a sustained pattern of provision. The sūrah is not asking the disbelievers to believe based on a single observation; it is asking them to observe the totality of the created world and recognize the power behind it. The wind that heralds the rain, the rain that revives the land, and the land that feeds the creatures are one chain of provision, and the chain’s links are forged by the same hand. The disbelievers who accept the rain as a natural phenomenon but deny its divine source are accepting one link while denying the chain.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:49

> giving life to a lifeless land, and providing water for countless animals and humans of Our Own creation

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Resurrection of Dead Land**

The verse continues the description of rain's purpose: *li-nuḥyiya bihi balḍatan maytan* — "so that We may give life thereby to a dead land." The word *mayt* (dead) applied to land describes earth that has been stripped of vegetation, color, and vitality — desert, parched ground, land that produces nothing. The rain transforms this dead land into something living: seeds germinate, grasses grow, the brown expanse turns green. The transformation is one of the most visible and dramatic signs in the natural world, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly as an analogy for the resurrection of the dead. In 22:5 — "You see the earth barren, but when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells and grows every delightful pair" — the same observation is made with the same implication: the God who brings dead land to life can bring dead bodies to life. In 30:50 — "Look at the effects of God's mercy: how He gives life to the earth after its death. Verily, that is the One who will give life to the dead, and He has power over all things" — the connection is made explicit.

The disbelievers of Makkah witnessed this transformation regularly. The Arabian climate is one of extremes: months of dryness followed by sudden, intense rainfall that transforms the landscape overnight. The transformation was visible, repeatable, and dramatic — everything the disbelievers demanded in a sign. Yet they did not interpret it as evidence of divine power or as an analogy for the resurrection they denied. The sūrah's catalogue of natural signs is therefore not merely a description of the natural world; it is an indictment of the disbelievers' failure to read the world correctly. The signs are everywhere; the evidence is overwhelming; and the failure to see it is the failure the sūrah diagnoses in every verse.

**Providing for Creatures of Every Kind**

The second half of the verse specifies the recipients of the rain: *wa inʿāmin kathīrin mim-man khalaqnā* — "and many animals from among what We have created." The word *inʿām* refers specifically to livestock — camels, cattle, sheep, goats — the animals that the Arabian audience depended on for survival. The rain gives life to the land that feeds these animals, and through the animals, the rain feeds the humans who keep them. The chain of provision — rain, land, animal, human — is the sūrah's way of showing that divine mercy is not abstract but practical, not theoretical but embodied in the daily reality of sustenance.

The word *kathīr* (many, countless) emphasizes the breadth of the provision. The rain does not serve one species or one tribe; it serves everything that lives on the land. The universality of the provision is the sign: a God who provides for all creatures without discrimination is a God whose mercy is not limited by tribe, nation, or species. The disbelievers who attributed the rain to stars or to chance (a practice al-Iṣfahānī and al-Qurṭubī note) were ignoring the most basic fact about the rain: it falls on everyone, serves everyone, and sustains everyone, and the universality of the sustenance is evidence of the universality of the Provider.

**The Continuity of the Sign Catalogue**

Verse 25:49 continues the natural sign catalogue that began in 25:45. The sequence — shade (45-46), night-sleep-day (47), wind and rain (48), revival of dead land (49) — builds a cumulative argument. Each sign adds a new dimension of divine power: the ability to move shadows, to create the cycle of rest and waking, to send winds that herald rain, and to bring dead earth to life. The cumulative effect is stronger than any single sign because it demonstrates not one act of power but a sustained pattern of provision. The sūrah is not asking the disbelievers to believe based on a single miracle; it is asking them to observe the world they already live in and recognize the power behind it. The world is full of signs, and the signs all point in the same direction. The disbelievers' refusal to follow the signs is not a failure of evidence but a failure of will, and the sūrah names the failure in the verse that follows (25:50).

**The Resurrection Analogy Made Visible**

The revival of dead land is one of the Qur’an’s most frequently used analogies for the resurrection of the dead. The analogy works because it is visible, repeatable, and dramatic: a barren expanse of earth, apparently lifeless and incapable of producing anything, is struck by rain and within days produces grasses, flowers, and the green carpet of new life. The transformation is so complete that it seems like a creation from nothing, and the Qur’an uses it to argue that the God who performs this transformation daily can perform the greater transformation of raising the dead. In 22:5 the argument is stated explicitly: “We have made every living thing from water. Will they not then believe?” The analogy is not a proof in the philosophical sense; it is a demonstration of capacity. The God who revives dead land has the power to revive dead bodies, and the demonstration is renewed every time it rains. The disbelievers of Makkah witnessed this transformation regularly and did not draw the conclusion the sūrah draws. The sūrah draws it for them, and the drawing is the invitation.

**The Verse’s Contribution to the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:49 adds the most dramatic of the natural signs to the catalogue: the revival of dead land by rain. The drama lies in the visibility of the transformation — a barren desert becomes, within days of rainfall, a carpet of green. The transformation is not hidden or rare; it is one of the most visible and recurring phenomena in the natural world, and the Qur’an uses it as the primary analogy for the resurrection of the dead. The sūrah’s placement of this sign after the description of rain (25:48) and before the statement about human ingratitude (25:50) creates a specific argumentative sequence: God sends the rain, God revives the land, and the people who receive both are ungrateful. The sequence mirrors the sūrah’s larger argument: God sends the revelation, God guides the community, and the disbelievers who receive both are ungrateful. The parallel between rain and revelation, between the revival of land and the revival of hearts, is the sūrah’s way of saying that the Quran is as necessary, as available, and as life-giving as rain, and the rejection of the Quran is as irrational as the refusal to drink the water that sustains life.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:50

> We certainly disperse it among them so they may be mindful, but most people persist in ungratefulness

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Distribution of Rain as a Moral Test**

The verse names the purpose behind the rain's distribution: *wa laqad ṣarrafnāhu baynahum li-yadhdhakkarū* — "And We have certainly distributed it among them that they may be mindful." The word *ṣarrafnā* (We have distributed, We have turned, We have dispersed) describes the pattern of rainfall: it does not fall everywhere at once or in equal measure. Some regions receive abundant rain; others receive little. Some years bring floods; others bring drought. The variation is not random but deliberate — designed to provoke reflection (*tadhakkur*). If rain fell uniformly and predictably, it would be taken for granted. Because it varies, it invites the question: who controls the variation? The answer, for anyone willing to reflect, is the One who sends it — God, whose sovereignty over the weather is absolute and whose distribution of resources is purposeful.

The commentators note that the variation in rainfall also serves as a reminder of human dependence. A tribe that receives abundant rain is reminded that the abundance is a gift, not a right; a tribe that receives little is reminded that the scarcity is a test, not a punishment. Both experiences are designed to produce *tadhakkur* — mindfulness, remembrance, the awareness that one's sustenance comes from a source beyond one's control. The word *tadhakkur* is the same root as *dhikr* (remembrance, the Reminder), and the connection is deliberate: the rain is a reminder of the same truth the Quran is a reminder of — that God is the provider, the sustainer, the one on whom all creatures depend.

**Most People Persist in Ingratitude**

The verse's second clause — *wa aktharu'n-nāsi illā kafarū* — states the outcome of the test: "but most people persist in ungratefulness." The word *kafarū* (they disbelieved, they were ungrateful, they covered over) carries the dual meaning that the Qur'an consistently assigns to *kufr*: denial of God and ingratitude for His gifts. The rain falls, the land produces, the animals are fed, and the people who receive all of this attribute it to something other than God — to the stars, to the weather, to their own ingenuity, to chance. The attribution is an act of *kufr* because it covers over (*k-f-r* literally means "to cover") the truth of the source. The rain is the evidence; the source is God; and the refusal to acknowledge the source is the covering-over that the word *kufr* names.

Al-Iṣfahānī and al-Qurṭubī note that the practice of attributing rainfall to the rising and setting of certain stars was common among the pre-Islamic Arabs. The Qur'an addresses this directly in 52:49 — "And in the alternation of night and day, and in what God sends down from the sky of provision by which He gives life to the earth after its death, and in the directing of the winds — truly there are signs for people who use reason" — where the natural phenomena the Arabs attributed to stars are explicitly attributed to God. Verse 25:50 names the attribution to stars as a form of *kufr* — not atheism in the modern sense but a misattribution that denies God's direct involvement in the provision of sustenance.

**The Relationship Between Rain and Revelation**

The placement of the rain sign immediately after the disbelievers' mockery (25:41-44) and before the command to strive with the Quran (25:52) creates a structural parallel between rain and revelation. Both are sent down from above; both are distributed according to God's wisdom; both are designed to produce *tadhakkur* (mindfulness); and both are rejected by most people. The rain falls on disbelievers and believers alike, and the Quran is recited for disbelievers and believers alike. The disbelievers receive both and respond with *kufr* — covering over the truth of the source. The parallel between rain and revelation is the sūrah's way of saying that the Quran is as natural, as necessary, and as available as rain, and the rejection of the Quran is as irrational as the refusal to acknowledge the source of the water that sustains life.

**The Persistent Ingratitude of Humanity**

The verse’s closing statement — “most people persist in ungratefulness” — is one of the Qur’an’s most sweeping observations about human nature. The word kafarū (they disbelieved, they were ungrateful) carries the dual meaning that the Qur’an consistently assigns to kufr: denial of God and ingratitude for His gifts. The rain falls, the land produces, the animals are fed, and the people who receive all of this attribute it to something other than God. The attribution is an act of kufr because it covers over (k-f-r literally means “to cover”) the truth of the source. The word aktharū (most) is significant: the verse does not say all people are ungrateful; it says most. The minority who are grateful — who recognize the rain as a gift and its source as God — are the believers, and their gratitude is the mark of the distinction the sūrah has been drawing since 25:1 between those who accept the Criterion and those who reject it. The rain is the test, the gratitude is the response, and the response reveals the heart.

**The Verse’s Role in the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:50 closes the natural sign catalogue that began in 25:45 and transitions to the next section of the sūrah. The transition is from observation to command: after demonstrating God’s power through the signs of shade, night, sleep, day, wind, rain, and the revival of dead land, the sūrah will command the Prophet to strive with the Quran (25:52). The statement that “most people persist in ungratefulness” is the bridge between the two sections: the signs have been presented, the evidence has been offered, and the result is that most people remain unconvinced. The failure of the evidence to produce universal faith is not a failure of the evidence but a failure of the audience, and the failure is the reason the striving is necessary. If the signs were sufficient on their own, no striving would be needed. Because most people resist the signs, the Prophet must strive with the Quran — the instrument that interprets the signs and gives them their meaning. The sūrah’s structure thus moves from the sign to the interpreter, from the evidence to the voice that explains it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:51

> Had We willed, We could have easily sent a warner to every society

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Singling Out of the Prophet**

The verse presents a counterfactual: *wa law shi'nā la-baʿthnā fī kulli qaryatin nadhīrān* — "Had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town." The counterfactual serves a specific purpose: it magnifies the status of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ by showing what God did not do. God could have sent a warner to every town — a separate prophet for each community, as in earlier times — but He chose instead to send one Prophet to all of humanity. The singling out is not a limitation but an honor: the Prophet who carries the final message must be extraordinary enough to carry it alone, and the message must be universal enough to reach every town without being re-sent.

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as God telling the Prophet that it might have been easier for him if more messengers had been sent to share the burden. In earlier times, each community had its own prophet, and the responsibility of warning was distributed across many individuals. In the final dispensation, the responsibility is concentrated in one person, and the weight of that concentration is the Prophet's unique burden. The verse does not complain about the burden; it names it, and in naming it, honors it. The Prophet's singularity is not a deficiency in the system but its design.

**The Contrast Between Rain and Revelation**

The verse follows the description of rain falling on many lands (25:48-50), and the contrast is deliberate. Rain is distributed universally — it falls on every town, every land, every community. Revelation, in the final dispensation, is sent through one person to all communities. The rain reaches everyone directly; the revelation reaches everyone through a single channel. The comparison highlights the different modes of divine provision: physical sustenance (rain) is given directly to all; spiritual guidance (revelation) is given through a messenger to all. The disbelievers who acknowledged the rain as a natural phenomenon but rejected the revelation as a human invention were accepting one form of divine provision while refusing the other. The verse names the refusal and shows that it was not inevitable — God could have sent a warner to every town, but He chose to send one to all of them, and the choice was an act of trust in the Prophet's capacity and the message's universality.

**The Implication for the Prophet's Mission**

The verse also implies that the Prophet's mission is, in a specific sense, more difficult than the missions of earlier prophets. Each earlier prophet was sent to a single community, in a single language, addressing a single set of cultural circumstances. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was sent to all communities, in a message that must transcend every language and every culture. The breadth of the mission required a message that was itself universal — the Quran, a text that speaks to every era and every society without being limited to any of them. The verse's counterfactual — "Had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town" — is therefore not merely a statement about what God could have done; it is a statement about what God chose to do and why. He chose one Prophet and one message because the message was sufficient for all, and the Prophet was capable of carrying it alone. The choice is the honor, and the honor is the message.

**The Weight of Being Singled Out**

Al-Ṭabarī reads the verse as God telling the Prophet that it might have been easier on him if more messengers were sent to share the burden. The concentration of prophetic responsibility in a single person is a heavy load, and the verse acknowledges it without diminishing it. Earlier prophets had the advantage of community: Moses had Aaron, and every prophetic community had a local leader who understood its specific circumstances. The Prophet Muhammad was sent alone to all of humanity, across all languages and cultures and centuries, and the weight of that universality is the weight the verse names and the honor the verse confers. The singling out is not a deficiency in the system but a feature of the final message: the last message must be universal, and the last messenger must be capable of carrying it alone. The verse’s counterfactual — “Had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town” — is therefore not a lament but a statement of trust: God trusted the Prophet with the whole world, and the trust is the honor.

**The Verse’s Theological Implication**

The verse’s counterfactual — “Had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town” — implies that God’s choice of a single messenger was deliberate, not arbitrary. The singling out of the Prophet Muhammad for the final, universal message was a decision made in divine wisdom, and the decision reflects God’s knowledge of the Prophet’s capacity and the message’s sufficiency. The verse does not explain why God chose one messenger instead of many; it simply states the choice and, in stating it, honors the chosen. The Prophet’s burden is heavier than any earlier prophet’s, but the honor is proportionate: the one who carries the final message to all of humanity has been judged capable of carrying it alone. The sūrah’s placement of this verse after the statement about human ingratitude (25:50) and before the command to strive (25:52) creates a specific message: the Prophet’s task is difficult, the audience is resistant, and the response is not despair but effort. The “great striving” of 25:52 is the human response to the divine choice of 25:51. This cooperative dynamic — God chooses, and the chosen one strives — is the fundamental pattern of prophetic mission throughout the Qur’an.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:52

> So do not yield to the disbelievers, but strive diligently against them with this ˹Quran˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Resist**

The verse issues a direct command to the Prophet: *falā tuṭiʿi'l-kāfirīna wa jāhidhum bihi jihādan kabīrān* — "So do not obey the disbelievers, and strive against them with it — a great striving." The word *lā tuṭiʿ* (do not obey) is addressed to the Prophet in the singular, making it personal: this is not a general instruction to the community but a specific instruction to the messenger. The disbelievers had offered the Prophet various compromises — wealth, kingship, a share of political power — in exchange for the modification or withdrawal of his message. The command not to obey them is a command to refuse every compromise, to accept no terms that would alter the message, and to maintain the Quran's integrity against every form of social or economic pressure.

The word *jihād* (striving, struggle) is paired with the qualifier *kabīr* (great, immense), intensifying the effort required. The *jihād* in this verse is not military — al-Qurṭubī and others explicitly state that this is a Makkan sūrah and the permission to fight had not yet been given. The striving here is intellectual, spiritual, and social: the Prophet is commanded to oppose the disbelievers' rejection with the full force of the Quran's message. The instrument of the striving is specified: *bihi* — "with it," referring to the Quran itself. The Quran is the weapon, and the striving is the effort of presenting, explaining, defending, and living the message in the face of sustained opposition.

**The Word "With It": The Quran as the Instrument**

The pronoun *bihi* ("with it") in the phrase "strive against them with it" identifies the Quran as the instrument of *jihād*. This is one of the most significant statements in the sūrah about the nature of the prophetic mission: the Prophet's primary tool is not political power, military force, or social leverage — it is the text itself. The Quran is the Criterion (25:1), the Reminder (25:29), the message that distinguishes truth from falsehood (25:1), and it is also the weapon with which the Prophet is commanded to strive. The striving is not physical combat but textual engagement: presenting the Quran's arguments, answering the disbelievers' objections (25:33), and maintaining the message's clarity against every attempt to distort or dismiss it.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that the phrase "with it" makes the Quran both the message and the means of delivering the message. The Prophet does not need another tool; the Quran is sufficient for every argument the disbelievers raise, and the sūrah has demonstrated this by recording the disbelievers' objections (25:4-8, 25:21) and God's responses (25:6, 25:10-16, 25:20, 25:33). The pattern is the instruction: the Prophet's role is to deliver the Quran's answers to the disbelievers' questions, and the delivery itself is the striving. The command in 25:52 is therefore not a call to violence but a call to persistence — to continue delivering the message regardless of the opposition, and to use the message itself as the instrument of opposition.

**The Jihād of the Makkan Period**

The sūrah's Makkan context shapes the meaning of *jihād* in this verse. During the Makkan period, the Prophet ﷺ and the believers were a minority without political power, military capacity, or social protection. The *jihād* available to them was the *jihād* of patience, perseverance, and presentation — continuing to recite the Quran, continuing to invite people to the truth, continuing to live by the message's principles in the face of persecution and mockery. This form of *jihād* is, in many ways, more demanding than physical combat because it requires sustained effort without the possibility of a quick resolution. The command to "strive against them with it — a great striving" names the effort required and the instrument available, and the combination — great effort with the Quran as the only tool — defines the Prophet's mission during the years in Makkah.

**The Great Striving Defined**

The phrase jihādan kabīran (a great striving) specifies the magnitude of the effort required. The jihād is not casual or occasional; it is great — all-consuming, sustained, and demanding the full measure of the Prophet’s capacity. The word jihād in this Makkan context refers to the intellectual, spiritual, and social effort of presenting the Quran in the face of determined opposition. The Prophet is not commanded to fight; he is commanded to strive, and the instrument of striving is the Quran itself. The distinction between jihād and qitāl (fighting) is crucial: the Makkan jihād is the effort of persuasion, endurance, and steadfast presentation of the truth. It requires patience (sabr) in the face of mockery (25:41), resilience in the face of opposition (25:52), and trust in God’s sufficiency (25:58). The “greatness” of the striving is measured not by its physical intensity but by its spiritual demands: the Prophet must maintain the message’s integrity, the community’s morale, and his own faith in the face of a society that rejects everything he stands for.

**The Verse’s Role in the Sūrah’s Structure**

Verse 25:52 is the pivot between the sūrah’s catalogue of natural signs (25:45-50) and its catalogue of divine signs in the physical world (25:53-55). After demonstrating God’s power through shade, night, day, wind, rain, and the revival of dead land, the sūrah commands the Prophet to use the Quran as the instrument of striving. The command marks a transition from observation to action: the signs have been seen, the evidence has been presented, and now the Prophet must act on what the evidence shows.
