The instrument of action is specified: the Quran. The Prophet does not need another tool; the Quran is the Criterion (25:1), the Reminder (25:29), and the instrument of striving (25:52). The sūrah’s structure thus moves from the natural world to the revealed text, from the sign to the interpreter, and from the observation to the command. The movement is the sūrah’s way of saying: the signs are evidence, and the Quran is the voice that gives the evidence its meaning.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:53

> And He is the One Who merges the two bodies of water: one fresh and palatable and the other salty and bitter, placing between them a barrier they cannot cross

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Seas and Their Separation**

The verse describes another sign in the natural world: the meeting of two bodies of water — one fresh and sweet (*'adhb furāt*), the other salty and bitter (*milḥ ujāj*) — and the barrier (*barzakh*) that prevents them from merging. The phenomenon is observable at estuaries and river mouths around the world, where freshwater rivers flow into the salty ocean and the two waters remain distinct for a measurable distance before gradually mixing. The Qur'an's observation of this phenomenon is not merely scientific but theological: the separation is a sign of divine power, because the natural tendency of two liquids placed in contact is to mix, and the maintenance of the boundary requires a force that opposes the tendency.

The word *barzakh* (barrier, isthmus, partition) appears in the Qur'an in both physical and eschatological contexts. In 23:100 — "And behind them is a barrier until the Day they are resurrected" — the *barzakh* refers to the interval between death and resurrection, the state in which the dead exist before the Day of Judgment. In 55:19-20 — "He released the two seas, meeting side by side; between them is a barrier which they do not transgress" — the same physical phenomenon is described with the same word. The dual usage — physical barrier and eschatological interval — suggests that the *barzakh* is a principle, not merely a phenomenon: God places barriers between things that should not mix, and the barriers hold because God wills them to hold. The fresh water and the salt water are kept apart by the same power that keeps the living and the dead apart.

**The Symbolic Reading of the Two Waters**

Several commentators offer symbolic readings of the two bodies of water. Ibn ʿAjībah reads the fresh water as a reference to the *Sharīʿah* (the sacred law), which is easy to attain and accessible to all, and the salty water as *ḥaqīqah* (ultimate spiritual truth), which can only be reached through effort and courage. Al-Qurṭubī reads the two waters as hope and fear in the believer's heart — the two qualities that should coexist without one overwhelming the other. The fresh water represents hope in God's mercy; the salty water represents fear of God's judgment. The believer who maintains both — neither drowning in despair nor floating in complacency — has achieved the spiritual balance that the *barzakh* represents.

These symbolic readings are not the verse's primary meaning, which is a sign in the natural world. But they demonstrate the Qur'an's capacity to function on multiple levels: the physical phenomenon points to a spiritual truth, and the spiritual truth illuminates the physical phenomenon. The barrier between fresh and salt water is real, observable, and measurable; it is also a metaphor for the barriers God places between every pair of opposites — life and death, belief and disbelief, mercy and punishment. The barriers are God's work, and the maintenance of the barriers is a sign of His power and His wisdom.

**The Repeated Structure of the Sign Catalogue**

Verse 25:53 continues the pattern established in 25:45: each verse presents a natural phenomenon and identifies it as a sign of God's power. The shade (45-46), the night-sleep-day cycle (47), the winds and rain (48-49), and now the two seas (53) form a chain of evidence, each link adding a new dimension to the argument. The chain will continue with human creation (25:54), and the cumulative effect is a worldview in which every observable phenomenon — from the movement of shadows to the mixing of waters — is evidence of the One who controls it. The sūrah does not ask the disbelievers to believe based on a single sign; it asks them to observe the totality of the created world and recognize the power behind it. The totality is the argument, and the argument is addressed to people who have eyes and refuse to see.

**The Barrier Between the Mixable and the Unmixable**

The word barzakh (barrier) in 25:53 names a principle that extends beyond the physical meeting of fresh and salt water. In the Qur’an’s cosmology, barzakh is the space between opposites: between life and death (23:100), between mercy and punishment, between the seen and the unseen. The physical barrier between the two bodies of water is a visible demonstration of an invisible principle: God places boundaries between things that should not mix, and the boundaries hold because God sustains them. The fresh water would be polluted by the salt if the barrier dissolved; the salt water would lose its character if the fresh water overwhelmed it. Each body of water maintains its identity because the barrier maintains the separation. The theological implication is that God’s creation is characterized by differentiation: things are not blended into an undifferentiated mass but separated into distinct categories, each with its own function, each maintained by divine will. The barrier between fresh and salt water is the same kind of barrier that separates the believer from the disbeliever, the guided from the lost, and the living from the dead.

**The Verse’s Connection to the Sūrah’s Earlier Uses of Barzakh**

The word barzakh (barrier) appears earlier in the sūrah in a very different context: in 25:22, the disbelievers cry “Ḥijran maḥjūrā!” (“A barrier, forbidden!”) when they see the angels on the Day of Judgment. The echo between 25:22 and 25:53 is deliberate: the same word that names the barrier between fresh and salt water names the barrier the disbelievers will erect between themselves and the angels. In both cases, the barrier is something God maintains: the physical barrier keeps the waters from mixing; the eschatological barrier keeps the disbelievers from receiving mercy. The repetition of barzakh across the sūrah creates a thematic link between the natural world and the Hereafter, between the signs in creation and the consequences in judgment. The barrier that the disbelievers cry out for on the Day of Judgment is the same kind of barrier that God places between the mixable and the unmixable in the natural world — a boundary that holds because God wills it to hold.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:54

> And He is the One Who creates human beings from a ˹humble˺ liquid, then establishes for them bonds of kinship and marriage. For your Lord is Most Capable

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Water to Human Society**

The verse traces the origin of human social life to a single substance: *mā'* — water, or more precisely, the humble fluid from which human beings are created. The word *mā'* is used without elaboration, preserving the Qur'an's characteristic reticence about biological details while making the theological point: the beginning of every human being is a substance so humble that it would be dismissed as insignificant if God had not attached enormous consequences to it. From this substance, God creates the human being, and from the human being, God establishes the entire structure of social life: *fa-jaʿalahu nasaban wa ṣiḥrā* — "then He made for him lineage and marriage." The word *nasab* refers to kinship by blood — the family tree, the genetic connection that links parents to children and siblings to each other. The word *ṣiḥr* refers to kinship by marriage — the bond that links families through the union of husband and wife.

The two forms of kinship together constitute the foundation of human society. Blood kinship creates the family; marriage kinship creates the alliance between families. The combination produces the tribe, the community, the civilization. The verse's point is that all of this — the entire structure of human social organization — originates in a single creative act: God's creation of the human being from water. The chain of causation is: water → human being → family → marriage → society. At every link in the chain, God is the agent: He creates from water, He establishes kinship, He designs marriage. The social order is not a human invention but a divine design, and the design reflects God's power (*qadīr*) and His wisdom.

**The Theology of Human Origin**

The verse's mention of human creation from water connects to other Qur'anic passages that describe the same process. In 21:30 — "We made from water every living thing" — the principle is universal: all life depends on water. In 24:45 — "Allah has created every animal from water" — the same universality is stated. In 32:7-8 — "He began the creation of man from clay, then made his progeny from an extract of despised water" — the specific application to human beings is made. The Qur'an's consistent use of water as the origin of life serves a theological purpose: it establishes the humility of human origins and, by contrast, the grandeur of God's creative power. The same God who created the heavens and the earth (25:59) also created human beings from water, and the disparity between the origin and the outcome is the measure of His power.

**The Connection to the Disbelievers' Rejection**

The verse's placement in the sūrah after the command to strive with the Quran (25:52) and the sign of the two seas (25:53) creates a specific argumentative context. The disbelievers who deny God's power are reminded that they themselves are evidence of it. They were created from water, their families were established by God, and their marriages were designed by God. The social world they inhabit — the tribal structures, the family alliances, the kinship networks — is a product of divine design, not human effort. The disbelievers who attribute their social order to their own ingenuity or to the traditions of their forefathers are ignoring the most basic fact about their existence: they began as a drop of fluid, and everything they are was made from that drop by a power they did not choose and cannot control. The verse ends with *wa Rabbuka Qadīr* — "and your Lord is Most Capable" — a declaration that closes the argument: the God who created you from nothing is capable of doing whatever He wills, including the resurrection you deny.

**Marriage as a Divine Institution**

The mention of marriage (ṣiḥr) in 25:54 alongside blood kinship (nasab) establishes marriage as a divinely designed institution, not a human invention. The two forms of kinship — blood and marriage — together produce the social structure that makes human civilization possible. Blood kinship creates the family; marriage creates the alliance between families. The combination produces the tribe, the community, the society. The verse’s point is that this structure is not accidental or evolutionary; it is a deliberate design by the One who created human beings from water. The theological claim is that social organization — including the institution of marriage — originates in divine wisdom, not in human convention. The disbelievers who attribute their social structures to their own traditions or to the customs of their forefathers are ignoring the most basic fact about their existence: they were created by a power that designed their social world as carefully as it designed their bodies. The verse’s closing declaration — “your Lord is Most Capable” — reinforces the point: the One who designed the social world has the power to redesign it, and the Day of Judgment will be that redesign.

**The Verse’s Placement in the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:54 continues the catalogue of divine signs in creation that began with the shade (25:45) and continued through the night-sleep-day cycle (25:47), the winds and rain (25:48-49), the two seas (25:53), and now the creation of human beings and the establishment of kinship and marriage. The sequence builds a comprehensive picture of divine providence: God controls the atmosphere (shade, wind, rain), the hydrosphere (the two seas), and the biosphere (human creation and social organization). The sūrah’s catalogue is not random but systematic: it moves from the outer world to the inner world, from the signs that surround the human being to the signs that constitute the human being. The creation from water, the establishment of kinship, and the design of marriage are the most intimate of all the signs because they describe the human being’s own origin and social existence. The sūrah’s message is that there is no aspect of human life — from the shade that covers the body to the water from which the body was created — that is not a sign of God’s power and care.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:55

> Yet they worship besides Allah what can neither benefit nor harm them. And the disbeliever always collaborates against their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return to Idolatry**

After the catalogue of natural signs (25:45-54), the verse returns to the sūrah's central theme: the disbelievers' worship of false gods. The transition is abrupt — from the grandeur of creation to the absurdity of idol worship — and the abruptness is deliberate. The sūrah has just demonstrated that God controls the shade, the night, the day, the winds, the rain, the seas, and the origin of human life. Against this backdrop of total sovereignty, the worship of anything that cannot benefit or harm is not merely wrong but irrational. The idols the disbelievers worship are made of the same created matter as the stones they walk on and the water they drink. They have no power over any of the phenomena the sūrah has described.

The phrase *lā yanfa'u wa-lā yaḍurru* — "that can neither benefit nor harm" — recurs throughout the Qur'an's arguments against idolatry. In 10:18 — "They worship besides Allah what neither harms them nor benefits them, and they say, 'These are our intercessors with Allah'" — the disbelievers' rationale is recorded: the idols serve as intercessors. In 13:16 — "Say, 'Who is Lord of the heavens and the earth?' Say, 'Allah.' Say, 'Have you then taken besides Him allies who possess not even for themselves any benefit or harm?'" — the argument is stated as a challenge. Verse 25:55 adds the observation that the disbelievers continue to worship these powerless objects even after the sūrah has demonstrated God's control over every aspect of the natural world.

**The Disbeliever as a Partisan Against God**

The second clause — *wa'l-kāfīru ʿalā Rabbihī ẓahīr* — is translated as "And the disbeliever always collaborates against their Lord," but the Arabic carries additional layers. The word *ẓahīr* can mean "helper," "ally," "supporter" — one who aids a cause. The disbeliever is described as an ally — but an ally against whom? Against God. The disbeliever is helping Satan, helping desire, helping the forces that oppose divine guidance, and the help is given at the expense of the One who created them, sustained them, and provided for them.

Al-Rāzī offers an alternative reading: *ẓahīr* can also mean "one who is disregarded" or "one who deems his Lord unimportant." In this reading, the disbeliever is not actively opposing God but passively dismissing Him — treating God as irrelevant, as unimportant, as a factor that does not need to be considered. The two readings — active opposition and passive dismissal — are not mutually exclusive. The disbeliever who worships idols is both: actively allied with forces that oppose God and passively dismissive of God's claim on their worship. The combination is the complete form of *kufr*: the denial that is both active and passive, both opposition and indifference.

**The Verse's Placement in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 25:55 closes the section that began with the natural signs in 25:45. The sūrah has moved from shade to night to sleep to day to wind to rain to the revival of dead land to the two seas to the creation of human beings — and then, immediately after all of this evidence of divine power, it records the disbelievers' response: they worship what cannot benefit or harm. The placement is the sūrah's final indictment: after every sign has been presented, after every argument has been made, after every natural phenomenon has been identified as evidence of God's sovereignty, the disbelievers' response is to continue worshipping objects that possess none of the power they have just witnessed. The refusal is not based on ignorance; it is based on will. The disbelievers have seen the signs and chosen to ignore them, and the ignoring is the collaboration against their Lord that the verse names.

**The Theological Summary**

Verse 25:55 serves as the sūrah's summary statement on idolatry. After demonstrating God's power through the catalogue of natural signs (25:45-54), the sūrah presents the disbelievers' response: they worship what cannot benefit or harm them. The placement is the sūrah's final indictment: the disbelievers have seen the signs and chosen to ignore them. The theological conclusion is stark: idolatry is not a mistake but a choice, and the choice is made in the full presence of the evidence that refutes it. The disbelievers who worship idols in the shadow of the signs the sūrah has described are not victims of misinformation; they are agents of denial, and the denial is the collaboration against their Lord that the verse names.


**The Disbeliever's Alliance Against His Own Interest**

The word *ẓahīr* (helper, ally, partisan) in 25:55 describes the disbeliever as someone who is helping a cause — but the cause is against his own Lord. The alliance is therefore self-destructive: the disbeliever is using his own power, his own resources, his own social influence to support the forces that oppose his Creator. The self-destruction is not accidental but inherent in the nature of shirk (associating others with God): to worship something besides God is to direct one's loyalty toward a target that cannot reciprocate. The idols cannot protect the disbeliever, cannot benefit him, cannot intercede for him on the Day of Judgment (25:3). The alliance with them is therefore an alliance with nothing — a partnership with entities that will abandon their worshippers at the moment of greatest need (25:17-19). The disbeliever's collaboration against his Lord is, in the final analysis, collaboration against himself, because the Lord he opposes is the only one who can save him. The sūrah's placement of this statement after the catalogue of natural signs makes the self-destruction visible: the disbeliever has seen the evidence of God's power and chosen to ally himself with what cannot match it. The choice is the error, and the error is the collaboration the verse names.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:56

> And We have sent you ˹O Prophet˺ only as a deliverer of good news and a warner

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Functions of Prophethood**

The verse defines the Prophet's mission in its simplest terms: *wa mā arsalnāka illā mubashshiran wa nadhīrā* — "And We have sent you only as a bearer of good news and a warner." The two nouns — *mubashshir* (bearer of good news) and *nadhīr* (warner) — name the two faces of the prophetic mission. The first face is turned toward those who believe: the Prophet brings them the good news (*bishāra*) of God's mercy, forgiveness, and the promise of Paradise. The second face is turned toward those who disbelieve: the Prophet brings them the warning (*indhār*) of God's judgment, punishment, and the reality of the fire. The two functions are not in tension; they are complementary. The good news is meaningless without the warning — because without the threat of punishment, the promise of reward has no urgency — and the warning is cruel without the good news — because without the possibility of forgiveness, the threat of punishment has no mercy.

The same pairing appears throughout the Qur'an. In 5:19 — "O People of the Scripture, there has come to you Our Messenger making clear to you much of what you used to conceal of the Scripture and overlooking much. There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book" — the Prophet is presented as a source of clarity. In 7:188 — "Say, 'I hold not for myself benefit or harm, except what Allah has willed. And if I knew the unseen, I could have acquired much wealth, and no harm would have touched me. I am not except a warner and a bringer of good tidings to a people who believe'" — the Prophet explicitly limits his role to these two functions. The limitation is itself a theological statement: the Prophet is not a judge, not a king, not a miracle-worker — he is a warner and a bearer of good news, and his authority extends only to the delivery of the message.

**The Word "Only" as a Limitation and an Honor**

The word *illā* ("only," "except") in *mā arsalnāka illā* is both a limitation and an honor. It limits the Prophet's role: he is not responsible for the outcome of his message. He delivers the good news and the warning, and the response belongs to the recipient, not to the messenger. The Prophet cannot force belief; he can only present the evidence and invite the choice. The limitation is a relief — it removes the burden of responsibility for the disbelievers' rejection — and it is also a clarification of the prophetic function. The Prophet is not a salesman trying to close a deal; he is a witness testifying to what he has seen and heard.

But *illā* is also an honor: it defines the Prophet's role as exclusively this — a bearer of good news and a warner. The exclusivity means that these two functions are sufficient. The Prophet does not need to be anything else. He does not need to be a philosopher, a politician, or a general. His mission is complete in the delivery of good news and warning, and the delivery is the *jihād* of 25:52 — the great striving with the Quran. The simplicity of the definition is the sūrah's way of returning to the basics after the complexity of its arguments: the Prophet's mission is not complicated. It is the delivery of two messages — one of hope, one of fear — and the messages are contained in a single book.

**The Verse's Placement After the Natural Signs**

Verse 25:56 follows the catalogue of natural signs (25:45-55) and returns the sūrah to its core subject: the Prophet and his message. The transition is from the general to the specific — from the signs that point to God in the natural world to the Prophet who speaks for God in the human world. The sūrah's structure is deliberate: it moves from the cosmic to the personal, from the evidence in creation to the evidence in revelation, and the two forms of evidence converge on the same conclusion — that God is real, His power is total, and His message is true. The Prophet who delivers the message is the human link between the cosmic evidence and the human audience, and his role — good news and warning — is the final form of the divine communication that the natural signs began.

**The Prophetic Limitation as the Message’s Strength**

The verse’s limitation of the Prophet’s role to good news and warning is not a diminishment but a strength. By defining the Prophet’s function precisely, the verse protects the message from being identified with the messenger’s personal preferences, political positions, or social opinions. The Prophet is not a philosopher offering his own insights; he is a deliverer conveying God’s message. The limitation ensures that the message stands on its own, independent of the messenger’s personality, and this independence is what makes the message universal. A message tied to a specific person’s worldview is limited by that person’s era, culture, and perspective. A message that comes from God and is delivered by a messenger who adds nothing of his own to it is limited by nothing — it speaks to every era, every culture, and every perspective. The Prophet’s role as mubashshir and nadhīr is therefore not a restriction but a liberation: it frees the message from the limitations of its human carrier and allows it to reach every human being who encounters it.

**The Verse’s Relationship to the Sūrah’s Opening**

Verse 25:56 returns to the sūrah’s opening theme: the Prophet’s mission. In 25:1, the Prophet is described as “a warner to the whole world.” In 25:56, he is described as “a deliverer of good news and a warner.” (25:56)” The two descriptions bookend the sūrah’s argument: the opening names the function, the closing confirms it, and everything between — the disbelievers’ objections, the eschatological warnings, the natural signs, the prophetic catalogues — is the content of the warning and the good news. The sūrah’s structure is thus circular: it begins with the Prophet’s mission and ends with the same mission, having demonstrated through seventy-seven verses that the mission is necessary, the message is true, and the consequences of accepting or rejecting it are real. The Prophet’s role as mubashshir and nadhīr is not a limitation but the fulfillment of the purpose named in the very first verse.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:57

> Say, “I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺, but whoever wishes, let them pursue the Way to their Lord.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Disinterestedness**

The verse records a statement the Prophet is commanded to make: *qul mā as'alukum ʿalayhi min ajrin* — "Say, 'I do not ask you for any reward for it.'" The declaration of disinterestedness is one of the Qur'an's most consistent themes in describing the prophetic mission. The same statement appears in 26:109, 127, 145, 164, and 180 — each time attributed to a different prophet (Noah, Hud, Salih, Lot, Shu'ayb) — and in 34:47 — "Say, 'Whatever reward I have asked of you — it is yours. My reward is only from Allah.'" The repetition across prophetic history establishes a principle: the prophetic mission is not a commercial enterprise. The Prophet does not profit from the delivery of the message. He does not seek wealth, status, political power, or social approval. He seeks nothing from his audience except their engagement with the message, and the engagement is for their benefit, not his.

The declaration serves a practical purpose in the sūrah's argument. The disbelievers had accused the Prophet of pursuing personal goals through his message — wealth, leadership, a share of the Quraysh's power. The declaration in 25:57 answers these accusations directly: the Prophet asks for nothing. The absence of a demand for reward is itself evidence of sincerity: a fabricator would seek compensation for his effort; a true messenger seeks nothing but the delivery of the truth. The disinterestedness is therefore not merely a moral quality but an argument — it is one of the proofs that the Prophet's mission is genuine.

**The Option of the Way**

The second half of the verse — *illā man shā'a an yattakhidha ilā Rabbihi sabīlā* — offers the audience an alternative: "but whoever wishes, let them take a way unto his Lord." The word *sabīl* (Way, path) appears here for the thirteenth time in the sūrah, maintaining the thread that runs through the entire chapter. The Way is available; the choice to walk it is offered; and the offer is made without compulsion. The Prophet does not demand that his audience follow the Way; he presents it and steps aside. The word *man shā'a* ("whoever wishes") introduces radical freedom: the choice belongs entirely to the individual. No one is forced, no one is coerced, and no one is punished for the mere act of choosing. The punishment comes from the consequences of the choice, not from the choice itself.

Al-Rāzī notes that the phrase *yattakhidha ilā Rabbihi sabīlā* — "take a way unto his Lord" — implies effort, initiative, and personal decision. The Way does not carry the person; the person must take the Way. The verb *yattakhidha* (to take, to adopt, to choose) names the active role of the individual: guidance is offered, but acceptance is a human act. The Prophet delivers the message; God provides the Way; and the human being must decide to walk it. The three roles — messenger, provider, and chooser — are distinct, and the verse respects the distinction by not conflating them. The Prophet does not walk the Way for the audience; God does not force them onto it; and the audience cannot blame either one if they choose not to take it.

**The Universality of the Prophetic Disinterestedness**

The declaration in 25:57 connects to the sūrah's earlier statement in 25:51 — "Had We willed, We would have sent a warner to every town." The two verses together define the Prophet's relationship with his audience: he is sent to them without their asking, he asks nothing from them in return, and he offers them a Way they are free to take or leave. The combination of selflessness and freedom is the prophetic model: the Prophet gives without expecting, and the audience receives without obligation. The only obligation is to the truth itself — to engage with it honestly, to consider it seriously, and to make a choice that reflects the reality of what has been presented. The Prophet's disinterestedness ensures that the choice is genuinely free: there is no personal motive to complicate the invitation, no hidden agenda to distrust, and no profit to be made from the audience's response. The message is pure, the offer is open, and the choice is theirs.

**The Choice Framed as Freedom**

The phrase man shā’a (whoever wishes) in 25:57 introduces radical freedom into the sūrah’s moral framework. The choice to follow the Way is presented as entirely voluntary: no compulsion, no pressure, no consequence attached to the mere act of choosing. The consequence comes from the content of the choice, not from the fact of choosing. This framing respects human agency in a way that the disbelievers’ social system did not: in Makkah, the choice to follow the Prophet was met with boycott, persecution, and social exclusion. The verse counteracts this by presenting the choice in its purest form — a free decision made by a free agent, unencumbered by social pressure or economic threat. The Prophet asks nothing (25:57); God compels nothing (2:256: “There is no compulsion in religion”); and the individual is left to decide for themselves. The freedom is not a weakness in the system but its foundation: faith that is compelled is not faith, and guidance that is forced is not guidance. The Way must be taken voluntarily, and the taking is the proof of the taker’s sincerity.

**The Verse’s Place in the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:57 follows the Prophet’s role definition (25:56) and precedes the command to trust in God (25:58). The sequence — mission, disinterestedness, trust — defines the Prophet’s posture in the world: he delivers the message without asking for anything in return, and he relies on God for the outcome. The disinterestedness is not indifference; it is the Prophet’s way of saying that the message belongs to God, not to him, and the reward for delivering it belongs to God, not to the audience. The Prophet’s only request is that the audience consider the Way — the path to their Lord — and the request is made without pressure, without condition, and without threat. The sūrah’s structure thus moves from the Prophet’s selflessness (25:57) to his trust in God (25:58), and the movement defines the prophetic character: a person who gives without expecting and trusts without seeing, who delivers the message and leaves the outcome to the One who sent it.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:58

> Put your trust in the Ever-Living, Who never dies, and glorify His praises. Sufficient is He as All-Aware of the sins of His servants

**Expanded Commentary**

**Trust in the Living God**

The verse addresses the Prophet with a command: *wa tawakkal ʿalā'l-ḥayyī'lladhī lā yamūtu* — "And put your trust in the Ever-Living, Who does not die." The word *tawakkal* (to trust, to rely upon, to place one's affairs in the hands of) names a specific form of faith: not merely belief in God's existence but active reliance on God's care. The Prophet is commanded to trust — to release his grip on his own plans and strategies and place the outcome of his mission in God's hands. The command is addressed to the Prophet personally, but it applies to every believer who faces opposition, uncertainty, or the temptation to compromise.

The description of God as *al-ḥayy* (the Ever-Living) is paired with the negation *alladhī lā yamūtu* (Who does not die). The combination is a theological statement of the highest order: God is alive, and His life is permanent. Everything else in creation is subject to death — every person, every empire, every civilization, every star. The only being whose life is not subject to death is God, and the command to trust in Him is grounded in this permanence. The Prophet's opponents — the disbelievers of Makkah, their idols, their social structures — will all die. The Prophet himself will die. The only being who will not die is the One the Prophet is told to trust, and the trust is therefore placed in the only thing that is truly permanent. The attribute *al-ḥayy* appears prominently in 2:255 — "Allah — there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of existence" — the verse known as Āyat al-Kursī, the most recited verse in the Qur'an.

**Glorifying God's Praises**

The command *wa sabbih bi-ḥamdihi* — "and glorify His praises" — pairs the act of trust with the act of worship. *Tasbīḥ* (glorification) is the declaration of God's transcendence above all deficiency; *ḥamd* (praise) is the declaration of His perfection in all attributes. The combination is a comprehensive form of worship: the believer declares that God is above everything negative and perfect in everything positive. The command to glorify is not merely liturgical; it is strategic. In the face of opposition, the Prophet is told not to argue, not to defend, not to strategize — but to glorify. The act of glorification is itself a form of resistance: it declares, in the face of the disbelievers' mockery, that the God they reject is above their mockery, beyond their reach, and sovereign over the outcome.

The pairing of trust and glorification appears in other Qur'anic passages. In 3:173 — "Those to whom people said, 'Indeed, the people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best disposer of affairs'" — the believers respond to threat with trust. In 33:41-42 — "O you who have believed, remember Allah with much remembrance and exalt Him morning and evening" — the command to remember God is given in the context of the Prophet's mission. The consistency of the pattern — trust and glorify, rely and praise — defines the believer's posture in the world: not passive but active, not resigned but confident, not silent but vocal in the declaration of God's greatness.

**Sufficient as All-Aware**

The verse closes with *wa kafā bihi bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihī khabīrā* — "Sufficient is He as All-Aware of the sins of His servants." The word *kafā* (sufficient) echoes 25:31 — "Sufficient is your Lord as a Guide and Helper" — and establishes a refrain: God is sufficient. The Prophet does not need the disbelievers' approval (25:31), does not need their reward (25:57), and does not need their help. God is sufficient — as Guide, as Helper, as Judge, and as Witness. The word *khabīr* (All-Aware, fully informed) names God's knowledge of the sins of His servants. The combination of sufficiency and awareness is both a comfort and a warning: God is enough for the Prophet, and God knows what the disbelievers do. The awareness is total — no sin is hidden, no mockery is unseen, and no rejection is unrecorded. The Day of Judgment will make visible what God already knows, and the knowledge is the ground of the justice that the Day will deliver.

**The Ever-Living as the Ground of Trust**

The description of God as al-ḥayy (the Ever-Living) in 25:58 is the theological foundation for the command to trust. Trust requires reliability, and reliability requires permanence. A being that can die is a being that can fail; a being that cannot die is a being that cannot fail. The command to trust in the Ever-Living is therefore a command to place one’s reliance in the only being whose reliability is absolute. Every other source of support — wealth, power, allies, one’s own strength — is subject to decay, loss, and death. The Ever-Living is the only permanent source of support, and the trust placed in Him is the only trust that will not be betrayed. The attribute al-ḥayy appears in 2:255 — the Throne Verse — where it is paired with al-qayyūm (the Sustainer): “Allah — there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of existence. Neither drowsiness overtakes Him nor sleep.” The pairing establishes that God’s life is not passive but active: He lives, and His life sustains everything else. The trust the Prophet places in Him is therefore not a passive surrender but an active reliance on the One who sustains the entire created order. The command to trust in al-ḥayy is the sūrah's most concise statement of the proper human relationship with God.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:59

> ˹He is˺ the One Who created the heavens and the earth and everything in between in six Days, then established Himself on the Throne. ˹He is˺ the Most Compassionate! Ask ˹none other than˺ the All-Knowledgeable about Himself

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation in Six Days**

The verse returns to the theme of divine creation that has run through the sūrah since 25:2: *alladhī khalaqa's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍa wa mā baynahumā fī sittati ayyām* — "The One who created the heavens and the earth and what is between them in six days." The creation in six days is mentioned in multiple Qur'anic passages — 7:54, 10:3, 11:7, 32:4, 50:38, and 57:4 — each time as an assertion of God's sovereign creative power. The number six is not arbitrary; it is the number the Qur'an consistently assigns to the act of creation, and the commentators debate whether the "days" are literal twenty-four-hour periods or longer epochs. Al-Rāzī notes that the question is essentially imponderable — God could have created in an instant or in a million years, and the choice of "six days" is a divine decision that human beings cannot fully comprehend. The point is not the duration but the fact: the creation was deliberate, ordered, and complete.

The phrase *wa mā baynahumā* ("and what is between them") encompasses everything in the created order that is not the heavens or the earth — the stars, the planets, the atmospheric phenomena, the invisible realms, the entire cosmos that exists between the sky above and the ground below. The inclusiveness of the phrase is the sūrah's way of saying: God created everything, not merely the parts that are visible. The disbelievers who acknowledge God's role in some aspects of creation but deny it in others are reminded that the scope of creation is total. There is nothing that falls outside the domain of divine creative power.

**The Throne and Divine Sovereignty**

The phrase *thumma istawā ʿalā'l-ʿarsh* — "then He established Himself on the Throne" — is one of the most debated phrases in Qur'anic theology. The word *istawā* can mean "established Himself," "mounted," "sat upon," or "assumed authority over," and the commentators differ on which meaning is intended. Al-Rāzī records the positions: some take the phrase literally, understanding it as a description of God's physical relationship to the Throne; others take it metaphorically, understanding it as a declaration of God's sovereignty over the created order. The Ash'arite position emphasizes that *istawā* should be understood without asking "how" (*bi-lā kayf*): the meaning is affirmed as stated, but the manner is beyond human comprehension.

The Throne (*ʿarsh*) in the Qur'anic cosmology is the greatest of God's creations — the canopy that encompasses the entire cosmos, above the heavens and the earth. Its mention in 25:59 is not incidental; it establishes the hierarchy of creation. Everything below the Throne — the heavens, the earth, and what is between them — was created in six days. The Throne itself was created before or alongside them, and God's establishment upon it marks the completion of creation and the beginning of divine governance. The message is sovereignty: the God who created everything rules everything, and the rule is exercised from the position of absolute authority.

**Al-Raḥmān: Ask About Him**

The verse ends with a striking instruction: *fa-s'al bihi khabīrā* — "So ask about Him, the All-Aware" or "Ask about Him, One who is aware." The instruction can be read in two ways. First, "Ask about the Most Compassionate" — the Prophet is directed to seek knowledge about God from the One who knows, that is, from God Himself. Second, "Ask about it" — the Prophet is directed to ask about the creation of the heavens and the earth from someone who is knowledgeable, that is, from God or from the scholars who understand such matters. Al-Rāzī notes that the first reading is more likely if the instruction is addressed to the Prophet, since the Prophet would not need to ask a human being about God. The instruction is therefore a command to seek knowledge of God from God — to turn to revelation, to prayer, to the direct relationship between the servant and the Lord. The Most Compassionate is the subject of the question, and the answer comes from the One who knows Himself best.

**The Creation and the Throne: The Architecture of Sovereignty**

The verse’s sequence — creation in six days, then establishment on the Throne — describes the architecture of divine sovereignty. The creation is the act; the Throne is the seat of governance. God created the heavens and the earth and everything between them, and then He assumed authority over the created order from the position of the Throne. The sequence implies that creation was not an end in itself but a preparation for governance: the cosmos was built so that it could be governed, and the governance is exercised from the Throne. The mention of al-Raḥmān (the Most Compassionate) immediately after the Throne creates a specific theological claim: the sovereignty exercised from the Throne is not arbitrary or tyrannical but compassionate. The God who rules the cosmos rules it with mercy, and the mercy is the framework within which judgment occurs. The disbelievers who refuse to prostrate to al-Raḥmān (25:60) are refusing to acknowledge the compassionate nature of the sovereignty that governs their lives.

**The Command to Ask About God**

The instruction “Ask about Him, the All-Knowledgeable” in 25:59 is a command to seek knowledge of God through the proper channels. The Prophet is not expected to know everything about God through his own reasoning; he is directed to ask — to seek revelation, to turn to the source of knowledge that is God Himself. The instruction is also a response to the disbelievers’ demands for information: they asked about the nature of God, about the meaning of the Most Compassionate, about the evidence for His existence. The answer is: ask the One who knows. The command to ask is a command to trust the revelation as the primary source of knowledge about God, and the trust is the foundation of the prophetic mission. The Prophet does not invent his knowledge of God; he receives it through revelation, and the revelation is the All-Knowledgeable’s self-disclosure. The sūrah’s placement of this instruction after the description of creation (25:59) and before the disbelievers’ refusal to prostrate (25:60) creates a specific message: the knowledge of God is available to anyone who asks, and the refusal to ask is the refusal to know.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:60

> When it is said to them, “Prostrate to the Most Compassionate,” they ask ˹in disgust˺, “What is ‘the Most Compassionate’? Will we prostrate to whatever you order us to?” And it only drives them farther away

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal to Prostrate**

The verse describes the disbelievers' response to the command to prostrate before *al-Raḥmān* (the Most Compassionate): *wa idhā qīla lahumu'sjudū li'l-Raḥmāni qālū wa mā'r-Raḥmān* — "When it is said to them, 'Prostrate to the Most Compassionate,' they say, 'What is the Most Compassionate?'" The question is not genuine inquiry but contempt. The disbelievers are not asking for information; they are expressing their refusal to acknowledge a name for God that they do not recognize. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the pre-Islamic Arabs were familiar with the name *Allāh* but not with the name *al-Raḥmān*. At the signing of the Treaty of Hudaybiyyah, the Quraysh negotiators objected when the Prophet instructed that the treaty be written "In the Name of God, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful," saying they knew neither of those names. The objection was not theological but cultural: the name *al-Raḥmān* was unfamiliar, and unfamiliarity was treated as illegitimacy.

The disbelievers' question — *a-nasjudu limā ta'murunā* — "Shall we prostrate to whatever you command us?" — adds a layer of defiance. The disbelievers are not merely refusing the command; they are questioning the authority behind it. The implicit accusation is that the Prophet is inventing a god — imposing a name that their tradition does not support, demanding worship of a being they do not recognize. The refusal is therefore not a theological disagreement about the nature of God but a social rebellion against the Prophet's authority to redefine the terms of worship. The disbelievers' gods have names they know, rituals they inherited, and a place in their social order. *Al-Raḥmān* has none of these — and the absence, for the disbelievers, is reason enough to refuse.

**The Name al-Raḥmān and Its Significance**

The name *al-Raḥmān* (the Most Compassionate) is the intensive form of *raḥmah* (mercy, compassion), and it carries a force that exceeds the more common *al-Raḥīm* (the Most Merciful). *Al-Raḥmān* names the quality of compassion that pervades all of creation — the compassion that sends rain (25:48), that creates night for rest (25:47), that extends the shade (25:45), and that provides for every living thing (25:49). The sūrah has spent the last fifteen verses demonstrating this compassion through the catalogue of natural signs, and the command to prostrate before *al-Raḥmān* is the logical conclusion of the demonstration: the One who does all of this deserves prostration.

The disbelievers' refusal to prostrate to *al-Raḥmān* is therefore not merely a refusal to obey a command; it is a refusal to acknowledge the evidence the sūrah has just presented. They have been shown the shade, the night, the day, the wind, the rain, the seas, and the creation of human beings — and their response is "What is the Most Compassionate?" The question, coming after fifteen verses of evidence, is not ignorance; it is willful denial. The evidence is in front of them, and they refuse to see it.

**The Effect: Increased Aversion**

The verse closes with *wa zādahum nufūrā* — "And it only increases them in aversion." The word *nufūr* means aversion, recoil, the instinctive withdrawal from something repulsive. The command to prostrate does not soften the disbelievers; it hardens them. The more they are told about God's compassion, the more they recoil from the idea of submitting to it. The mechanism is psychological: the command to prostrate implies submission, and submission implies the loss of autonomy, and the loss of autonomy is, for the disbelievers, intolerable. They would rather reject the evidence, deny the name, and refuse the prostration than accept the submission that prostration requires. The word *nufūr* names the instinct — the animal reflex of recoiling from a perceived threat — and the sūrah's placement of this instinct at the end of the natural signs section is the final diagnosis: the disbelievers have seen every sign the sūrah can offer, and their response is to recoil. The evidence does not produce faith; it produces aversion, because the faith the evidence demands is the faith the disbelievers refuse to give. The problem was never the evidence. The problem was always the submission.

**The Cultural Barrier to Recognition**

The disbelievers’ question — “What is the Most Compassionate?” — reveals a cultural barrier to recognition. The name al-Raḥmān was unfamiliar to the pre-Islamic Arabs, who were accustomed to the name Allāh but not to this particular divine attribute. The unfamiliarity was treated as illegitimacy: because the name was not part of their tradition, it could not be part of their worship. The cultural barrier is one the Qur’an addresses repeatedly: the disbelievers judge truth by familiarity rather than by evidence, and what is unfamiliar is rejected regardless of its merits. The same barrier appears in the disbelievers’ rejection of the Prophet himself: he is from their own tribe, speaks their own language, and lives in their own city, but because his message is unfamiliar, it is rejected. The verse’s closing statement — “it only drives them farther away” (25:60)” — names the effect of the cultural barrier: the unfamiliar does not attract; it repels. And the repulsion is not rational but instinctive — the nufūr (aversion) of people whose attachment to the familiar is stronger than their attachment to the true.

**The Verse’s Role in the Sūrah’s Argument**

Verse 25:60 completes the section that began with the description of creation in 25:59. The God who created the heavens and the earth and established Himself on the Throne is the same God the disbelievers refuse to prostrate to. The connection between the two verses is the sūrah’s way of saying: the God who has the power to create the cosmos deserves prostration, and the refusal to prostrate is the most extreme form of the arrogance (istikbār) named in 25:21. The disbelievers’ question — “What is the Most Compassionate?” — is, after fifteen verses of natural signs (25:45-59), an act of willful ignorance. They have been shown the shade, the night, the day, the wind, the rain, the seas, the creation of human beings, and the sovereignty of the Throne — and their response is to ask the name of the One who did all of it. The sūrah does not answer the question because the question is not genuine; it is the disbelievers’ final refusal, the last barrier between them and the truth, and the barrier is made not of stone or water but of will.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:61

> Blessed is the One Who has placed constellations in the sky, as well as a ˹radiant˺ lamp and a luminous moon

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return of Tabāraka**

The verse opens with *tabāraka* — "Blessed is" — the same word that opened the sūrah in 25:1. The repetition creates a structural frame: the sūrah begins and ends its catalogue of signs with the same declaration of divine praise. The word *tabāraka* carries the sense of continuous increase in goodness, and its appearance in 25:61, after sixty verses of argument, warning, and evidence, returns the reader to the starting point. Everything that has been said — the disbelievers' objections, the fire, the garden, the prophetic catalogue, the natural signs — is placed under the umbrella of a God whose nature is blessing. The sūrah's argument is not made from a position of anger but from a position of praise: the God who warns is the God who blesses, and the blessing is visible in the sky above.

The specific signs mentioned — constellations (*burūj*), a lamp (*sirāj*), and a luminous moon (*nūr*) — are the most visible features of the night sky. The constellations mark the fixed patterns against which the celestial bodies move; the lamp is the sun, which provides the primary light; and the moon reflects that light in a softer, gentler form. The three together constitute the visible architecture of the heavens, and the sūrah names them as signs of the One who placed them there. The word *burūj* (constellations) appears elsewhere in 15:16 and 85:1, each time as a marker of God's creative power. The constellations are not deities, not influences on human fate, not arbiters of fortune — they are decorations placed by God in the sky for human observation and reflection.

The celestial bodies named in this verse were objects of worship in the pre-Islamic Arabian world. The sun and the moon were associated with various deities, and the constellations were used in divination and astrology. The sūrah's declaration that these objects are signs (*āyāt*) of God — not gods themselves — is a direct challenge to the polytheistic worldview. The constellations do not control human destiny; they decorate the sky. The lamp does not demand worship; it illuminates the earth. The moon does not arbitrate fortune; it marks the months. The sūrah's use of *tabāraka* at the beginning of the verse reasserts the sovereignty of the One who created these objects and placed them in their positions: the blessing belongs to God alone, and the objects in the sky are evidence of His power, not rivals to it.

**The Sun as a Lamp and the Moon as Light**

The distinction between the sun (*sirāj*, lamp) and the moon (*nūr*, light) is both physical and theological. The sun produces its own light through nuclear fusion; the moon reflects the sun's light. The Qur'anic vocabulary captures this distinction: a *sirāj* is a source of illumination, a burning lamp; a *nūr* is a derived light, a reflection. The distinction appears in 71:16 — "And made the moon a light among them, and made the sun a lamp" — and in 78:13 — "And made a blazing lamp." The consistency of the vocabulary across the Qur'an establishes a cosmological framework in which the sun and the moon are not equals but complementary: one produces, the other receives; one burns, the other glows. The theological implication is that creation is hierarchical: some things are sources, and some things are reflections, and the hierarchy is part of the design.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that some commentators read *sirāj* in the plural (*suruj*) and interpret it as the stars rather than the sun. In this reading, the verse names three categories of celestial objects: constellations (the fixed patterns), stars (the individual points of light), and the moon (the reflected light). The reading is supported by 71:16, where the moon is described as a *nūr* (light) among them — "them" referring to the stars. Either reading produces the same theological conclusion: the night sky is a sign of divine creative power, and the objects that fill it are not autonomous but created, not self-generating but placed, and not purposeless but designed for the benefit of the creatures who observe them from below.

**Constellations as Navigational and Spiritual Markers**

The *burūj* (constellations) named in this verse served a practical function in the Arabian world: navigation. The desert traveler, crossing vast expanses of featureless terrain, relied on the positions of the stars and the patterns of the constellations to determine direction and location. The Qur'an acknowledges this function in 6:97 — "And it is He who has made the stars for you, that you may be guided by them through the darkness of the land and sea" — and in 16:16 — "and landmarks. And by the stars they are guided." The constellations are therefore signs in the double sense: they point to God (theological function) and they point the way (practical function). The same stars that declare God's power also guide the lost traveler, and the dual function is itself a sign — a sign that the universe is designed for the benefit of its inhabitants.

The word *burūj* also carries architectural connotations: the constellations are like towers or fortresses in the sky, fixed and immovable, forming the framework within which the planets and the moon move. The image is of a structured sky — not a random scattering of points but an ordered arrangement of patterns, each with its own name, its own position, and its own role in the cosmic architecture. The sūrah's naming of the constellations alongside the lamp and the moon presents the sky as a complete system: the fixed framework (constellations), the primary light source (lamp), and the secondary light source (moon) work together to produce the night sky that human beings observe. The system is the sign, and the sign points to the System-Maker.

**The Verse's Role in the Sūrah's Final Sign Catalogue**

Verse 25:61 opens the sūrah's final section — a brief catalogue of cosmic and natural signs that prepares for the closing address to the Prophet in 25:77. The section moves from the sky (25:61) to the alternation of day and night (25:62) to the character of God's true servants (25:63-76) to the Prophet's final declaration (25:77). The movement is from the cosmic to the moral, from the signs in the heavens to the signs in human character. The sūrah's method is consistent: it demonstrates God's power through the observable world and then shows what that power demands of the human beings who observe it. The constellations, the lamp, and the moon are the last natural signs in the sūrah, and they prepare the reader for the description of the servants of the Most Compassionate — the people who observe the signs and respond with the humility, prayer, and generosity that the signs call for.

The transition from the celestial signs to the portrait of the servants is seamless. The sky declares God's power; the servants respond with humility. The constellations mark the framework of the heavens; the servants mark the framework of a righteous society. The lamp illuminates the earth; the servants illuminate their communities through prayer and example. The moon reflects the sun's light; the servants reflect God's attributes in their character. The correspondence between the cosmic signs and the human signs is the sūrah's way of saying that the universe is a mirror: what is visible in the sky is also visible in the hearts of the faithful, and both are signs of the same God.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:62

> And He is the One Who causes the day and the night to alternate, ˹as a sign˺ for whoever desires to be mindful or to be grateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Alternation as a Sign for the Willing**

The verse describes the alternation of day and night — *yukhlifu bayna'l-layli wa'n-nahār* — as a sign (*āyah*) for two kinds of people: whoever desires to be mindful (*yattadhakkar*) and whoever desires to be grateful (*yashkur*). The two categories are not identical but complementary: mindfulness is the recognition of the truth; gratitude is the response to the recognition. The person who observes the alternation of day and night and reflects on its cause — who recognizes that the transition from darkness to light and back again is not random but designed — practices *tadhakkur* (mindfulness). The person who then acknowledges the Designer and thanks Him for the provision of time — time to work, time to rest, time to worship — practices *shukr* (gratitude). The two together constitute the complete response to a divine sign: see it, understand it, and thank the One who made it.

The word *yattadhakkar* (to be mindful, to remember, to reflect) is the same root as *tadhakkur* in 25:50, where the distribution of rain is described as a sign "so they may be mindful." The repetition connects the two passages: the rain is a sign that invites mindfulness, and the alternation of day and night is a sign that invites mindfulness. The sūrah's catalogue of signs — rain, shade, night, day, wind, seas, constellations — is unified by the single purpose of *tadhakkur*: every sign exists to provoke the same response, and the response is the beginning of faith.

The alternation itself is a profound sign. The transition from day to night and back again is not instantaneous but gradual: the dawn brightens slowly, the dusk fades gently, and the change is so smooth that the human observer barely notices the moment of transition. The gradualness is part of the design: God does not switch the lights on and off abruptly but dims and brightens them with the care of a host who wants his guests to be comfortable. The alternation provides balance — enough light for work and enough darkness for rest — and the balance is the sign that invites mindfulness and gratitude. The person who thinks about why the day and night are structured as they are — why the hours of light are balanced against the hours of darkness, why the seasons shift, why the solstices and equinoxes occur with mathematical precision — is a person who is practicing *tadhakkur*.

**The Verse's Implication: Signs Require Willingness**

The phrase *liman urīda an yattadhakkar aw urīda an yashkur* — "for whoever desires to be mindful or to be grateful" — introduces a crucial condition: the sign is effective only for those who are willing to receive it. The alternation of day and night is universal — it happens everywhere, to everyone, regardless of belief or disbelief. But the sign's power to produce mindfulness or gratitude depends on the disposition of the observer. A person who observes the alternation with indifference sees only a natural phenomenon; a person who observes it with willingness sees a divine communication. The condition is not in the sign but in the receiver, and the sūrah's consistent message is that the disbelievers' failure to see the signs is not a failure of the signs but a failure of the will.

Al-Rāzī observes that the verse's use of *urīda* (was desired, was willed) in the passive voice leaves the agent unnamed. The desire to be mindful or grateful can be understood as the human being's own desire — the person who chooses to reflect — or as God's desire — the God who wills that His signs be recognized by those who are open to them. The ambiguity is productive: both readings are true. The human being must choose to be mindful, and God must will the mindfulness to be effective. The cooperation of human will and divine will is the mechanism by which a sign becomes a source of guidance.

**The Day and Night as a Framework for Worship**

The alternation of day and night is not only a sign to observe but also a framework within which worship occurs. The five daily prayers are distributed across the hours of light and darkness: *Fajr* at dawn, *Ẓuhr* at midday, *ʿAṣr* in the afternoon, *Maghrib* at sunset, and *ʿIshāʾ* at night. The distribution ensures that the Muslim's day is punctuated by worship at every transition — from darkness to light, from light to darkness, from work to rest. The alternation of day and night thus provides not only the sign but also the structure: the hours of the day are the hours of worship, and the alternation is the rhythm that organizes the worshipper's life. The sūrah's naming of the alternation as a sign "for whoever desires to be mindful or to be grateful" connects the cosmic phenomenon to the human practice: the alternation is the sign, and the prayer is the response.

In 17:78 — "Establish prayer at the sun's decline until the darkness of the night, and recite at dawn — indeed, the recitation of dawn is witnessed" — the Qur'an specifies the times of prayer by reference to the movement of the sun and the onset of darkness. The alternation of day and night is thus the temporal framework within which the Muslim community organizes its worship, and the framework is itself a sign of God's design. The person who prays at the appointed times is responding to the sign in the most direct way possible: not merely observing the alternation but using it, not merely being grateful but expressing the gratitude in action.

**The Verse's Placement Before the Servants of the Compassionate**

Verse 25:62 serves as a bridge between the natural signs (25:61) and the description of God's true servants (25:63-76). The alternation of day and night is the last of the cosmic signs, and the two qualities it is designed to produce — mindfulness and gratitude — are the qualities that define the servants of the Most Compassionate. The servants described in 25:63-76 are people who have responded to the signs correctly: they walk humbly (25:63), they pray at night (25:64), they ask God to protect them from Hell (25:65-66), they spend moderately (25:67), and they avoid the sins of idolatry, murder, and fornication (25:68). Every quality they possess is a response to the signs the sūrah has described: the humility comes from recognizing God's power in the natural world; the prayer comes from recognizing God's generosity in the provision of sustenance; the moderation comes from recognizing God's design in the balance of creation. The servants of the Compassionate are the living proof that the signs work — for those who are willing.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:63

> The ˹true˺ servants of the Most Compassionate are those who walk on the earth humbly, and when the foolish address them ˹improperly˺, they only respond with peace

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Portrait of the Servants**

The verse introduces the sūrah's most sustained portrait of ideal human character: *ʿibādu'l-Raḥmān* — "the servants of the Most Compassionate." The name *al-Raḥmān* (the Most Compassionate) connects this portrait to the sūrah's earlier uses of the same name: in 25:26, true sovereignty belongs to the Most Compassionate on the Day of Judgment; in 25:59, God is described as the Most Compassionate who created the heavens and the earth; and in 25:60, the disbelievers refuse to prostrate to the Most Compassionate. The servants of the Most Compassionate are the human response to the divine attribute: they are the people who recognize the compassion behind creation and model their lives on it.

The portrait that begins here and extends through 25:76 is one of the Qur'an's most celebrated passages. It has been used as a template for Muslim character formation for fourteen centuries, and its influence on Islamic ethics is incalculable. The portrait does not describe a theoretical ideal but a lived reality: the servants walk, speak, pray, spend, and interact with others in specific ways that are recognizable and replicable. The sūrah's method is not to prescribe abstract principles but to describe concrete behaviors, and the behaviors together constitute a character that is both spiritual and practical.

**Humility in Walking**

The first quality named is *humility in walking* — *yamshūna ʿalā'l-arḍi hawnan*. The word *hawn* means humility, gentleness, a lack of arrogance in one's bearing. The commentators note that this does not mean weakness: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb once encountered a young man walking with exaggerated meekness and told him to walk with strength, because humility is not the absence of vigor but the absence of pride. The instruction is about attitude, not posture: the servants of the Compassionate move through the world without the self-importance that the disbelievers displayed in 25:21 — those who "have been carried away by their arrogance and have entirely exceeded all limits."

The specification that the servants walk "on the earth" (*ʿalā'l-arḍ*) is significant. The earth is the stage on which human life is performed, and the servants' humility is displayed on that stage — in public, in the presence of others, in the ordinary interactions of daily life. The humility is not private; it is visible. The servants do not retreat to monasteries or hide in caves; they walk among people, and their walking is itself a sign — a sign that the one who recognizes God does not need to assert dominance over other human beings. The contrast with 25:21 is the sūrah's way of showing the two modes of being: the disbelievers walk with arrogance and exceed all limits; the servants walk with humility and maintain all limits. The contrast is the Criterion (25:1) in action.

**The Response to the Ignorant**

The second quality is the response to provocation: *wa idhā khāṭabahumu'l-jāhilūna qālū salāmān* — "and when the foolish address them improperly, they only respond with peace." The word *jāhilūn* (the ignorant, the foolish) names the provocateurs: people who lack understanding, who respond to the servants' humility with mockery, insult, or aggression. The word *salām* (peace) names the response: the servants do not retaliate, do not argue, do not match insult with insult. They say "peace" — not as a greeting but as a declaration of disengagement. They refuse to enter the cycle of provocation and retaliation.

Al-Qurṭubī records a debate about the meaning of *salām* in this context. Some commentators understand it as a greeting — the servants wish peace upon those who insult them, which is an act of extraordinary generosity. Others understand it as a declaration of disassociation — "I am free of you; there is nothing between us" — which is an act of dignified withdrawal. Al-Qurṭubī himself argues that the verse is not a legal command subject to abrogation but an attitude of restraint and forbearance that transcends the specific circumstances of conflict. The servants of the Compassionate do not engage with foolishness because engagement would lower them to the level of the foolish. Their response — peace — is a statement about who they are, not about who the provocateurs are.

**The Connection to the Sūrah's Earlier Descriptions of Mockery**

The portrait of the servants' response to the foolish connects directly to the sūrah's earlier descriptions of the disbelievers' mockery. In 25:41, the disbelievers see the Prophet and make fun of him. In 25:42, they claim he nearly led them astray from their gods. In 25:60, they respond to the command to prostrate with contempt. The servants of the Compassionate are the mirror image of the disbelievers: where the disbelievers mock, the servants say peace; where the disbelievers demand, the servants give; where the disbelievers are arrogant, the servants are humble. The portrait is not merely a list of virtues but a response to the specific vices the sūrah has described. The servants are what the disbelievers are not, and the contrast is the sūrah's way of showing what the Criterion (25:1) produces when it is accepted rather than rejected.

The portrait also connects to the broader Qur'anic ethic of patience (*ṣabr*). In 41:34-35 — "Good and evil are not equal. Repel ˹evil˺ with what is best, and then the one you are in a feud with will be like a close friend. But this cannot be attained except by those who are patient" — the Qur'an describes the transformation that patience produces: the enemy becomes a friend. The servants' response of peace is the beginning of that transformation: the fool who is met with peace may be moved to reconsider, and the reconsideration is the first step toward guidance. The servants do not argue because argument rarely persuades; they demonstrate, because demonstration always communicates.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:64

> ˹They are˺ those who spend ˹a good portion of˺ the night, prostrating themselves and standing before their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Night Vigil as the Mark of Devotion**

The verse describes the second quality of the servants of the Most Compassionate: their practice of spending the night in prayer — *alladhīna yabītūna li-Rabbihim sujjadan wa qiyāmān* — "those who pass the night before their Lord, prostrating and standing." The word *yabītūna* (they pass the night) specifies the time: these are not prayers performed in the convenience of daylight but in the solitude and silence of the night, when the world sleeps and the worshipper is alone with God. The two postures — *sujūd* (prostration) and *qiyām* (standing) — describe the physical acts of Islamic prayer: standing upright in recitation and bowing low in submission. The combination covers the full range of prayer's physical expression: the worshipper stands before God in attention and falls before God in humility.

The practice of night prayer (*tahajjud* or *qiyām al-layl*) is one of the most emphasized acts of devotion in the Qur'an. In 17:79 — "And rise from sleep during the night, as an extra offering of your own, so that your Lord may raise you to a praised station" — the Prophet is specifically commanded to pray at night. In 73:1-4 — "O you who wraps himself in clothing, arise to pray the night, except for a little" — the same command is given with even more urgency. The night prayer is not obligatory for the general community, but it is the mark of those whose devotion goes beyond the minimum. The servants of the Compassionate are identified by this practice because it reveals the depth of their relationship with God: they give God the time that is most precious — the hours of rest — and the sacrifice is the proof of their sincerity.

The phrase *li-Rabbihim* ("before their Lord") specifies the orientation of the prayer: the servants are not praying for show, not praying in public, not praying to be seen. They are praying before their Lord — in His presence, for His sake, in the privacy of the night when no human audience is present. The preposition *li* (for, before) names the relationship: the prayer is directed toward God and performed in His awareness. The absence of a human audience is the point: the night prayer reveals the worshipper's true relationship with God, because it is performed when no one else is watching. The servants of the Compassionate are the same in private as they are in public, and the night prayer is the evidence.

**The Social Contrast: Night Prayer vs. Night Entertainment**

The sūrah's placement of the night prayer in the portrait of the servants creates a silent contrast with the disbelievers' use of the same hours. The disbelievers of Makkah spent their nights in storytelling, poetry competitions, and social gatherings that served no spiritual purpose. The servants of the Compassionate spend their nights in prostration and standing before their Lord. The contrast is not stated but implied: the same hours, the same night, and radically different uses. The night is a test — the same test that the alternation of day and night (25:62) was designed to produce — and the servants pass the test by giving the night to God.

The contrast extends to the modern world as well. The night, in contemporary society, is the time of entertainment, consumption, and distraction. Television, social media, and the twenty-four-hour news cycle have turned the night into an extension of the day — a time of activity, stimulation, and engagement with the trivial. The servants of the Compassionate are people who resist this cultural pressure. They reclaim the night for worship, and in doing so, they demonstrate that the night is not for the world but for God. The practice of night prayer is therefore not only a personal devotion but a social statement: the servants refuse to give the night to the world, and the refusal is the proof of their independence.

**The Theology of Presence Before God**

The phrase *sujjadan wa qiyāmān* (prostrating and standing) describes two postures of prayer, and the two postures together express the full range of the worshipper's relationship with God. The standing posture (*qiyām*) is the posture of attention and address: the worshipper stands before God, recites His words, and speaks to Him. The prostration posture (*sujūd*) is the posture of submission and surrender: the worshipper falls before God, places the forehead on the ground, and acknowledges His sovereignty. The alternation between the two postures — standing and falling, address and submission — is the physical expression of the worshipper's theology: God is both the One who listens (the worshipper speaks to Him while standing) and the One who commands (the worshipper submits to Him while prostrating). The two postures together constitute the complete worship, and the completeness is the servants' answer to the partial worship of the disbelievers, who worshipped idols without standing before them or prostrating to them in the way God commanded.

**The Verse's Connection to the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:64 continues the portrait that began in 25:63. The first quality was humility in walking and patience with the foolish; the second quality is devotion in prayer. The two are related: the humility that characterizes the servants' public life is rooted in the submission that characterizes their private worship. A person who spends the night prostrating before God will not walk through the day with arrogance; a person who stands before God in the darkness will not respond to the foolish with anything but peace. The night prayer is the source, and the daytime virtues are the fruit. The sūrah's portrait of the servants is therefore not a random collection of virtues but a coherent character: the person who worships at night is the person who walks humbly by day, and the person who walks humbly by day is the person who says peace to the foolish. The character is whole, and the wholeness comes from the single source: submission to the Most Compassionate.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:65

> ˹They are˺ those who pray, “Our Lord! Keep the punishment of Hell away from us, for its punishment is indeed unrelenting

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer That Defines the Servants**

The verse records the servants' own words: *wa'lladhīna yaqūlūna Rabbanā'ṣrif ʿannā ʿadhāba jahannama* — "and those who say, 'Our Lord, avert from us the punishment of Hell.'" The prayer is addressed to God as *Rabbanā* (our Lord), a possessive form that expresses intimacy and dependence. The servants do not address God from a position of merit or entitlement; they address Him as children address a parent — with need, with vulnerability, with the recognition that their safety depends on His mercy. The word *aṣrif* (avert, turn away) is a request for protection, not a claim of immunity. The servants do not say "we deserve to be spared"; they say "spare us," and the request acknowledges that without God's intervention, they have no defense against the punishment.

The description of Hell's punishment as *gharām* — "unrelenting," "inescapable," "a lasting penalty" — carries a specific etymological weight. The word is related to *gharīm* (debtor) and to the idea of infatuation: just as a person who is infatuated with something cannot free themselves from it, the punishment of Hell clings to its victims and cannot be shaken off. Al-Zamakhsharī connects the word to the sense of being permanently attached: the punishment does not come and go; it adheres, it persists, it becomes part of the person who suffers it. The servants' use of this word in their prayer reveals their understanding of what Hell is: not a temporary correction but a permanent condition, and the permanence is what makes the prayer urgent.

**The Theology of Fear in the Servants' Portrait**

The servants' prayer in 25:65-66 introduces fear as a component of their spiritual character. They fear Hell — not abstractly but specifically, not theoretically but with the kind of concrete awareness that produces prayer. This fear is not the opposite of love; it is its companion. The servants love God (expressed through their worship) and fear God (expressed through their prayer for protection), and the two together constitute the complete relationship. The Qur'an consistently pairs fear and love as the two wings of faith: "Call upon Him in fear and hope" (7:56); "They used to hasten to good deeds and call upon Us in hope and fear" (21:90).
