The servants of the Compassionate are people who have not chosen one wing over the other; they fly with both.

The placement of this prayer after the night vigil (25:64) is significant. The servants who spend the night in prostration and standing are the same people who pray for protection from Hell. The night prayer is the context; the fear of Hell is the content. The servants' worship is not joyful only; it is also fearful. The night is not spent in celebration alone but in supplication, in the acknowledgment that the one who worships God most deeply is also the one who fears God most honestly. The relationship between devotion and fear is not paradoxical but causal: the closer one draws to God, the more one understands the gravity of sin, and the more one understands the gravity of sin, the more one prays for protection from its consequence.

**The Servants' Understanding of Hell's Reality**

The servants' prayer reveals that they take the descriptions of Hell literally and seriously. The sūrah has already described Hell in vivid terms: in 25:11-14, it presented the fire that sees and hears, the narrow confinement, the cry for destruction, and the chains and boiling water. The servants have heard these descriptions — or they have heard the Prophet's recitation of them — and they have responded with prayer. The response is the proof of their faith: they believe in the reality of Hell, and the belief produces the prayer. The disbelievers, by contrast, dismissed the descriptions as exaggeration or fiction (25:21: "We have never heard of this in any previous faith"). The difference between the servants and the disbelievers is not intelligence or education but disposition: the servants are open to the truth, and the disbelievers are closed.

**The Verse's Connection to the Sūrah's Eschatology**

The mention of Hell in 25:65 connects the servants' portrait to the sūrah's extended eschatological passages. In 25:11-14, the sūrah described the fire that sees, the narrow place, the cry for destruction, and the denial of annihilation. The servants' prayer in 25:65 is a direct response to that description: they have heard what Hell is, and they pray to be spared from it. The prayer is therefore not a generic supplication but a specific response to the sūrah's own warnings. The servants are the people who listened to the warnings, believed them, and acted on them. Their prayer is the proof that the warnings worked — not on everyone, but on those whose hearts were open to the truth.

The connection between 25:65 and the sūrah's earlier eschatological passages also reveals the Qur'anic method of building spiritual awareness through repetition. The descriptions of Hell in 25:11-14 were not intended to be read once and forgotten; they were intended to sink into the hearts of the listeners and produce a lasting change in behavior. The servants of the Compassionate are the evidence that the method works: they heard the descriptions, they internalized them, and the internalization produced the prayer. The prayer is the final step in a process that began with hearing, continued with believing, and culminated in supplication. The process is the sūrah's model of spiritual formation: hear the signs, believe them, and let the belief produce the action. The servants have completed the process, and the completion is the proof of their faith.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:66

> It is certainly an evil place to settle and reside.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Servants' Verdict on Hell**

The verse completes the servants' prayer that began in 25:65: *innahā sā'at mustaqarran wa muqāmān* — "Truly it is an evil place to settle and abode." The two words — *mustaqarr* (settlement, dwelling) and *muqām* (abode, place of residence) — are the same words used in 25:24 to describe the righteous in Paradise: "the residents of Paradise will have the best settlement (*mustaqarr*) and the finest place to rest (*maqīl*)." The verbal echo between the two verses is deliberate: the servants of the Compassionate, in their prayer for protection from Hell, use the same vocabulary that the sūrah used for Paradise. The effect is a comparison: what is the best dwelling for the righteous is the worst dwelling for the condemned. The same words — settlement and abode — describe two opposite realities, and the difference is the person who inhabits them.

The word *sā'at* (evil, wretched, terrible) describes the quality of Hell as a dwelling. The servants do not describe Hell's fire, its chains, its narrow places, or its sounds — all of which the sūrah has already described in 25:12-14. They describe it simply as an evil place to live. The simplicity is devastating: the most terrible thing about Hell is not the intensity of the punishment but the permanence of the residence. The servants understand that Hell is not a place one visits and leaves; it is a place one settles in, and the settlement is forever. The word *muqām* (abode) names the permanence: the inhabitant does not merely pass through Hell; they remain.

**The Rhetorical Power of the Verse's Brevity**

Verse 25:66 is among the shortest verses in the sūrah, consisting of only six words in Arabic. The brevity is itself a rhetorical device. After the extended description of Hell in 25:12-14 — the fire, the smoke, the narrow place, the cry — the servants' verdict is delivered in the most compressed form possible. The compression is effective: the servants do not need to repeat the details because the details have already been established. All they need to say is *sā'at* — "evil" — and the reader's memory fills in the rest. The brevity also conveys the servants' certainty: they do not hedge, do not qualify, do not soften the verdict. Hell is an evil place to settle, and the statement is final.

The verse's position as the conclusion of the servants' prayer adds to its force. The prayer began in 25:65 with the request for protection and the description of the punishment as *gharām* (unrelenting). The prayer closes in 25:66 with the verdict on Hell as a dwelling. The structure — request, description, verdict — is the structure of a legal argument: the servants present their case (we need protection), the evidence (the punishment is permanent), and the conclusion (Hell is an evil place to live). The argument is directed to God, who needs no persuasion, but the argument's structure is for the reader's benefit: it shows that the servants' fear of Hell is not emotional but reasoned, not irrational but grounded in the sūrah's own descriptions.

**The Contrast With the Garden**

The sūrah's structural pairing of 25:66 with 25:24 creates one of its most powerful juxtapositions. In 25:24, the righteous have "the best settlement and the finest place to rest." In 25:66, the condemned have "an evil place to settle and reside." The same nouns — settlement and dwelling — describe both outcomes, and the adjective is the only difference: best vs. evil. The juxtaposition is the sūrah's way of presenting the two options as a single choice with a single variable: where will you settle? The answer depends on what you do in this life — whether you walk humbly (25:63), pray at night (25:64), ask for protection from Hell (25:65), and live by the principles the sūrah describes. The choice is binary, the vocabulary is identical, and the difference is the life that precedes the settlement.

**The Servants' Awareness of the Afterlife**

The servants' prayer in 25:65-66 reveals their awareness of the afterlife and their understanding of its stakes. They do not pray for wealth, for status, for long life, or for worldly comfort. They pray for one thing: protection from Hell. The singularity of the request reveals the singularity of their concern: everything in this life is temporary, and the only thing that matters permanently is the destination. The servants of the Compassionate are people who have internalized the sūrah's central message — the denial of the Hour (25:11) is the root of every error — and who live their lives in the light of the Hour. Their prayer is the proof of their belief, and their belief is the foundation of their character. The servants do not fear Hell because they are weak; they fear Hell because they understand the gravity of what the sūrah has described, and the understanding produces the prayer.

The servants' awareness of the afterlife is also a mark of their intellectual maturity. The disbelievers of Makkah were not unintelligent people; they were merchants, poets, and leaders who understood the world around them. But their intelligence was limited to the material world — the world of trade, politics, and social status. The servants of the Compassionate possess a broader intelligence: they see beyond the material world to the world that awaits, and the vision shapes their choices. The prayer for protection from Hell is the fruit of this broader vision: the servants see what the disbelievers cannot see, and the vision produces the prayer. The sūrah's portrait of the servants is therefore not only a portrait of faith but a portrait of wisdom — the wisdom that comes from seeing the whole picture, not just the part that is visible to the physical eye.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:67

> ˹They are˺ those who spend neither wastefully nor stingily, but moderately in between

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Middle Way in Spending**

The verse describes the servants' relationship to wealth: *wa'lladhīna idhā anfaqū lam yusrifū wa lam yaqturū wa kāna bayna dhālika qawāmān* — "and those who, when they spend, are neither extravagant nor miserly, but maintain a middle way between them." The word *isrāf* (extravagance, wastefulness) names one extreme; the word *iqṭār* (miserliness, stinginess) names the other; and the word *qawām* (middle, balanced, upright) names the position between them. The servants are people who have found the balance — the point at which spending is generous enough to fulfill its purpose and restrained enough to preserve its sustainability.

The concept of the middle way (*wasaṭ*) is one of the Qur'an's most fundamental ethical principles. In 2:143 — "And thus We have made you a middle community" — the same root is used to describe the Muslim community as a whole. The middle way is not mediocrity; it is the point of maximum effectiveness, the position from which the most good can be done over the longest period. The extravagant person gives too much and soon has nothing left to give; the miserly person gives too little and accumulates what benefits no one. The servant of the Compassionate gives enough — enough to be generous, enough to be sustainable, and enough to maintain the capacity to give again.

**The Verse's Connection to the Sūrah's Earlier Warnings Against Excess**

The mention of *isrāf* (wastefulness) in 25:67 connects to the Qur'an's broader warnings against excess. In 7:31 — "Eat and drink, but do not be excessive. Indeed, He does not like those who commit excess" — the same word is used in the context of food and drink. In 17:26-27 — "And do not make your hand chained to your neck or extend it completely... Indeed, the wasteful are brothers of the devils" — extravagance is linked to moral corruption. The servants of the Compassionate are the opposite of the wasteful: they are measured, balanced, and deliberate in their spending. The measurement is not calculation but character — it flows naturally from the humility (25:63) and the devotion (25:64) that define their relationship with God.

The pairing of extravagance and miserliness as twin errors reflects a pervasive Qur'anic concern with the misuse of wealth. The Qur'an does not condemn wealth itself — Abraham and Solomon were wealthy — but it consistently condemns the extremes of its use. The wealthy person who wastes their wealth on luxury and display is guilty of *isrāf*; the wealthy person who hoards their wealth and refuses to share it is guilty of *iqṭār*. Both errors stem from the same root: a failure to understand that wealth is a trust (*amānah*), not a possession. The servants of the Compassionate understand this, and their spending reflects the understanding: they give what is due, they keep what is needed, and they maintain the balance that the trust requires.

**The Practical Nature of the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:67 adds a practical dimension to the portrait that has been building since 25:63. The servants walk humbly, respond to the foolish with peace, pray at night, ask for protection from Hell, and now spend moderately. The portrait moves from the spiritual (humility, prayer) to the practical (spending), and the movement demonstrates that the servants' character is not limited to private worship but extends to public conduct. The servants of the Compassionate are not monks; they are members of society who earn, spend, and interact with others. Their spirituality is not withdrawn from the world but expressed in the world — in the way they walk, the way they speak, and the way they handle money. The sūrah's portrait is therefore not an ideal that exists only in theory but a model that exists in practice, and the practice is the proof of the faith.

**The Word Qawām and Its Implications**

The word *qawām* — the middle way, the balanced position — is an intensive form that suggests the servants actively maintain the balance. The balance is not passive; it requires effort, vigilance, and continuous correction. The servants do not spend randomly or instinctively; they spend deliberately, with the awareness that every expenditure is a moral choice. The word *qawām* implies standing upright — the servant stands in the middle, not swaying to the left (extravagance) or the right (miserliness) but maintaining the upright position that the middle way requires. The image is of a person standing firm in a world of pressures: the pressure to consume, the pressure to hoard, the pressure to impress — and the servant resists all of them, standing in the position that God has defined as the correct one. The balance is the servant's answer to the disbelievers' obsession with wealth and status: the disbelievers measure themselves by what they have, while the servants measure themselves by how they use what they have.

The sūrah's portrait of the servants as moderate spenders also has implications for the social order. Extravagance and miserliness are not merely personal failings; they are social diseases. The extravagant person distorts the economy by spending beyond their means, creating artificial demand and inflating prices. The miserly person distorts the economy by hoarding wealth, removing it from circulation and depriving others of the opportunity to benefit from it. The servant of the Compassionate does neither: they spend what is appropriate, keeping the economy in balance and ensuring that their wealth benefits both themselves and their community. The moderation in spending is therefore not only a personal virtue but a social responsibility — the responsibility of the person who understands that wealth is a trust and the trust must be discharged with care and generosity.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:68

> ˹They are˺ those who do not invoke any other god besides Allah, nor take a ˹human˺ life—made sacred by Allah—except with ˹legal˺ right, nor commit fornication. And whoever does ˹any of˺ this will face the penalty

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Three Prohibitions**

The verse names three prohibitions that define the moral boundaries of the servants of the Compassionate: *shirk* (associating others with God), murder (taking a life sanctified by God without right), and *zinā* (fornication or adultery). The three are grouped together because they represent violations of the three fundamental relationships in human existence: the relationship with God (*shirk*), the relationship with other human beings (murder), and the relationship with the family and social order (fornication). The servants of the Compassionate are people who maintain all three relationships in their proper form: they worship God alone, they respect human life, and they preserve the integrity of the sexual and familial order.

The prohibition of *shirk* — "those who do not invoke any other god besides Allah" — returns the sūrah to its opening theme. The Criterion (25:1) was sent down to distinguish truth from falsehood, and the primary falsehood is the association of other beings with God. The servants of the Compassionate are the people who have accepted the Criterion and live by it: their worship is directed to God alone, and their refusal to invoke other gods is the foundation of their character. The prohibition of murder — "nor take a life made sacred by Allah except with right" — affirms the inviolability of human life. The word *ḥaram* (sacred, inviolable) describes the status of human life in God's law: it cannot be taken except in the specific circumstances that the law permits (capital punishment for capital crimes, self-defense in the face of aggression). The prohibition of fornication — "nor commit fornication" — protects the integrity of the family, the social unit that the sūrah described in 25:54 as divinely designed through kinship and marriage.

**The Sacredness of Human Life**

The phrase *illā bi'l-ḥaqq* ("except with right") is a qualifier that distinguishes lawful from unlawful killing. The Qur'an and the Prophetic tradition define the specific circumstances in which the taking of life is permitted: lawful warfare in defense of the community, the execution of a lawful sentence for a capital crime, and the defense of one's own life when under lethal attack. In all other circumstances, the taking of life is a violation of the sanctity that God has placed upon it. The word *ḥaram* (sacred) is the same word used for the inviolability of the Kaaba and the sacred months, and its use for human life places human beings under the same divine protection as the holiest sites in Islam. The implication is profound: every human life is sacred, and the sacredness is God-given, not socially constructed.

The Qur'an's treatment of murder elsewhere reinforces this principle. In 5:32 — "Whoever kills a soul — unless for a soul or for corruption in the land — it is as if he had slain mankind entirely. And whoever saves one, it is as if he had saved mankind entirely" — the value of a single life is equated with the value of all humanity. The servants of the Compassionate, by refusing to take life unlawfully, affirm this equation: they treat every human being as bearing the sacred status that God has granted, and they refuse to violate that status. The refusal is not passive; it is an active commitment to the principle that life is a trust, and the trust cannot be broken.

**The Penalty for Violation**

The verse closes with a warning: *wa-man yafʿal dhālika yalqā athāmā* — "and whoever does any of this will face the penalty." The word *athām* (penalty, punishment, sin) names the consequence. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *athām* can refer to the sin itself or to the punishment for the sin, and some commentators identify it as the name of a valley in Hell. The warning is general: the penalty applies to anyone who commits any of the three violations, regardless of their other qualities. A person who walks humbly and prays at night but associates others with God, murders, or commits fornication has violated the fundamental boundaries, and the violation produces its own consequence.

The linking of the three prohibitions into a single verse with a single consequence is significant. The verse does not rank the sins; it places them side by side and applies the same penalty to all three. The theological implication is that *shirk*, murder, and fornication are equally grave — not because they cause the same harm (they do not) but because they all violate the sacred boundaries that God has established. The servant who commits any one of them has broken the covenant that the portrait of the servants describes, and the broken covenant has consequences. The warning is not a threat but a statement of fact: the violations produce their own penalties, and the penalties are the natural consequence of the violation.

**The Verse's Role in the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:68 is the moral center of the servants' portrait. The portrait has described their virtues — humility (25:63), prayer (25:64-66), moderation (25:67) — and now it names the vices they avoid. The combination of positive qualities and negative prohibitions creates a complete picture: the servants are defined not only by what they do but by what they refuse to do. The refusal is as important as the action, because the prohibitions protect the foundations — faith, life, family — without which the virtues cannot stand. A person who is humble but idolatrous is humble in the service of a falsehood; a person who prays but murders is praying with blood on their hands; a person who spends moderately but commits fornication is moderating their wealth while violating their integrity. The prohibitions are the guardrails that keep the virtues on track, and the servants of the Compassionate are people who honor both.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:69

> Their punishment will be multiplied on the Day of Judgment, and they will remain in it forever, in disgrace

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Multiplication of Punishment**

The verse describes the consequence for those who violate the three prohibitions of 25:68: *yudāʿaf lahu'l-ʿadhābu yawma'l-qiyāmati* — "the punishment will be doubled for him on the Day of Judgment." The word *yudāʿaf* (doubled, multiplied) appears in other Qur'anic eschatological passages: in 11:20 — "For those who disbelieved and denied Our signs, those will be the companions of the Fire; they will abide therein, and for them the punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be reprieved" — and in 33:30 — "And whoever of you commits immorality, We will multiply the punishment for her, and that is easy for Allah." The multiplication is not arbitrary; it is proportional to the severity of the sin. The violations named in 25:68 — *shirk*, murder, fornication — are among the gravest sins in the Qur'anic moral framework, and the punishment corresponds to their gravity.

The theological question of how punishment can be "doubled" for someone who is already in Hell is addressed by several commentators. Al-Rāzī notes that the doubling can refer to the intensity of the punishment — the fire burns hotter, the confinement is tighter, the disgrace is deeper — or to its duration — the punishment lasts longer, with no relief, no interruption, and no end. Others hold that the doubling refers to the compound nature of the sin: the person who commits *shirk* is guilty of both disobedience (violating God's command) and ingratitude (denying God's right), and the two forms of guilt produce two forms of punishment. The multiplication is thus not merely quantitative but qualitative: the punishment is not just more of the same but a different kind of suffering that corresponds to the different dimensions of the sin.

**The Disgrace That Never Ends**

The verse closes with the phrase *khālidīn fīhi muhanān* — "abiding therein forever, in disgrace." The word *khālidīn* (abiding, eternal) is the same word used for the righteous in Paradise (25:15-16), and its use for the condemned in Hell establishes the symmetry of the two outcomes: both are permanent, both are irreversible, and both are the consequence of choices made in this life. The word *muhanān* (disgraced, humiliated) names the specific quality of the punishment: it is not merely pain but humiliation. The condemned are not merely suffering; they are ashamed. The disgrace is the opposite of the honor (*karām*) that the servants of the Compassionate possess — the honor of humility, prayer, and obedience. The condemned have traded that honor for the disgrace of *shirk*, murder, and fornication, and the trade is permanent.

The concept of disgrace in Hell is not incidental but central. In 25:34, the sūrah warned that those who reject the Prophet's message will be assembled on the Day of Judgment "prone on their faces — blind, dumb, and deaf." The prone position, the loss of sight and hearing — these are forms of humiliation that correspond to the sin of rejecting the truth. The person who refused to see the signs of God is blinded; the person who refused to hear the Prophet's message is made deaf; the person who refused to speak the truth is made dumb. The punishment fits the sin, and the fitting is the justice of God. The servants of the Compassionate, who heard the signs, saw the signs, and spoke the truth, are honored in Paradise with sight, hearing, and speech; the condemned, who rejected all three, are disgraced in Hell with the loss of all three.

**The Verse's Placement After the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:69 follows the servants' portrait (25:63-68) and serves as the counterweight: after describing what the servants are, the sūrah describes what happens to those who are not servants. The structure is the sūrah's standard juxtaposition: the righteous are described (25:63-68), and then the condemned are described (25:69). The two descriptions are placed side by side without commentary, allowing the reader to feel the contrast. The servants have peace, humility, and prayer; the condemned have multiplied punishment, eternity, and disgrace. The contrast is not argued; it is shown, and the showing is the sūrah's most effective argument.

**The Reversal of the Disbelievers' Expectations**

The multiplication of punishment is also a reversal of the disbelievers' expectations. In 25:21, the disbelievers said: "We have never heard of this in any previous faith. This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but a fabrication." Their dismissal of the punishment was based on the assumption that no consequence would follow from their disbelief. Verse 25:69 corrects the assumption: the consequence not only exists but is multiplied. The multiplication is the sūrah's answer to the disbelievers' dismissal: the punishment is not diminished by their disbelief; it is increased. The person who denies the punishment receives more of it, and the "more" is the justice of God — the justice that responds to rejection with severity and to acceptance with mercy.

The word *muhanān* (disgraced, humiliated) also carries overtones of the social humiliation that the disbelievers inflicted on others during their lives. The disbelievers of Makkah humiliated the weak, the poor, and the enslaved — people like Bilāl, ʿAmmār, and Sumayyah, who were tortured for their faith. The word *muhanān* in 25:69 reverses the social order: in the Hereafter, the humiliators become the humiliated, and the humiliated become the honored. The reversal is the sūrah's way of demonstrating that the social order of this world is not the final order — that the hierarchies of power, wealth, and status that the disbelievers relied on will be overturned on the Day of Judgment, and the people who were at the bottom in this life will be at the top in the next. The servants of the Compassionate, who walked humbly and responded to the foolish with peace, will be the ones who are honored; and the disbelievers, who walked arrogantly and mocked the servants, will be the ones who are disgraced.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:70

> As for those who repent, believe, and do good deeds, they are the ones whose evil deeds Allah will change into good deeds. For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Proves the Rule**

The verse introduces an exception to the punishment described in 25:69: *illā man tāba wa āmana wa ʿamila ʿāmalan ṣāliḥan* — "except for those who repent, believe, and do good deeds." The exception is significant because it follows the most severe warning in the sūrah — the doubled punishment, the eternity of disgrace — and shows that the punishment is not inevitable. The door of repentance remains open, even for those who have committed the gravest sins (*shirk*, murder, fornication in 25:68). The condition is threefold: repentance (*tawbah*), faith (*īmān*), and righteous action (*ʿamal ṣāliḥ*). The three together constitute the complete reversal of the condemned person's trajectory: repentance turns away from the sin, faith turns toward God, and righteous action builds the new life on the foundation of the turn.

Al-Ṭabarī records that this verse was revealed in response to the anxiety of some idolaters who were inclined to embrace Islam but felt that their previous sins — the very sins listed in 25:68 — could never be forgiven. The verse's message is that no sin is beyond God's forgiveness, provided the repentance is genuine. The theological significance is enormous: the verse teaches that God's mercy is wider than any human assessment of sin. The disbelievers may have thought that their sins were too grave, their rejection too deep, their opposition too entrenched for forgiveness. The verse corrects the assumption: God forgives anyone who turns to Him, regardless of what they have done. The only condition is the sincerity of the turn.

**The Transformation of Evil into Good**

The verse's most striking promise is *fa-ulā'ika yubaddilu'llāhu sayyi'ātihim ḥasanātin* — "for those, God will change their evil deeds into good deeds." The transformation is not metaphorical. Al-Qurṭubī records several interpretations: the evil deeds are erased and replaced with good ones; the evil deeds are literally transformed into good ones in the Hereafter; or the person's life after repentance is so radically different that the good deeds outweigh and overwhelm the evil. The most common reading is the first: God forgives the evil deeds and records good deeds in their place. The forgiveness is not a subtraction but a replacement — the evil is not merely removed but actively replaced with good.

The theological significance is enormous. The verse teaches that repentance is not merely an apology but a transformation. The person who repents is not the same person they were before; they have changed direction, changed allegiance, and changed the foundation of their life. The change is so thorough that God responds to it by changing the record — turning the sins into good deeds, the failures into merits. This is the ultimate expression of *raḥmah* (mercy), and the verse closes by naming the quality: *Allāhu Ghafūr Raḥīm* — "Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful." The mercy is not limited by the severity of the sin; it is limited only by the sincerity of the repentance, and the sincerity is something only God knows.

**The Verse's Structural Position Between Punishment and Portrait**

Verse 25:70 is structurally positioned between the punishment of the condemned (25:69) and the continuation of the servants' portrait (25:71-76). The position is significant: the sūrah presents the punishment first, then the exception, and then returns to the positive portrait. The message is that the punishment is real but not final — the door remains open, and the door is opened by repentance. The sūrah's arrangement thus balances severity and mercy: the severity of 25:69 is real, but the mercy of 25:70 is also real, and the two together constitute the complete picture of divine justice. The justice is not one-sided; it includes both the consequence and the escape, and the human being chooses which side to stand on.

**The Connection to the Sūrah's Earlier Forgiveness Passages**

The promise of forgiveness in 25:70 connects to the sūrah's earlier references to divine mercy. In 25:10, God describes the disbelievers' objection — "If only the Compassionate had a son, I would be the foremost in worship" — and the implication is that God's mercy is available to anyone who turns to Him. In 25:65-66, the servants pray for protection from Hell, and the prayer is itself an act of repentance — an acknowledgment of their dependence on God's mercy. Verse 25:70 generalizes the principle: not only the servants but anyone who repents sincerely can receive the transformation of evil into good. The verse is therefore the sūrah's most inclusive statement of divine mercy — inclusive not in the sense that everyone is forgiven regardless of their actions but in the sense that everyone is eligible for forgiveness if they meet the conditions. The conditions are clear, the mercy is real, and the transformation is guaranteed for those who fulfill the conditions.

The three conditions of 25:70 — repentance, faith, and righteous action — are not arbitrary but structurally necessary. Repentance without faith is regret without direction: the person feels bad about what they have done but has no new framework within which to build a different life. Faith without righteous action is belief without expression: the person affirms the truth but does not live by it. And righteous action without repentance is effort without foundation: the person does good deeds but carries the weight of the old sins without having turned away from them. The three together form a complete system: repentance clears the ground, faith plants the seed, and righteous action cultivates the harvest. The servants of the Compassionate have completed all three steps, and the completion is what earns them the transformation of their evil deeds into good. The sūrah's promise is not a free gift but a reward for a process — a process that begins with the hardest step (admitting the sin) and ends with the most productive step (doing good).
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:71

> And whoever repents and does good has truly turned to Allah properly

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confirmation of Genuine Repentance**

The verse confirms the sincerity of the repentance described in 25:70: *wa man tāba wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-innahu yatūbu ilā'llāhi matabān* — "and whoever repents and does good, truly turns to God with a true turning." The word *matabān* (a true turning, a genuine repentance) is the intensive form of *tawbah* (repentance), and its use here emphasizes that the repentance described is not superficial. The person who repents and does good has not merely apologized; they have fundamentally reoriented their life. The word *matabān* names the quality of the reorientation: it is complete, it is sincere, and it is directed to God.

Al-Rāzī offers a nuanced reading of the verse's two mentions of repentance. The first — *man tāba* ("whoever repents") — refers to turning away from sin: the person stops doing what they were doing and acknowledges the wrongness of their previous actions. The second — *yatūbu ilā'llāhi* ("turns to God") — refers to turning toward God: the person does not merely abandon the old life but actively enters a new one, directed toward God, grounded in faith, and expressed through righteous action. The two movements — away from sin and toward God — are both necessary. A person who turns away from sin but does not turn toward God has created a vacuum; a person who turns toward God but does not turn away from sin is trying to serve two masters. The servants of the Compassionate do both, and the doing of both is the *tawbah* this verse names.

**The Linguistic Depth of Matabān**

The word *matabān* deserves particular attention. It is the intensive form (*faʿāl*) of *tawbah*, and the intensification suggests that the repentance is not ordinary but extraordinary — not a simple apology but a profound transformation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the *faʿāl* pattern in Arabic often indicates a sustained or intensive quality: *ṣawwāb* means "very accurate," *jawwād* means "very generous," and *matabān* means "very repentant" or "thoroughly turned." The servants' repentance is not a momentary regret but a sustained commitment, a reorientation that lasts for the rest of their lives. The word *matabān* thus names not only the act of repentance but the quality of the life that follows: the servants who turn to God do not turn back.

The distinction between ordinary repentance and *matabān* is relevant to the sūrah's overall message. The disbelievers have been warned throughout the sūrah — through eschatological descriptions, prophetic examples, and natural signs — and some of them may have been moved to regret. But regret is not repentance: regret is the feeling of sorrow for what has been done; repentance is the act of turning away from it and turning toward God. The sūrah's warnings are designed to produce not merely regret but *matabān* — the thorough, permanent, life-altering turn that the verse names. The servants of the Compassionate are people who have made that turn, and the turn defines their character.

**The Verse's Role in the Sūrah's Theology of Mercy**

Verse 25:71 follows 25:70's promise that God will change evil deeds into good deeds, and it reinforces the promise by confirming that the repentance is genuine. The two verses together form the sūrah's most concentrated statement of divine mercy: God forgives the gravest sins, transforms the record, and accepts the turning of the one who turns. The mercy is not abstract; it is operational. It changes the person's status from condemned to forgiven, from Hell-bound to Paradise-bound, from the multiplied punishment of 25:69 to the transformed deeds of 25:70. The transformation is the sūrah's ultimate answer to the disbelievers who denied the Hour: the Hour is not only the moment of punishment but also the moment of reward, and the reward is available to anyone who repents sincerely.

**The Verse's Placement as a Bridge**

The placement of 25:71 immediately before the continuation of the servants' portrait (25:72-76) creates a structural bridge. The sūrah has described the servants' virtues (25:63-68), the punishment for those who violate the servants' principles (25:69), the possibility of repentance (25:70-71), and now returns to the servants' character (25:72-76). The message is that the servants are not a closed category; anyone can join them through repentance. The door is open, the conditions are clear, and the reward is guaranteed. The sūrah's portrait of the servants is therefore not a description of a spiritual elite but a description of what every human being can become — if they choose to turn. The bridge of 25:71 connects the warning (25:69) to the hope (25:72-76), and the connection is the sūrah's final gift to its audience: the chance to change.

The word *matabān* also carries a grammatical force that deserves attention. In Arabic, the *faʿāl* pattern of the noun can indicate the manner or quality of the action: it does not describe the act of repenting but the way in which the repentance is performed. The repentance is thorough, it is deep, and it is total. The servants of the Compassionate do not repent half-heartedly or provisionally; they repent with the full force of their being, holding nothing back, and the totality of the repentance is what earns the adjective *matabān*. The word is the sūrah's final characterization of the servants' relationship with their own sins: they do not excuse them, they do not rationalize them, and they do not minimize them. They face them, they turn from them, and they turn to God with the thoroughness that the word *matabān* names. The thoroughness is the servants' answer to the human tendency to soft-pedal sin, and the answer is the mark of their sincerity. This quality of total commitment distinguishes the servants' repentance from the superficial regret that characterizes those who acknowledge their wrongs without truly changing.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:72

> ˹They are˺ those who do not bear false witness, and when they come across falsehood, they pass ˹it˺ by with dignity

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal to Bear False Witness**

The verse resumes the portrait of the servants of the Compassionate with a new quality: the refusal to bear false witness — *wa'lladhīna lā yashhadūna'z-zūr*. The word *zūr* means falsehood, fabrication, distortion of truth. To "bear witness to *zūr*" is to testify to a lie — to participate in the distortion of reality, to lend one's credibility to a falsehood. The servants refuse to do this. They do not testify to what is false, whether in a legal proceeding, a social gathering, or a private conversation. Their commitment to truth is absolute: they will not distort it, will not amplify it, and will not stand by while others distort it.

Al-Qurṭubī offers a broader reading of *zūr*: not only false testimony in the legal sense but also the witnessing of falsehood in general — idolatry, lewd speech, lascivious entertainment, and any other activity that distorts the moral order. In this reading, the servants' refusal to bear false witness is not limited to the courtroom but extends to every domain of life. They do not participate in the propagation of falsehood, whether through testimony, through entertainment, through social media (in modern terms), or through silence. The refusal is active: the servants do not merely avoid falsehood; they refuse to be present when it is being performed.

**The Gravity of False Witnessing in Islamic Ethics**

The connection between false witnessing and moral corruption is deeply rooted in Islamic ethics. The Prophet is reported to have said: "Shall I not tell you of the greatest of the major sins? Associating partners with God, and disobeying parents." He was reclining, then sat up and said: "And false testimony." The repetition of the hadith — the Prophet's physical repositioning to emphasize the gravity of the sin — underlines the importance of truthfulness in the Islamic moral framework. False witnessing is not merely a lie; it is an assault on the social order, a corruption of the judicial process, and a betrayal of the trust that human beings place in one another. The servants of the Compassionate refuse to participate in this corruption, and the refusal is a defining feature of their character.

The placement of false witnessing alongside the prohibitions of 25:68 (*shirk*, murder, fornication) reveals the sūrah's understanding of what constitutes a fundamental sin. The three prohibitions of 25:68 violate the relationships with God, with other human beings, and with the family. False witnessing violates the relationship with truth itself — the foundation upon which all other relationships rest. A society in which false witnessing is tolerated is a society in which no contract is reliable, no testimony is trustworthy, and no justice is possible. The servants' refusal to bear false witness is therefore not merely a personal virtue but a social service: by maintaining their commitment to truth, they protect the social fabric that holds their community together.

**Passing by Falsehood With Dignity**

The second quality is the servants' response when they encounter falsehood: *wa idhā marrū bi'l-laghw marrū kirāmān* — "and when they pass by falsehood, they pass by with dignity." The word *laghw* (idle talk, vanity, purposeless speech) describes the kind of falsehood the servants encounter in daily life — gossip, mockery, pointless argument, and the general noise of a society that does not prioritize truth. The servants do not engage with this noise; they pass by it. The word *kirām* (dignified, noble, generous) describes the quality of their passing: they do not flee in disgust, do not linger in fascination, and do not stop to argue. They pass by with the dignity of people who know the difference between what deserves their attention and what does not.

The word *kirām* connects this verse to the Qur'an's broader use of the concept of *karāmāh* (nobility, dignity). In 17:70 — "We have honored (*karramnā*) the children of Adam" — the same root describes God's gift of dignity to all human beings. The servants of the Compassionate live out that dignity: they do not lower themselves to the level of the foolish (25:63), they do not participate in the falsehoods of their society, and they pass by what is beneath them with the quiet assurance of people who know their worth. The dignity is not arrogance; it is the natural expression of a character formed by prayer, humility, and devotion.

**The Practical Ethics of the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:72 adds a practical ethical dimension to the portrait. The servants are not merely spiritually devoted (praying at night, asking for protection from Hell) but morally disciplined (refusing false testimony, passing by idle talk with dignity). The combination of spiritual devotion and moral discipline is the sūrah's definition of the complete human being: one who worships God in private and maintains integrity in public. The servants of the Compassionate are not monks who withdraw from society; they are citizens who engage with society on their own terms — terms set by the Criterion (25:1) and maintained by the character the Criterion produces.

The portrait, taken as a whole from 25:63 to 25:76, is one of the Qur'an's most comprehensive character sketches. Each verse adds a new dimension: humility in walking, patience with the foolish, night prayer, fear of Hell, moderation in spending, avoidance of the gravest sins, refusal of false witnessing, and dignified passing by falsehood. The dimensions together create a person who is fully integrated — a person whose inner life (prayer, fear, repentance) matches their outer conduct (walking, speaking, spending, witnessing). The integration is the servants' greatest achievement, and the sūrah presents it as the model for all believers. The Criterion (25:1) was sent down to distinguish truth from falsehood, and the servants are the people who have internalized the distinction and live it out in every dimension of their lives.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:73

> ˹They are˺ those who, when reminded of the revelation of their Lord, do not turn a blind eye or a deaf ear to it

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response to Divine Reminder**

The verse describes the servants' relationship with revelation: *wa'lladhīna idhā dhukkirū bi-āyāti Rabbihim lam yakhirrū ʿalayhā ṣumman wa ʿumyānān* — "and those who, when reminded of the signs of their Lord, do not fall upon them deaf and blind." The word *dhukkirū* (they are reminded) uses the same root as *tadhakkur* (mindfulness, remembrance) that appeared in 25:50 and 25:62. The signs of God — in nature, in revelation, in the experience of life — are reminders, and the servants respond to them correctly: they do not shut their ears (*ṣumman*, deaf) or close their eyes (*ʿumyānān*, blind) to the signs. They hear them, they see them, and they act on them.

The phrase *lam yakhirrū ʿalayhā* — "they do not fall upon them" — is vivid. The word *kharrū* (to fall, to collapse) describes a physical action: the deaf and blind person collapses onto the signs, covering them, burying them, preventing them from being seen or heard. The image is of a person who deliberately smothers the truth — not by ignoring it passively but by actively covering it. The servants of the Compassionate do not do this. When the signs appear — in a verse of the Quran, in the pattern of the night sky, in the cry of a newborn — the servants attend. They do not turn away; they do not cover their ears; they do not close their eyes. The response is the response of people who recognize the signs for what they are and refuse to let them pass unheeded.

**The Qur'an's Recurring Theme of Spiritual Deafness and Blindness**

The metaphor of spiritual deafness and blindness runs through the Qur'an. In 22:46 — "Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is the hearts within the breasts that go blind" — the Qur'an distinguishes between physical and spiritual sight. In 2:18 — "They are deaf, dumb, and blind, so they do not return" — the disbelievers are described in the same terms. In 7:179 — "They have hearts with which they do not understand, and they have eyes with which they do not see, and they have ears with which they do not hear" — the description is extended. By contrast, those who bear the title of God's servants are the opposite of these descriptions: they hear, they see, they understand, and they return. The contrast between the servants and the disbelievers is the contrast between the open heart and the closed one, and the openness is the result of the humility, prayer, and repentance that define the servants' character.

The Qur'an's use of the triple metaphor — deaf, dumb, blind — is not arbitrary but diagnostic. Each sense organ corresponds to a spiritual faculty: the ear corresponds to understanding, the eye corresponds to insight, and the tongue corresponds to expression. The person who is spiritually deaf cannot hear the truth; the person who is spiritually blind cannot see the truth; the person who is spiritually dumb cannot speak the truth. These devoted servants possess all three faculties: they hear the signs of God (25:73), they see the signs of God (25:61-62), and they speak the truth (25:72: they do not bear false witness). The possession of all three is the mark of the complete human being — the person whose spiritual senses are intact and functioning.

**The Verse's Placement in the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:73 adds the quality of receptivity to the portrait. The servants walk humbly (25:63), pray at night (25:64), ask for protection from Hell (25:65-66), spend moderately (25:67), avoid the gravest sins (25:68), do not bear false witness (25:72), and now receive the signs of God without resistance. The portrait is cumulative: each verse adds a new dimension of character, and the dimensions together create a person who is fully oriented toward God — in posture, in prayer, in spending, in ethics, and in receptivity. Those who follow this path of receptivity are not defined by a single virtue but by the totality of their orientation: every aspect of their being — body, mind, heart, and wallet — is directed toward the Most Compassionate.

**The Contrast With the Disbelievers' Response to Revelation**

The servants' receptivity to the signs stands in sharp contrast to the disbelievers' response described earlier in the sūrah. In 25:4-5, the disbelievers said: "This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but a fabrication... These are only fables of the ancients." In 25:21, they said: "We have never heard of this in any previous faith." In 25:30, the Prophet lamented: "My people have abandoned this Quran." The disbelievers heard the same signs that the servants heard, but they responded with rejection rather than receptivity. The difference is not in the signs but in the hearts: the servants' hearts were open, and the disbelievers' hearts were closed. The sūrah's placement of 25:73 after the description of the punishment (25:69) and the promise of forgiveness (25:70-71) suggests that the disbelievers' deafness and blindness are not permanent conditions but choices — choices that can be reversed through repentance and faith.

The word *ṣumman* (deaf) and *ʿumyānān* (blind) are used here in the accusative, functioning as adverbial descriptions of the manner in which the disbelievers fall upon the signs. The grammar itself tells a story: the deafness and blindness are not the servants' condition but the disbelievers' condition — the condition of those who refuse to hear and refuse to see. Those who stand in God's service have chosen the opposite condition: they hear with open ears and see with open eyes, and the openness is the foundation of their faith. The sūrah's contrast between the open and the closed is one of its most persistent themes, and 25:73 is the verse that states it most directly. The servants are open; the disbelievers are closed; and the difference between openness and closure is the difference between Paradise and Hell.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:74

> ˹They are˺ those who pray, “Our Lord! Bless us with ˹pious˺ spouses and offspring who will be the joy of our hearts, and make us models for the righteous.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer for Family**

The verse records the servants' most personal prayer: *wa'lladhīna yaqūlūna Rabbanā hab lanā min azwājinā wa dhurriyyātinā qurrata aʿyunin* — "and those who say, 'Our Lord, grant us from our spouses and our offspring the coolness of our eyes.'" The word *qurrata aʿyunin* (coolness of the eyes) is an Arabic idiom for comfort, joy, and the relief that comes from seeing something one loves. The servants do not pray for wealth, for status, or for worldly power. They pray for their families — for spouses and children who will be a source of joy and spiritual comfort. The prayer reveals the servants' priorities: the most important thing in their lives is not what they possess but who they share their lives with, and the quality they seek in their companions is spiritual — not beauty, not wealth, but the "coolness of the eyes" that comes from shared faith and mutual devotion.

Al-Rāzī observes that the "coolness of the eyes" is understood to be of a spiritual nature, not mere worldly enjoyment. The servants pray that their families will join them in worship and obedience — that the spouse will be a partner in faith and the children will be a continuation of the tradition. The Qur'an elsewhere warns against treating family as a distraction from God: "Your wealth and your children are only a trial" (64:15). The servants' prayer resolves the tension: they ask not that their families be a source of worldly comfort but that they be a source of spiritual comfort — the comfort of shared devotion, of a household oriented toward God.

**The Prayer for Leadership**

The second part of the prayer — *wa-j'alnā li'l-muttaqīna imāmān* — "and make us leaders (*imām*) for the God-conscious" — expands the servants' concern beyond the family to the community. The word *imām* (leader, model, example) carries the sense of one who stands in front, who sets the direction, who provides the pattern for others to follow. The servants do not pray for leadership in the political sense; they pray for the kind of leadership that comes from exemplary character — the kind that inspires others to follow not through authority but through example.

Al-Rāzī connects this prayer to Abraham's prayer in 26:84 — "And grant me a reputation of honor among later generations" — and notes that the desire for spiritual leadership is a positive thing when it comes from a desire to serve rather than to dominate. The servants of the Compassionate want to be models for the righteous — not because they seek status but because they want their lives to be useful, to be a guide for others who are trying to find the Way (*sabīl*). The prayer for leadership is therefore the servant's final expression of devotion: not only do they worship God in private and maintain integrity in public, but they also want their worship and integrity to be visible enough to inspire others.

**The Family as a Vehicle for Spiritual Continuity**

The pairing of the two requests — for pious family and for leadership among the righteous — reveals the servants' understanding of how spiritual traditions survive. The primary vehicle of spiritual transmission is the family: parents pass their faith to their children, who pass it to their children, and the chain continues across generations. The servants pray for pious offspring because they understand that their own faith is not sufficient; it must be transmitted, and the transmission begins in the household. The prayer for leadership is the second stage: after the family has been established on a foundation of faith, the servants pray for the ability to extend their influence beyond the family to the community — to become models for others, to set the standard, and to lead by example.

The prayer also reveals the servants' awareness of their own limitations. They do not claim to be leaders already; they ask God to make them leaders. The request is an act of humility: the servants know that leadership is not something they can seize but something they must be given, and the giving is God's. The same humility that characterizes their walking (25:63) characterizes their aspiration: they do not demand leadership; they pray for it, and the prayer is the proof of their fitness for it. A person who demands leadership is the last person who should have it; a person who prays for it is the first.

**The Verse's Place in the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:74 is the last of the servants' own words in the sūrah. The portrait has described their actions (walking, praying, spending, avoiding sin), their character (humility, dignity, receptivity), and now their aspirations (a faithful family, a legacy of leadership). The aspirations reveal the depth of the servants' concern: they are not merely trying to save their own souls; they are trying to build something that will outlast them — a family of faith, a community of righteousness, a legacy of guidance. The prayer for pious offspring and for the status of *imām* is the servant's answer to the question of purpose: why do they walk humbly, pray at night, and avoid sin? Because they want their lives to matter — not in the way the disbelievers measure mattering (through wealth and status) but in the way God measures it (through faith and example).

The contrast between the servants' aspiration and the disbelievers' ambition could not be sharper. In 25:8, the disbelievers said: "Why was this ˹Quran˺ not sent down to some great man from the two cities?" — revealing their obsession with worldly status. The servants of the Compassionate do not pray for the kind of greatness the disbelievers admire; they pray for the kind of greatness that comes from serving God and serving the community. The difference is the difference between the two worldviews the sūrah has been presenting: the worldview that measures value by wealth and power, and the worldview that measures value by faith and service. The servants have chosen the latter, and the prayer of 25:74 is the expression of that choice.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:75

> It is they who will be rewarded with ˹elevated˺ mansions ˹in Paradise˺ for their perseverance, and will be received with salutations and ˹greetings of˺ peace

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reward for Patience**

The verse describes the reward that awaits the servants of the Compassionate: *ulā'ika yujzawna'l-ghurfata bi-mā ṣabarū* — "Those are the ones who will be rewarded with the elevated chamber for their patience." The word *ghurfah* (elevated chamber, lofty abode) refers to the highest rooms of Paradise — the apartments closest to God's presence, the most honored positions in the Hereafter. The word *ṣabarū* (they were patient, they persevered) names the quality that earned the reward: patience. The servants' patience was demonstrated in every aspect of their lives: patience with the foolish (25:63), patience in night prayer (25:64), patience in spending (25:67), patience in avoiding sin (25:68), and patience in the face of a society that mocked and rejected them. The reward — the *ghurfah* — corresponds to the patience, and the correspondence is the justice of God: what was given in effort is returned in reward.

The word *ghurfah* appears in 29:58 — "And those who believed and did righteous deeds, We will surely give them lofty chambers in Paradise, beneath which rivers flow" — and in 34:37 — "And it is not your wealth or your children that bring you nearer to Us in station, but it is whoever believes and does righteous deeds; for them there will be a double reward for what they did, and they will be in lofty chambers, secure." The repetition across the Qur'an establishes the *ghurfah* as the highest reward in Paradise — not merely a dwelling but a position, a station, a level of closeness to God that surpasses everything else. The servants of the Compassionate earn this position not through a single act of devotion but through the totality of their lives — through the patience that runs through every verse of their portrait.

**Greetings of Peace**

The verse continues: *wa yulaqqawna fīhā taḥiyyatan wa salāmā* — "and they will be greeted therein with salutations and peace." The greeting of peace (*salām*) is the same word the servants used in 25:63 when they responded to the foolish. The verbal echo is deliberate: in life, the servants said peace to those who insulted them; in Paradise, peace is said to them. The reversal is complete: the servants gave peace and received contempt; now they receive peace and are honored. The greeting is not from other human beings alone but from God Himself, as 36:58 confirms: "Peace! — a word from a Merciful Lord." The *salām* the servants receive in Paradise is the ultimate expression of divine approval: God greets them with the same word they used to greet the world.

**The Verses of Reward as Counterbalance to the Verses of Punishment**

The reward described in 25:75 mirrors the punishment described in 25:69 in precise detail. In 25:69, the punishment is multiplied; in 25:75, the reward is a mansion. In 25:69, the condemned abide forever; in 25:76, the servants abide forever. In 25:69, the condemned are in disgrace; in 25:75, the servants are greeted with peace. The symmetry is the sūrah's way of presenting the two outcomes as mirror images: the same structure, the same language, the same permanence — and opposite qualities. The sūrah does not need to argue for the justice of the two outcomes; it shows them, and the showing is the argument.

The concept of *ṣabr* (patience, perseverance) as the basis for the reward is significant. The servants are not rewarded for their wealth, their status, their lineage, or their intelligence. They are rewarded for their patience — the quality that sustained them through every trial the sūrah described. The patience of walking humbly (25:63) in a world that rewards arrogance. The patience of praying at night (25:64) in a world that values sleep. The patience of spending moderately (25:67) in a world that worships excess. The patience of avoiding sin (25:68) in a world that normalizes transgression. The patience of passing by falsehood (25:72) in a world that celebrates it. Every act of patience was an act of resistance — resistance against the disbelievers' culture, against the pressure to conform, against the temptation to take the easy path. The reward for resistance is the *ghurfah*, and the *ghurfah* is the proof that the resistance was worth it.

**The Connection to the Servants' Prayer**

Verse 25:75 answers the servants' prayer from 25:74. They asked for pious spouses and offspring; they are rewarded with the highest chambers of Paradise. They asked to be models for the righteous; they are greeted with peace by God Himself. The answer exceeds the request — the *ghurfah* is more than any earthly family could provide, and the divine *salām* is more than any earthly recognition could offer. The sūrah's structure thus connects prayer and reward: the servants prayed (25:65-66, 25:74), and the prayer was answered (25:75-76). The answer is not immediate — it comes after death, after judgment, after the long patience of a life lived in devotion — but it is certain, and the certainty is the ground of the servants' perseverance.

The certainty of the reward is also the sūrah's answer to the disbelievers' skepticism. In 25:21, the disbelievers said: "We have never heard of this in any previous faith." Their skepticism was based on the assumption that the afterlife is a fiction — a story invented to frighten people into obedience. Verse 25:75 answers the skepticism with specificity: the reward is not abstract but concrete — it has a name (*ghurfah*), a quality (elevated), a basis (patience), and a reception (greetings of peace). The specificity is the sūrah's way of saying that the afterlife is not a vague hope but a detailed reality, and the details are the proof of its truth. The servants of the Compassionate, who believed in the details and lived by them, are the ones who will inhabit the reality, and the inhabitation is the final vindication of their faith.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:76

> staying there forever. What an excellent place to settle and reside

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Permanence of the Reward**

The verse confirms the permanence of the servants' residence in Paradise: *khālidīna fīhā* — "abiding therein forever." The word *khālidīn* (abiding, eternal) is the same word used for the condemned in Hell (25:69) and for the righteous elsewhere in the sūrah (25:15-16). The symmetry is the sūrah's consistent message: both outcomes are permanent, and the permanence is the consequence of choices made in this life. The servants who persevered (25:75) will remain in their elevated chambers forever; the condemned who violated the prohibitions (25:68-69) will remain in their disgrace forever. The eternity of both outcomes is the sūrah's way of emphasizing the seriousness of the choice: what you do in this life determines where you live in the next, and the residence is permanent.

The word *khālidīn* (abiding) carries a semantic weight that distinguishes it from mere duration. The Arabic root *kh-l-d* implies not only lasting forever but becoming inseparable from the state — becoming part of it, merging with it, so that the person and the place become one. In the case of Paradise, the merger is joyful: the servant becomes part of Paradise's beauty, part of its peace, part of its light. In the case of Hell, the merger is terrible: the condemned becomes part of Hell's fire, part of its confinement, part of its despair. The permanence is therefore not merely temporal but ontological: the servant does not merely reside in Paradise; they become part of it. The condemned do not merely reside in Hell; they become part of it. The ontological dimension of the permanence makes the choice even more serious: the choice is not merely about where you will live but about what you will become.

**The Echo of 25:66**

The verse closes with *niʿma mustaqarran wa muqāmān* — "What an excellent settlement and abode!" The phrase uses the same two words — *mustaqarr* (settlement) and *muqām* (abode) — that appeared in 25:66, where the servants described Hell as "an evil place to settle and reside." The verbal echo is the sūrah's final structural device: the same vocabulary describes both outcomes, and the only difference is the adjective. In 25:66, the word was *sā'at* (evil); in 25:76, the word is *niʿma* (excellent, blessed). The juxtaposition is the sūrah's way of presenting the two options in their starkest form: the same words, the same structure, the same finality — and opposite results. The choice between the two is the choice the sūrah has been offering since its first verse, and the servants of the Compassionate are the people who chose correctly.

The word *niʿma* (excellent, blessed) is the Qur'anic standard for the highest praise. It appears in 16:30 — "What an excellent servant! He was truly one who turned in repentance" — and in numerous other verses where God praises those who obey. Its use in 25:76 is God's own assessment of the servants' dwelling: not merely good, not merely pleasant, but *niʿma* — excellent, blessed, the best that can be. The servants' dwelling in Paradise is the highest form of existence, and the description of it as *niʿma* is the sūrah's final word on the subject.

**The Completion of the Servants' Portrait**

Verse 25:76 completes the portrait of the servants that began in 25:63. Across fourteen verses, the sūrah has described the servants' humility (25:63), their night prayer (25:64), their fear of Hell (25:65-66), their moderation (25:67), their moral boundaries (25:68), their rejection of falsehood (25:72), their receptivity to revelation (25:73), and their personal aspirations (25:74). The portrait is the sūrah's answer to the question posed in 25:1: what does the Criterion produce? The answer is: the servants of the Compassionate. The Criterion (25:1) was sent down to distinguish truth from falsehood, and the servants are the living embodiment of the distinction. They are the people who accepted the Criterion, lived by it, and were rewarded for it with the highest chambers of Paradise. The portrait is the sūrah's most sustained argument for the value of faith: not a philosophical proof but a description of what faith looks like when it is lived.

**The Structural Position of 25:76 in the Sūrah**

Verse 25:76 is the last verse of the servants' portrait and the penultimate verse of the sūrah. Its position is strategic: the portrait ends with the reward (25:75-76), not with the description of the servants' daily conduct (25:63-74). The sūrah's final message about the servants is therefore not about what they do but about what they receive — not about the effort but about the result. The result is the *ghurfah* (elevated chamber), the *salām* (peace), the *khālidīn* (eternal abiding), and the *niʿma* (excellence). The result is the answer to every act of patience, every night of prayer, every walk of humility, and every word of peace. The sūrah's portrait of the servants is therefore not only a description but a promise: the promise that the servants' lives — lived in accordance with the Criterion — will be rewarded with the best that God has to offer, and the reward will last forever.

The word *muqāmān* (abode, dwelling) in 25:76, compared with *maqīl* (resting place) in 25:24, reveals a subtle difference in the sūrah's vocabulary of Paradise. The *muqām* implies a permanent residence — a place where one stays, not merely a place where one rests. The servants' dwelling in Paradise is not temporary; it is their home, their final destination, the place they will inhabit for eternity. The permanence of the *muqām* is the opposite of the transience of earthly life: in this world, every dwelling is temporary — the house will crumble, the city will decay, the earth itself will pass away. In Paradise, the dwelling is eternal, and the eternality is the servants' reward for the patience that sustained them through the transience of this life.
## Sūrah al-Furqān 25:77

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “You ˹all˺ would not ˹even˺ matter to my Lord were it not for your faith ˹in Him˺. But now you ˹disbelievers˺ have denied ˹the truth˺, so the torment is bound to come.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Final Word**

The verse is the sūrah's last address to the disbelievers, spoken through the Prophet by divine command: *qul mā ya'bā'u Rabbī bikum law-lā du'ā'ukum* — "Say, 'My Lord would not care for you were it not for your supplication.'" The word *ya'bā'u* (to care, to regard, to take notice) is the most devastating statement the sūrah makes about the disbelievers' significance. God does not need them. Their existence, their worship, their rejection, their mockery — none of it matters to God in the sense of affecting Him. What matters is their prayer — their *du'ā'*, their worship, their recognition of God's sovereignty. Without that recognition, they are nothing: not enemies to be feared, not opponents to be respected, not even objects of divine attention. The statement strips the disbelievers of every claim to significance they might have made: their wealth, their social standing, their tribal power, their mockery of the Prophet — none of it registers with God. The only thing that registers is worship, and worship is the one thing the disbelievers refuse to give.

Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr understand *du'ā'* in this verse to mean worship (*ʿibādah*) in its broadest sense: the recognition of God through prayer, gratitude, and submission. The verse thus teaches that human beings have value before God only insofar as they worship Him. The value is not intrinsic but relational: the human being matters because of their relationship with God, and the relationship exists only through worship. The disbelievers, who deny God, deny the Hour, and deny the Prophet, have severed the relationship, and the severance makes them insignificant. The sūrah's final word to the disbelievers is therefore not a threat but a verdict: you do not matter, and the reason you do not matter is that you have refused the one thing that would make you matter.

**The Inevitability of the Torment**

The verse's closing clause — *fa-qad waqa'a'l-wa'd* — "so the torment is bound to come" or "so the promise has fallen" — declares the inevitability of the consequence. The word *waqa'a* (to fall, to happen, to occur) uses the past tense, giving the punishment the quality of something that has already happened — not a future possibility but a settled fact. The promise of punishment is as certain as the promise of reward, and the certainty is the sūrah's final message to the disbelievers: the time for argument is over, the time for mockery has passed, and what remains is the consequence. The word *wa'd* (promise) applies to both reward and punishment in the Qur'an — God's promise to the righteous is Paradise, and His promise to the disbelievers is the fire. Both promises are true, both are certain, and both will be fulfilled. The sūrah closes with the punishment-side of the promise because the audience is the disbelievers, and the disbelievers have chosen the side of the promise that applies to them.

**The Prophet's Role in the Final Address**

The verse begins with *qul* ("Say") — the divine command to the Prophet to speak God's words to the disbelievers. The command is the sūrah's final confirmation of the Prophet's role: he is not speaking his own words but God's words, and the message he delivers is not his own but God's. The word *qul* appears throughout the Qur'an — over three hundred times — and its appearance here, in the final verse of the sūrah, places the Prophet in the role of God's mouthpiece. The disbelievers may have dismissed the Prophet as a poet, a madman, or a fabricator (25:4-8), but the final verse corrects the dismissal: the Prophet is God's spokesperson, and the words he speaks are God's. The correction is the sūrah's answer to every accusation the disbelievers made: the Prophet is not a poet (25:77 is not poetry but prophecy), not a madman (25:77 is not madness but truth), and not a fabricator (25:77 is not fabrication but revelation).

**The Sūrah's Architecture**

Verse 25:77 closes the circle that opened in 25:1. The sūrah began with *tabāraka* — "Blessed is the One Who sent down the Criterion" — and it ends with the Prophet commanded to speak the final word to the disbelievers. Between the opening and the closing, the sūrah has presented the disbelievers' objections (25:4-8, 25:21), the eschatological consequences (25:11-19, 25:22-29), the Prophet's consolation (25:30-31), the explanation of gradual revelation (25:32-33), the prophetic catalogue (25:35-40), the second cycle of mockery (25:41-44), the natural signs (25:45-55), the definition of the prophetic mission (25:56-60), the cosmic signs (25:61-62), and the portrait of the servants of the Compassionate (25:63-76). The sūrah's structure is comprehensive: it addresses every possible objection, presents every possible form of evidence, and offers every possible incentive for faith. The final verse is the sūrah's last word: the disbelievers have been given everything they need, and the consequence of their refusal is now inevitable. The Criterion has been sent down; the servants have been described; the choice has been presented; and the choice is theirs.

The sūrah's final word — *fa-qad waqa'a'l-wa'd* ("so the torment is bound to come") — is also a reassurance for the Prophet and his followers. The disbelievers' mockery, their wealth, their social power — none of it will protect them from the consequence of their disbelief. The Prophet, who endured their mockery with patience (25:63: the servants say peace to the foolish), can take comfort in the certainty of the promise: the torment will come, the justice will be served, and the truth will prevail. The sūrah's final word is therefore not only a warning to the disbelievers but a consolation to the believers — a promise that the world's injustices will be corrected, that the mockers will be punished, and that the servants of the Compassionate will be rewarded. The Criterion has been sent down, and the Criterion will have the last word.
